| 50 | **A ranking pass and a scoping pass catch different dependency bugs — run the scoping pass on the top pick BEFORE locking the ranking, not after (Session 43, grill-me feature-priority decision).** Ran a `/grill-me` session to rank `docs/POSIT-COMPARISON.md`'s 12 gap candidates by usability heuristic (frequency × silent-failure-prevention × workflow-class breadth, since no usage telemetry exists to rank empirically). The ranking pass alone landed on a plausible, internally-consistent order (YAML diagnostics #1, Project-level render #2) using only candidate-level facts (each item's own frequency/effort/impact). That order was WRONG — not because any single fact was mismeasured, but because a later scoping question on item #2 ("should diagnostics also validate `_quarto.yml`, since multi-file is the confirmed dominant workflow?") surfaced that `_quarto.yml` discovery doesn't exist anywhere in the codebase (grep-verified) and item #1 (Project-level render) would have to build exactly that discovery as its own core deliverable — an inter-candidate infrastructure dependency invisible to a ranking pass that scores each candidate independently. **🔑 Ranking answers "which is more valuable," scoping answers "what does building this actually require" — and only the second kind of question surfaces shared/missing infrastructure between candidates that look independent at the ranking altitude.** Recovering after locking the ranking would have meant either building `_quarto.yml` discovery twice (once in each session, wastefully) or re-planning mid-implementation (FM #8, mode-switch mid-task) — instead the swap happened inside the same grill, before anything was written to `BACKLOG.md`, at zero cost. The session's own self-critique: even this order was reactive (the dependency surfaced FROM an answer, not from a proactive check) — a sharper opening move for a future multi-candidate ranking grill is to ask "which candidates might share undiscovered infrastructure?" (a quick repo grep per candidate's stated mechanism) before the ranking questions begin, not after. | Any session ranking 3+ implementation candidates against each other (backlog prioritization, grill-me feature-priority sessions, planning-session candidate selection): after producing a ranking from candidate-level heuristics, run one scoping pass on at least the top 1-2 picks ("what would building this actually require, concretely, in this codebase?") BEFORE treating the order as final — a scoping question can surface a shared-infrastructure or build-order dependency between candidates that no amount of independent-candidate scoring will reveal, and the fix is nearly free if caught before the ranking is written down anywhere durable. |
| 49 | **A per-claim adversarial verify pass with tool access (not just a synthesis agent) is where competitive-research defects actually surface — 45% of rows had one (Session 42, Posit feature-comparison doc).** Built `docs/POSIT-COMPARISON.md` (31 feature-parity rows vs. Posit's official AGPL-3.0 Quarto extension) via a `Workflow`: 5 parallel web-research agents by domain → 1 synthesis agent merging into a draft matrix → 31 independent verify agents, one per row, each re-grepping OUR repo and re-fetching every cited Posit URL, explicitly instructed to find problems rather than confirm. **14 of 31 rows (45%) had a real, confirmed defect at first draft** — none on our own side (every `ourStatus`/`ourEvidence` claim, grounded from `ROADMAP.md`+code before research began, survived verification untouched); all 14 were either (a) stale Posit-side citations presented as current (a 2022 changelog entry for a feature since redesigned — verified by pulling the LIVE `package.json`/source at today's commit, not just the changelog), (b) a citation that didn't support its own quote (fetched twice independently and the exact quoted string wasn't on the page — likely synthesized/misremembered by the research agent, not maliciously fabricated), (c) a citation pointing at the wrong URL for an otherwise-true fact (the right fact existed, just at a different page than named), (d) an overclaim about OUR OWN code (calling a deliberately-capped 1-level-deep completion "recursive"/"3+", contradicted by our own function's docstring), (e) an undercounted gap (claimed we're missing 2 commands, verified 4), or (f) a direction-reversal (claimed a Posit advantage that, on re-verification against their CURRENT source, turned out to be something Posit removed years ago and never restored — we were actually ahead). **🔑 The failure mode this catches is specific to competitive/multi-source research: a synthesis agent under time/token pressure tends to accept its own research agents' claims at face value and doesn't re-fetch to confirm a citation still says what it's quoted as saying "as of today," nor re-check whether a claim about OUR OWN codebase (which the synthesis agent did NOT independently verify, only merged) is precisely worded.** The fix is structural, not "be more careful": give a dedicated verify-stage agent BOTH read access to our repo (Bash/Grep) AND live web access (WebFetch) to independently re-derive each claim from primary sources, one claim per agent so an agent's attention isn't split across 31 claims. Regular, not campaign-scale research (a 31-row doc, ~22 min wall-clock, ~1.47M subagent tokens) still benefited enormously from this — it is not only for exhaustive/high-stakes verification campaigns. | Any deliverable that makes multiple comparative or externally-sourced factual claims (competitive analysis, "how does X do it" research, any doc citing a third party's current behavior): budget a dedicated adversarial-verify stage — one agent per discrete claim, with tool access to re-derive both sides independently — as a separate pipeline stage after synthesis, not folded into the synthesis agent's own pass. Expect roughly half of first-draft claims in this kind of research to need correction; treat a verify pass that confirms 100% of claims unchanged as a signal the verify agents weren't actually adversarial. |
| 48 | **`collectFields`'s name-collision dedup fix — a one-off bug session done right-sized, not as a b2-iii campaign slice (Session 41).** Fixed a review-caught (Session 37) bug: `collectFields` (`core/yaml-schema.ts`) deduplicated same-named `document-*.yml` fields by first-occurrence-wins, which is an accident of JSON key/file-naming order, not richness — Quarto's `copyright` is defined in BOTH `document-attributes.yml` (bare, childless, JATS-only) and `document-metadata.yml` (the real `year`/`holder`/`statement` object), and the childless one iterated first and silently won, discarding the richer definition under every format. (a) **🔑 A shared dedup/collision-resolution function's policy should be judged against ALL its inputs, not just the one instance that triggered the bug report.** Before writing the fix, grepped the real 1.7.33 schema for every duplicated visible `document-*` field name (not just `copyright`) and found 3 more (`logo`/`subject`/`footer`) — checking each showed their duplicates TIE in richness (both childless/valueless), so a generic "richest wins" fix changes behavior only for `copyright` and is a safe no-op for the other three. Fixing the general policy (not a `name === "copyright"` special case) was proportional BECAUSE the inventory confirmed no other collision would regress — skipping that inventory would have made "generic vs. special-cased" a guess instead of a verified call. (b) **DEVELOPMENT_WORKSTREAM.md's explicit "not for one-off fixes... if it's a single bug with a clear fix, just fix it" carve-out applied correctly here** — no campaign tracker, no multi-session decomposition; strict TDD (RED via a fixture mirroring the real collision — the poorer entry inserted FIRST, matching the real schema's actual JSON key order, since `Map`/`Object.entries` iteration order is insertion order for string keys — then the minimal `Map`-based richest-wins fix) plus the project's standing gate-d break-revert convention (forcing `quartoSharePath` to throw) were still the right bar for a pure bugfix session, not just for feature slices. (c) **A `Map<name, SchemaField>` (not an array + `Set<string>`) makes "keep the richer of two collisions, preserve first-seen ORDER" trivial**: `.set()` on an existing key replaces its VALUE without moving its position in iteration order, so richness-comparison and position-stability are independent concerns — no separate index-tracking needed. 483 unit (+1) / 172 integration (+1); clean 35-file `.vsix`; compile clean. | Any shared dedup/merge function found buggy for one named instance: grep the real data for every OTHER instance of the same collision shape before deciding whether the fix should be generic or name-specific — a generic policy is safe to ship once you've confirmed no other instance's behavior regresses (a tie is not a regression). A single, clearly-scoped bug (not a repeating pattern needing campaign tracking) still gets the full strict-TDD + gate-d break-revert bar — DEVELOPMENT_WORKSTREAM.md's "campaigns only" framing narrows the PROCESS overhead (no tracker table, no multi-session plan), not the VERIFICATION bar. When a dedup-by-name policy must also preserve first-seen ordering, reach for a `Map<key, value>` over an array+Set pair — `.set()` on an existing key updates content without disturbing position. |
| 46 | **CLAUDE.md size-management — extract a runaway append-only section into its own committed, non-auto-loaded file (Session 39).** `CLAUDE.md`'s "Project-specific Learnings" table had grown to 45 rows / 159 KB of the file's 164 KB (~97%) over 38 sessions — and `CLAUDE.md` is injected in full into every session's context (confirmed via the system-reminder at session start), so every session was paying that cost regardless of relevance. Extracted the table verbatim into a new `PROJECT_LEARNINGS.md` at the project root, leaving a short pointer paragraph in `CLAUDE.md`'s "Project-specific Learnings" subsection (net: CLAUDE.md 164 KB → 5.8 KB). (a) **🔑 The pointer MUST be a plain Markdown link (`[text](PROJECT_LEARNINGS.md)`), never an `@`-import (`@PROJECT_LEARNINGS.md`) — an `@`-import auto-expands into every session's context exactly like the inline table did, defeating the entire point of the extraction** (operator-flagged mid-task before the edit was made — the harness treats `@path` specially even inside prose). Grep any future extraction's pointer text for a stray leading `@` before committing. (b) **Move large content with tooling, never by retyping.** Used `sed -n` to locate exact line boundaries, a Python script to slice-and-replace `CLAUDE.md` in place, and — the load-bearing verification step — `diff <(sed -n '<old-range>p' CLAUDE.md-before) <(sed -n '<new-range>p' PROJECT_LEARNINGS.md)` to prove the moved content is BYTE-IDENTICAL before deleting it from the source. A 160 KB block is not something to hand-copy into an `Edit` `old_string`/`new_string` pair — the tool call itself becomes the risk. (c) **Check whether the extracted heading is a hard-coded anchor in files you cannot edit before assuming a rename or restructure is free.** `SESSION_RUNNER.md` (synced, byte-identical, canonical-only) instructs sessions to "put project learnings in your CLAUDE.md → Project-Specific Methodology Adaptations → Project-specific Learnings subsection" — grepped `SESSION_RUNNER.md`/`BOOTSTRAP.md`/`CLAUDE_TEMPLATE.md`/`docs/methodology/HOW_TO_USE.md` for that exact heading string first; all four are synced/canonical and unelidable. The fix was to KEEP the heading text identical and only change what's under it to a pointer — satisfying the synced instruction's letter (the heading still exists, still in CLAUDE.md) while relocating the payload. Renaming or removing the heading itself would have silently broken every synced file's instruction with no way to fix the synced side. (d) **A pure-content-move needs no unit test (CLAUDE.md's own TDD-mandate exemption clause: "pure declarative/config/doc edits with no logic") but still needs the two normal checks** — `npm run compile` (clean; zero `src/**` touched) and `python3 methodology_dashboard.py` (unchanged 78/100, same pre-existing CRITICAL flag) — plus a repo-wide grep for the old heading text and for any stray `@`-import syntax, since a docs-restructure's failure mode is a dangling reference outside the diff's obvious blast radius (Learning #7/#10 applied to a file split rather than a citation). | Any future CLAUDE.md (or other always-loaded context file) subsection that has grown large and append-only (this table, a decision log, a fixture inventory): extract to a project-owned, git-committed sibling file; point to it with a **plain Markdown link, never an `@`-import**; move content via `sed`/scripted slicing + a byte-exact `diff`, never by retyping; grep every synced/canonical file for a hard-coded reference to the heading you're touching BEFORE renaming or removing it — keep the heading stable and relocate only the payload if any synced file depends on it; verify with the project's normal doc-only checks (compile + dashboard) plus a repo-wide grep for the old content and for accidental auto-import syntax. |
| 51 | **Grounding a PLANNING session's load-bearing external-tool claim means running the tool live, not just reading its docs/source — and the payoff was a real, easy-to-miss design trap (Session 44, Project-level render plan).** Prior "ground firsthand" learnings (#27, #32, #41c, #47, …) are all about grounding STATIC data — reading the installed Quarto schema JSON before trusting a resolver. This session's load-bearing fact was Quarto CLI *runtime behavior* instead: does `quarto render` (no args) auto-discover an ancestor project the way npm/git walk up for `package.json`/`.git`? Official docs are silent on this; `quarto-cli`'s own TypeScript source (`project-context.ts`, fetched from GitHub) confirmed the ancestor-walk EXISTS — but reading source code alone would have missed the actual, subtler behavior: a `Workflow` agent with Bash access scaffolded a real temp Quarto project and ACTUALLY RAN `quarto render` from a project subdirectory, discovering that it neither fails NOR renders the whole project — it silently renders ONLY the current directory's file(s) while inheriting the ancestor's config. **🔑 A plan built on "the CLI walks up, so bare `quarto render` from anywhere under the project will work" (source-code-only grounding) would have shipped a command that silently under-renders for the single most common invocation shape** (a user editing a nested chapter file) — success dialog and all, since the CLI exits 0. The empirical test also caught a second, related trap invisible to source-reading: `Output created: <path>` is reported relative to the RENDER TARGET directory, not the spawning process's actual `cwd` — a naive port of the existing single-file `runRender`'s `path.resolve(cwd, reported)` pattern would silently compute a wrong "Open" path unless `cwd` is deliberately pinned to the discovered root. Both findings were locked into the plan's design (explicit-root-argument invocation; `cwd` pinned to the root) and each got its own adversarial-verifier agent independently re-running the SAME live test from scratch (not just re-reading the first agent's report) before being treated as settled — matching this project's standing "REFUTE, don't rubber-stamp" verify-pass discipline (Learning #49), applied here to a planning session's design assumption rather than a shipped code claim. | Any planning session whose design depends on how an EXTERNAL CLI/tool actually behaves at runtime (not just its static schema/config format): budget a live empirical-test agent (scaffold a minimal real-world case, actually run the tool, observe stdout/exit code/filesystem effects) in addition to doc/source research — official docs and even primary source code can describe a mechanism's EXISTENCE without revealing its exact, non-obvious edge-case behavior (a partial/hybrid result, a path resolved relative to something other than the obvious candidate). Independently re-run the single most load-bearing empirical test with a second agent before locking a design decision on it, exactly as you would adversarially verify a factual claim in a research doc. |
| 52 | **Implementing the Session 44 Project-level render plan as one vertical-slice session (Session 45): the plan held up exactly as grounded, plus three new reusable facts.** All four pre-declared layers (`core/project.ts` `findProjectRoot` → `render-args.ts`+`features/render-project.ts`+`package.json`+`extension.ts` wiring → `test/fixtures/project/` → `render-project.test.ts`) shipped in one session with checkpoint commits at each boundary; 490 unit / 176 integration; clean 35-file `.vsix`. (a) **🔑 A vscode-adapter's internally-computed value that has no read-back API (no channel-text getter, no dialog-argument inspection convention existed in this repo before) can still be integration-tested by monkey-patching the `vscode` namespace function it calls** — `vscode.window.showInformationMessage`/`vscode.env.openExternal` are plain writable properties on the module object, not frozen; reassigning them for the duration of one test (restore in a `finally`) let this session capture the EXACT resolved `Uri` passed to `openExternal` and assert it matched `path.join(root, "index.html")` — the only way to directly prove the plan's Dragon D1 (the "Open" success path must resolve against the discovered project root, not the extension host's own cwd) rather than inferring it from a passing render. No new devDependency (no sinon) needed — a plain reassign-and-restore is sufficient when only 1-2 calls need stubbing. (b) **🔑 Quarto auto-generates a `.gitignore` (containing just `/.quarto/`) inside a project root on its FIRST render, if none already exists** — this surfaced only once this session ran real integration tests against the real (freshly-created) fixture, not during the planning session's own `/tmp`-scaffolded sanity checks (a different, throwaway directory each time). Any NEW project fixture this repo adds will trigger the same auto-generation the first time it's rendered for real; gitignore `test/fixtures/<name>/.gitignore` up front (this session added it to both the repo-root `.gitignore` AND the integration test's own artifact-cleanup step) rather than being surprised by an untracked file after the first green test run. (c) **🔑 A plan's flagged "Dragon" that depends on host-tool VERSION behavior can sometimes be resolved analytically instead of by manual F5 launch** — the plan's D4 asked the executor to empirically verify (via a real Extension Development Host launch with zero `.qmd` ever opened) whether `quarto.renderProject` needs an explicit `onCommand:` activation event. A `WebSearch` confirmed VS Code 1.74.0+ auto-generates implicit activation events for any `contributes.commands` entry (official docs, cross-checked against two independent sources); this project's `engines.vscode` is `^1.90.0`, well past that floor, so no `activationEvents` change was needed — resolved with evidence, without needing a GUI session this CLI-only agent can't drive interactively. (d) **⚠ Self-critique: gate (c) ("full build/test matrix at EVERY layer boundary, not once at the end") was only PARTIALLY honored.** `npm test` (vitest) + `npm run compile` ran after L1 and L2, but `npm run test:integration` and `npm run package` were deferred to a single run after L4 — meaning a wiring regression from L2 (e.g. the new command breaking discoverability, matching the historical `EXTENSION_ID` incident of Learning #18) would only have surfaced at the very end, not at L2's own boundary. Nothing regressed this time (confirmed retroactively), but the discipline gap is real: `test:integration` takes ~18s and `package` ~15-20s in this repo — both cheap enough to run at every one of a 4-layer slice's boundaries without materially slowing the session. | (a) Any integration test that needs to verify a vscode-adapter's internally-computed value with no public read-back API: monkey-patch the specific `vscode.*` function it calls (capture-and-restore, no library needed) rather than concluding the value is untestable. (b) Any new multi-file Quarto project test fixture: gitignore its own auto-generated `.gitignore` (and `.quarto/` cache) up front, in both the repo `.gitignore` and the test's own cleanup, before the first real render surprises you with an untracked file. (c) A plan's flagged open question about VS Code API/version-dependent behavior: check the official docs against this project's `engines.vscode` floor before assuming a live GUI smoke test is the only way to resolve it. (d) In any vertical-slice session: run the FULL verification matrix (compile + unit + integration + package) at every layer boundary, not just the cheap subset (compile+unit) for early layers — the fuller checks are cheap enough that deferring them to "once at the end" is discipline erosion, not efficiency. |
| 53 | **A backlog item's stated v1 scope can be UNSAFE to build as written — "flag unknown key" needs a live empirical open-vs-closed-schema check before any diagnostics feature is planned, not just a design pass (Session 46, YAML schema diagnostics plan).** `BACKLOG.md` item #2 asked for unknown-key diagnostics across `.qmd` front matter, cell options (`#\|`), AND `_quarto.yml`. A live test against the installed Quarto 1.7.33 CLI (a fabricated key vs. a genuine typo of a real option, in EVERY candidate surface, independently reproduced twice by adversarial verifiers with their own fixtures) found front matter, cell options, per-format nested options, and even `_quarto.yml`'s own ROOT keys are all **open** — Quarto itself cannot distinguish a typo from an intentional custom field there (both exit 0, silently, byte-for-byte identical behavior). Only the INTERIOR of `_quarto.yml`'s `project:`/`website:`/`book:` blocks is genuinely **closed** (root-caused in the bundled `quarto.js` CLI source: `getProjectConfigSchema()`'s outer object never sets `closed`, but each inner block's own definition does) — already enforced by `quarto render`/`quarto inspect` today, with fuzzy "did you mean" suggestions. Flagging "unknown key" anywhere in an open region would false-positive on every real project using custom metadata — this is not a hypothetical risk, VS Code's own `SKILL.md` frontmatter validator shipped exactly this bug (issue #294520, a fixed allowlist breaking legitimate third-party extensions) before being fixed. **Separately, a previously-flagged "validation-shaped `json-schemas.json`" lead (distinct from the completion-shaped `yaml-intelligence-resources.json` this extension already reads) was chased down and killed**: direct inspection + tracing the bundled `quarto.js` source found it's an unused, non-standard, dangling-`$ref`ed byproduct of Quarto's own release-time TypeScript-type-export tooling, never read back by the CLI at runtime — everything needed (including `"closed": true` flags) is already in the file this extension already parses. **A third, smaller correction:** the backlog item's own stated dependency on Session 45's `findProjectRoot` ("now available to reuse for validating `_quarto.yml` itself") turned out false once the safe v1 scope was determined — the correct design reacts to a document literally named `_quarto.yml` being open, with no root-discovery step needed at all; `findProjectRoot` would only be needed by a larger, deliberately-deferred "proactively validate from `.qmd` context" enhancement. This generalizes Learning #51's live-CLI-grounding pattern from "does invocation X behave as expected" to a sharper, prior question: "is this diagnostic surface even safe to build, anywhere, before designing how." | Before planning ANY "flag unknown/invalid X" diagnostics feature over a semi-structured or freeform-metadata document format (YAML front matter, JSON with informal conventions, any config format that traditionally allows arbitrary user extension keys): empirically test the actual tool's own behavior on both a fabricated key and a misspelled real key, in EVERY candidate surface, before assuming closed-set validation is safe anywhere — an open region's "unknown key" is indistinguishable from a legitimate extension point, and flagging it is a false-positive machine, not a feature. Also: before building new validation-artifact-reading infrastructure, trace whether a candidate schema/type-export file is actually read back at runtime by the tool that produced it, or is a build-time-only byproduct nobody consumes. |
| 54 | **Implementing the Session 46 YAML schema diagnostics plan (Session 47): the plan's grounding held up exactly, but a multi-agent adversarial-review Workflow found 2 HIGH-severity, core-promise-violating bugs the careful, TDD-disciplined implementation still shipped — plus two genuinely new operational/environmental facts.** All four pre-declared layers shipped with checkpoint commits (`SchemaIndex.projectKeys`/`resolveClosedKeys` incl. the `book:` super-merge chain → `core/project-yaml.ts` scanner → `features/yaml-diagnostics.ts` `DiagnosticCollection` wiring → fixtures → integration tests), each grounded firsthand against the real installed Quarto 1.7.33 schema/CLI, not just the plan's prose (Learning #6 applied again: independently re-probed `schema/project.yml`/`definitions.yml`'s exact shapes with Python before writing the resolver, and re-verified both fixtures with `quarto inspect` before committing — all matched the plan exactly). **(a) 🔑 A 26-agent adversarial review (5 lenses → per-finding adversarial verify) of this SAME session's own just-shipped, unit+integration-tested code found 2 HIGH-severity false positives the implementation never anticipated: a quoted YAML key (`"output-dir": docs`, spec-legal and Quarto-accepted) was compared against the schema's bare names WITH its quote characters still attached, and the filename gate used `.endsWith("_quarto.yml")` — a SUFFIX test, not an exact-basename check, so `not_quarto.yml`/`backup_quarto.yaml`/any file merely ending in those characters also matched.** Neither was in the plan's own §7 risk table (D1-D5) — quoting wasn't considered anywhere in the plan — and both are the kind of bug a careful, grounded, TDD-following implementer does not spontaneously think to test for, because the "happy path" grounding (real CLI, real schema) never exercises a stylistic YAML choice like key-quoting. **This is the strongest evidence yet in this project's history that adversarial review of one's OWN just-shipped vertical slice is not optional even when implementation was careful, TDD-disciplined, and grounded against live tools — it is a DIFFERENT verification axis (adversarial "how could this be wrong" vs. constructive "does this do what I intended"), and skipping it because "I already tested this carefully" would have shipped a feature that violated its own single stated core promise (zero false positives) on day one.** Also confirmed real: a `hops>10` depth cap on the closed-key resolver was REDUNDANT (seenRefs alone fully guards a parsed-JSON tree, which can never be circular without a named ref) and, when it fired on a legitimately deep non-cyclic chain, silently dropped that branch's names while a shallower sibling's `closed:true` still propagated — the identical "closed:true, incomplete names" false-positive SHAPE a real cycle would produce, without an actual cycle; removed rather than raised, since `seenRefs` was always sufficient on its own. **(b) 🔑 `vscode.workspace.onDidCloseTextDocument` — this codebase's first-ever use of it (plan flagged it as genuinely new) — never fires for a REAL on-disk file-backed document in the `@vscode/test-electron` integration host, confirmed via extensive probing (`workbench.action.closeAllEditors`, `vscode.window.tabGroups.close()` directly, waits up to 8s, `vscode.workspace.textDocuments` still showing the document present/not-closed throughout).** It fires reliably for `untitled:`/virtual-scheme documents elsewhere in the SAME suite, so the gap is specific to file-backed documents in this automated host — and it matches `vscode.d.ts`'s own documented caveat ("There is no guarantee that this event fires when an editor tab is closed"), confirmed via `WebSearch` as still the correct, standard production pattern (Microsoft's own `code-actions-sample` uses the identical `onDidCloseTextDocument`+`collection.delete(uri)` shape) — so the fix was disclosing the automated-coverage gap in-file and verifying by code trace + (attempted) manual smoke test, NOT changing the implementation. This extends Learning #3 ("automated runtime verification WORKS here... stronger than manual F5 for wiring/activation/dispatch") with an important boundary: it does NOT extend to document-CLOSE lifecycle events for file-backed documents specifically. **(c) ⚠ Process learning, not a code learning: running a large `Workflow` (26 agents) WITHOUT `isolation: 'worktree'` against a live repo let concurrent agents' own scratch/repro test files (written directly into `test/unit/` to empirically verify findings by actually running vitest) become visible to EACH OTHER and to the orchestrator as unexplained untracked files** — one review agent misread this as a possible security/injection incident (including an unverifiable claim of a concealment-instructing system-reminder no other agent corroborated). The mundane explanation (shared scratchpad + shared working tree across parallel non-isolated agents) was confirmed directly: the stray files were real, present in the actual repo, and their content was benign and on-topic. **No malicious injection was substantiated; the files were deleted, nothing was ever committed.** 523 unit (+37 total across L1 + fix layers) / 183 integration (+7); clean 35-file `.vsix`. | (a) After any vertical-slice implementation — EVEN a carefully grounded, strict-TDD, live-CLI-verified one — run a genuine adversarial review (multiple independent lenses + per-finding refute-by-default verification) before treating the slice as done, specifically probing stylistic/formatting variations of the input the happy-path grounding never exercised (quoting, casing, whitespace, encoding) and any raw string-matching gate (`.endsWith`/`.startsWith`) for whether it's actually an exact/boundary check. (b) A closed-set/merge resolver that already has a proper cycle guard (a `seenRefs`-style set over NAMED references) does not also need a generic depth/hops cap "for safety" — on data that can only cycle through a named reference (any parsed-JSON tree), a redundant cap is pure risk: it can silently truncate a legitimate deep branch while a shallower sibling's closed/valid flag still propagates, producing a false positive with no real cycle. (c) Before trusting a claimed `onDidCloseTextDocument`-driven behavior (or any close-lifecycle test) inside `@vscode/test-electron`: confirm empirically whether the event fires for FILE-backed documents in that harness specifically (it may not, even after `workbench.action.closeAllEditors` and multi-second waits) — the official `vscode.d.ts` doc comment already says this isn't guaranteed; don't assume automated coverage is possible just because the underlying VS Code API pattern is standard and well-documented. (d) When running a large multi-agent `Workflow` where individual agents do hands-on empirical verification that writes real files (not just reads), consider `isolation: 'worktree'` even for a "review" workflow, not only for workflows that intentionally mutate the deliverable — otherwise concurrent agents' own legitimate scratch artifacts can appear as unexplained cross-contamination and be misread as a security event. |
| 55 | **A BACKLOG item's own "both declarative, TDD-exempt, ship together one session" framing can be wrong on BOTH axes at once, and only firsthand CLI/API research catches it (Session 48, onboarding walkthrough + scaffolding commands plan).** BACKLOG.md item #3 asked for `quarto.newDocument`/`quarto.createProject`/a getting-started walkthrough as one declarative, TDD-exempt session. Live research (a 4-agent parallel `Workflow`: firsthand CLI source-read + live invocation of `quarto create`/`create-project` against installed 1.7.33; VS Code's `contributes.walkthroughs` schema grounded against `microsoft/vscode`'s own extension-point source, not docs; this repo's own command-adding pattern + test conventions; Posit's public black-box UX from manifest/changelog/docs only, never `.ts` source) found **two independent corrections**: (1) `quarto create document` does not exist as a CLI feature at all in 1.7.33 (`kArtifactCreators` is a fixed 2-entry registry, `project`/`extension` only — confirmed by reading the bundled `quarto.js` AND by a live `ERROR: Failed to create document` invocation) — `quarto.newDocument` must synthesize its own YAML-safe template in TypeScript, genuine logic (title-escaping), not declarative config; `quarto.createProject` likewise needs an arg-builder + three prompt APIs this codebase has never used (`showInputBox`/`showQuickPick`/`showOpenDialog`, confirmed zero existing hits) + a brand-new OS-temp-dir integration-test pattern (confirmed zero `mkdtemp`/`tmpdir` usage anywhere in `test/`, since every existing feature validates *existing* committed fixtures, never writes to an arbitrary runtime-chosen location). Only the walkthrough itself turned out genuinely declarative/TDD-exempt. (2) The three components are not one capability but three independently-useful ones with a real dependency edge (the walkthrough's `completionEvents` reference the other two commands' IDs, so it structurally cannot ship before they exist) — "ship together, one session" would have been FM #26 (mega-session masquerading as a vertical slice) if followed literally; the plan instead pre-declares three separate vertical-slice contracts (Gate a) so three future sessions can each execute one without re-planning. Also confirmed en route: this project's `engines.vscode: ^1.90.0` already exceeds every walkthroughs-feature version floor, no bump needed — a fact worth having grounded rather than assumed, even though it turned out to be a non-issue. | Before accepting a BACKLOG item's own stated scope/TDD-exemption/session-count as given — even one written by a careful prior grill-me/ranking session — run firsthand research (read the actual CLI/library source, execute real invocations, grep this repo's own conventions) before planning: a "looks declarative" or "looks like one command" framing can hide (a) a missing CLI capability that pushes real logic into the extension, and (b) multiple independently-shippable capabilities wrongly bundled by a plan that only checked "does it feel like one session," not "does the dependency graph and TDD-gate classification actually support one session." This is Learnings #51/#53's live-grounding discipline generalized a third time — from "does invocation X behave as expected" and "is this diagnostic surface safe to build" to "is this BACKLOG item's own scope/session-count/TDD-exemption claim itself correct." |
| 56 | **The `showInformationMessage`/`openExternal` reassign-in-`finally` monkey-patch stubbing technique (`render-project.test.ts`) generalizes cleanly to `showInputBox` — first empirical confirmation of Dragon G2/D4 (Session 49, implementing Track A of the onboarding plan, `docs/planning/2026-07-09-onboarding-walkthrough-plan.md` §2/§5 D4).** The plan flagged this as an open, unconfirmed risk ("a reasonable bet, not yet proven") rather than assuming it — so the integration test that exercises it (`test/integration/suite/new-document.test.ts`) was written as the REAL test, not a throwaway spike, and driven test-first: written before `quarto.newDocument` existed, run, and confirmed RED for the right reason (`command 'quarto.newDocument' not found`), THEN the adapter was implemented and the same run went GREEN with `showInputBox` reassigned to a fixed resolved value in one test and to `undefined` (simulating an Escape-cancelled prompt) in another, both restored in `finally`. | 57 | **Track B (`quarto.createProject`, Session 50) confirmed the two remaining prompt-stubbing APIs Learning #56 left open, AND found a necessary further generalization of the same technique: intercepting `vscode.commands.executeCommand` itself, not just a `vscode.window`/`vscode.env` namespace function, to test a command whose success path calls a VS Code BUILT-IN command (`vscode.openFolder`) rather than a directly-callable API.** `showQuickPick`/`showOpenDialog` extended the reassign-in-`finally` monkey-patch with zero adjustment, exactly as Learning #56 predicted — five APIs now independently confirmed (`showInformationMessage`, `openExternal`, `showInputBox`, `showQuickPick`, `showOpenDialog`). Rather than taking that fallback, this session generalized the SAME technique one level up the API surface: reassign `vscode.commands.executeCommand` itself, intercept only calls whose first argument is `"vscode.openFolder"` (capture the `Uri`, resolve without executing), and delegate every other command name — including the very `quarto.createProject` invocation the test is driving through the SAME patched function — to the original implementation. (c) A kickoff question about an EXTERNAL CLI's own process-spawning/side-effect behavior (as opposed to a VS Code API's behavior) can usually be answered by a 30-second scratch-directory CLI invocation plus a before/after process-list check, without needing a GUI session. |

| 59 | **A cell-relative "next"/"previous" query needs no explicit "am I inside a cell?" branch — filtering `findAllCells` output by a plain cursor-LINE comparison (`c.startLine > line` / `c.endLine < line`) already produces the correct cell whether the cursor sits inside a cell or in prose, and finding the exact same VS Code default-keybinding collision risk BEFORE shipping, not after, mattered (Session 52, `quarto.runNextCell`/`runPreviousCell`/`runCellsBelow`, BACKLOG item #4).** The existing `runCellsAbove`/`advanceToNextCell` code already implied this but never stated it: because a cell's own line range is contiguous and cells never nest or overlap, comparing the RAW cursor line (not "the current cell's `startLine`, if any") against every cell's `startLine`/`endLine` automatically excludes the cell containing the cursor (its own bounds straddle `line`, so neither `>` nor `<` fires) and correctly reaches into either prose or an adjacent cell — one code path, no `if (currentCell) ... else ...` split, verified by both the "from prose" and "from inside a cell" integration test pairs passing unmodified. Separately: while choosing default keybindings for the 3 previously-unbound existing commands (`runCellsAbove`/`runAllCells`/`insertCell`) plus the 4 new ones, the first candidate scheme used `ctrl+alt+up`/`ctrl+alt+down` for Next/Previous Cell — a natural-seeming directional mnemonic — until checking against VS Code's own STOCK keybindings surfaced that `ctrl+alt+up`/`ctrl+alt+down` (`cmd+alt+up`/`down` on Mac) are the DEFAULT bindings for `editor.action.insertCursorAbove`/`Below` (multi-cursor add), an extremely commonly used core editing feature — silently shadowing it inside every `.qmd` file would have been a real, easy-to-miss regression. Re-picked a `ctrl+alt+<letter>` mnemonic scheme instead (a/b/n/p/i, plus `ctrl+alt+enter` for Run All and `ctrl+shift+enter` for Run Selected Line(s)), all confirmed free of known VS Code stock-keybinding collisions. | (a) When adding a command that needs to act on "the cell/thing near the cursor, whether or not the cursor is precisely inside it," try filtering the full `findAllCells`/similar position-indexed list directly by the raw cursor line first — a case-split for "inside vs. outside" is very often unnecessary once the comparison direction (`>` for after, `<` for before) is chosen correctly, since a contiguous, non-overlapping element's own bounds already exclude it from a strict comparison against any line it contains. (b) Before finalizing ANY new default keybinding, check it against VS Code's own stock keybindings (not just this extension's existing ones) — `ctrl+alt+up`/`ctrl+alt+down`/`ctrl+alt+left`/`ctrl+alt+right` in particular are claimed by core multi-cursor/tab-navigation actions; prefer a letter-mnemonic scheme over directional-arrow chords unless the arrow combo has been explicitly checked. |
| 60 | **Declarative authoring CONTENT (snippet bodies, template text) needs its own grounding pass, separate from the AGPL look-but-don't-copy gate — ground it against this project's OWN parser/recognizer code and fixtures, not against memory of "how Quarto syntax generally works" (Session 53, the 13-snippet `snippets/quarto.json`, BACKLOG item #5).** Learning #1 already forbids reading Posit's AGPL extension for snippet CONTENT — but that gate alone doesn't guarantee the content is CORRECT, only that it's independently authored. Also motivated the design choice to keep the 4 executable-cell snippets (`qpy`/`qr`/`qjulia`/`qojs`) deliberately fence-only, with no forced `#|` option boilerplate — the Phase 6d `#|` completion feature already owns option entry, so a snippet duplicating it would drift out of sync with the schema-reader-driven option list over a Quarto CLI upgrade, whereas an empty cell body never can. | When adding declarative authoring content (snippets, templates, scaffolded file bodies) to a project that ALSO has its own parser/indexer for that same syntax: grep the parser's recognized patterns and its own test fixtures FIRST, and match the new content's literal output against them — don't rely on general syntax knowledge (even if accurate) or trust that "the AGPL gate was satisfied" implies "the output is internally consistent with this codebase's own recognizer." (3) Zotero was "deferred, coupled to WYSIWYG," but a grep of `src/providers/citation.ts` found this project already reads/parses `.bib` files for citekey completion (Phase 6c, shipped) — a Better-BibTeX-exported Zotero library already works today with zero Zotero-specific code, so the "gap" was almost entirely already closed; (4) YAML front-matter/cell-option diagnostics was framed in `docs/POSIT-COMPARISON.md` as unimplemented future work, but re-reading `docs/planning/2026-07-09-yaml-schema-diagnostics-plan.md` §0 (Session 46's own prior research) found the unknown-key-flagging version had ALREADY been empirically tested against the live Quarto CLI and explicitly rejected as a **permanent scope boundary** (all three surfaces are open schemas — a typo and a legitimate custom key are indistinguishable to `quarto render`) — presenting this as "still open" to the operator would have re-litigated an already-closed, evidence-backed decision. Only the Contextual Assist Panel's "UI consolidation" framing held up largely as stated (though a grep still sharpened which of its two sub-capabilities are partial vs. zero). **The pattern: a candidate's EXISTING backlog label (parked/deferred/unranked) is itself an unverified claim, exactly like Learning #67's filed-item scope note — cheap to falsify with one grep/`gh api`/`WebSearch` call per candidate, and when it's wrong, it's wrong enough to flip the operator's actual decision, not just adjust effort estimate.** | At the start of EACH candidate in a multi-candidate ranking/grill session (not only the eventual top picks), spend one grounding pass — grep this repo for existing/partial infrastructure, `gh api`/`WebSearch`/`WebFetch` for the external tool's actual current capabilities and license, re-read any prior planning doc's own findings for that exact candidate — before presenting the question. Treat every existing "parked"/"deferred"/"unranked" label as a hypothesis someone wrote down once, not a settled fact; the label can be wrong in either direction (cheaper/already-solved, or a previously-investigated dead end), and the direction changes what question is even worth asking the operator. |
| 69 | **When planning a feature that shells out to an external CLI or touches a VS Code API surface this project has never used, verify the EXACT behavior firsthand — live invocation, or the actual installed `.d.ts` — rather than trusting public docs or general API knowledge; both under-specify exactly the details a plan needs (Session 62, planning notebook `.ipynb` conversion via `quarto convert`, `BACKLOG.md` item 8).** Firsthand testing against the installed 1.7.33 CLI (a scratch-dir round trip plus deliberate edge cases: overwrite, missing input, an `{r}` cell, an `{ojs}` cell, pure prose) found three real behaviors invisible on `quarto.org`'s own CLI reference page: it silently overwrites an existing output file with no prompt or error; the generated notebook's `kernelspec` metadata is hardcoded to Python 3 regardless of the source cell's actual engine (an `{r}`-cell `.qmd` converts cleanly but the resulting notebook falsely claims a Python kernel); and `{ojs}` cells are never split into notebook code cells at all (the whole document collapses into one markdown cell, since OJS has no Jupyter-kernel equivalent). None of these are stated in the CLI's public docs — only direct testing surfaced them, and each reshaped the plan's design (an adapter-side overwrite-confirmation gate; disclosing, not attempting to fix, the two fidelity gaps). Separately, general VS Code API knowledge was insufficient for one load-bearing design question — does an open `.ipynb` file read as `vscode.window.activeTextEditor`? Grepping the installed `@types/vscode/index.d.ts` directly (not relying on training-data memory of the API) confirmed a `.ipynb` file open in the Notebook UI is a `NotebookEditor`/`NotebookDocument` (`window.activeNotebookEditor`, a separate accessor with its own `isDirty`/`save()`), never a `TextEditor`/`TextDocument` — a naive `activeTextEditor`-only check would silently miss every open notebook, a gap a docs-only or memory-only plan would likely have missed until the implementing session hit it. | Before finalizing any plan that shells out to a CLI subcommand or touches an unfamiliar VS Code API surface: run the actual CLI against real inputs AND deliberate edge cases (don't stop at `--help` or the public docs page), and grep the installed `@types/vscode/index.d.ts` for the exact interface/accessor being relied on rather than trusting memory of the general API shape — this project's `node_modules/@types/vscode` is the authoritative, versioned source, not general training knowledge of "how VS Code's API usually works." |
| 70 | **A `NotebookDocument` loaded via `vscode.workspace.openNotebookDocument` stays resident (and contributes to VS Code's built-in notebook-aware workspace-symbol scanning) even after `workbench.action.closeAllEditors` closes its tab — and even if the editor was NEVER shown at all, e.g. only intercepted `showNotebookDocument`'s call for verification (Session 63, notebook conversion, BACKLOG item #8 L3 integration tests).** `test/integration/suite/convert-notebook.test.ts`'s real-CLI round-trip tests each opened/converted a `.qmd`/`.ipynb` fixture with an ATX heading (`# Hello`) for realism. The adapter's `openResult` calls the REAL `vscode.workspace.openNotebookDocument(uri)` even in tests that stub `showNotebookDocument` to avoid actually showing a tab (mirrors `create-project.test.ts`'s `withInterceptedOpenFolder` pattern) — `openNotebookDocument` alone is enough to register the document in VS Code's internal state. `workbench.action.closeAllEditors` in `afterEach` (this project's established leak-prevention idiom, Learning #62 and every suite since) closes VISIBLE tabs but does not dispose documents that were only ever loaded, not shown, and — surprisingly — did not reliably dispose even a genuinely shown-then-closed notebook either (the `.ipynb`→`.qmd` direction test's real, fully-shown input notebook leaked too). Confirmed empirically (a temporary debug probe logging `vscode.commands.executeCommand("vscode.executeWorkspaceSymbolProvider", "")`'s raw results, not guessed): 3 stray `"# Hello" @ /var/folders/.../doc.ipynb` entries polluted `workspace-symbols.test.ts`'s unrelated "aggregates matches across every workspace .qmd file" assertion, run ~15 suites later in the same shared Extension Development Host — first ruled out `vscode.workspace.findFiles` (proven correctly scoped to the real `test/fixtures/project` workspace folder via a probe printing its actual results) and cross-run VS Code hot-exit state (proven irrelevant by deleting `.vscode-test/user-data` and reproducing identically) before finding the true source. Fix: **use heading-free fixture content** (plain prose, no leading `#`) for any test fixture that gets loaded as a notebook — sidesteps the leak regardless of its VS Code-internal cause, since there is no public API to force-dispose a loaded `NotebookDocument`. Separately, the SAME test-writing pass caught a real adapter race: `runConvert`'s success branch did `void openResult(...)` then `resolve()` immediately (mirroring `render.ts`/`create-project.ts`'s existing fire-and-forget-the-final-UI-action convention) — but unlike those two (a non-modal info toast; a window-reloading `openFolder`), `openResult` is awaited-on by the interception technique itself, so the command's returned promise resolving before `showNotebookDocument`/`showTextDocument` was actually CALLED meant the test's `finally`-block restoration ran first, silently letting the REAL (uncaptured) API fire. Fixed by chaining `.then(resolve)` so the command promise now genuinely waits for the open step. | (a) Before writing an integration test that opens a `.ipynb` fixture (via the extension's own code path OR directly for test setup) — even one that's later intercepted/closed — give it heading-free content, or explicitly budget for cross-suite `workspace-symbol`-provider pollution; there is no known API to force-unload a `NotebookDocument` once `openNotebookDocument` has touched it. (b) When intercepting a `vscode.window.show*Document`-style call to verify which API a command used (this project's established technique, `create-project.test.ts`'s `withInterceptedOpenFolder`), check whether the adapter's own promise chain actually AWAITS that call before resolving — a fire-and-forget "open the result" step (an accepted convention when the action is a non-blocking toast or a window-reloading command) breaks this specific verification technique if the interception's scope closes before the real call fires; await it, don't assume the existing house fire-and-forget style transfers. |
| 71 | **A prior session's "MIT" / "here's the config setting" claim about a THIRD-PARTY tool can be wrong in ways that only the tool's own primary source (its LICENSE file, its shipped manifest) will reveal — and a settings-name claim can go stale even faster than a license, since settings get renamed/deprecated across versions (Session 64, planning the spell-checking config recipe, `BACKLOG.md` item 9).** Session 61's grill-me (Learning #68) asserted `streetsidesoftware.code-spell-checker` was "MIT" and that it "already spell-checks arbitrary VS Code language IDs via `cSpell.enabledLanguageIds`" — both plausible, neither independently verified against a primary source at the time. This session fetched the actual GitHub repos directly: the underlying `cspell`/`cspell-lib` ENGINE library is MIT, but the PUBLISHED VS Code EXTENSION (`streetsidesoftware/vscode-spell-checker`, the actual Marketplace artifact) is **GPL-3.0-or-later** — confirmed independently via both its `LICENSE` file and its manifest's `"license"` field, not inferred from the engine's license or the Marketplace listing's summary text. Separately, `cSpell.enabledLanguageIds` turned out to be **deprecated** in the current (v4.6.0) manifest — its replacement, `cSpell.enabledFileTypes`, ships a `"*": true` wildcard default that makes the entire "enable this custom languageId" step Session 61 named UNNECESSARY for the common case, a materially more favorable finding than the prior claim implied, not just a renamed equivalent. Neither correction blocked the plan (recommending, not vendoring, a GPL extension doesn't implicate this MIT project's own license; the setting simplification only made the recipe smaller) — but both would have shipped inaccurate, uncorrected claims into a durable planning document and a `BACKLOG.md` entry if taken on faith. | Before a plan recommends, cites the license of, or documents the exact settings/config-key names for ANY third-party tool/extension/library — even one a prior session already characterized — fetch that tool's OWN primary source directly (its repo's `LICENSE` file + its shipped manifest's `"license"`/`contributes.configuration` fields, not the Marketplace summary or a general reputation) rather than propagating a prior session's unverified characterization forward. Settings-name claims are especially perishable — a config key that worked when a prior session wrote it down can be deprecated/renamed by the time an executor session reads it back. |
| 72 | **A CLI tool's own ancestor-directory config auto-discovery can silently corrupt an empirical scratch-directory verification, producing a misleadingly "already works with less config than expected" result — investigate any surprising result before reporting it, don't just write it down (Session 65, implementing the spell-checking recipe, `BACKLOG.md` item 9).** Re-verifying Session 64's recipe against the real `cspell` CLI in a fresh scratch directory, an early run showed `--language-id quarto` alone (zero custom config) already suppressing most false positives — seemingly contradicting Session 64's own firsthand §0.3 finding (cspell has NO built-in region-skipping for ANY languageId, including real `markdown`) and this session's own plan premise. Root-caused rather than trusted: a `cspell.json` this session had itself written into a PARENT directory of the fixture-under-test was being silently auto-discovered via `cspell`'s own ancestor config search — `--no-config-search` did **not** suppress it in this cspell version (10.0.1), the opposite of what its own `--help` text implies. Confirmed by systematically varying the `--language-id` value (`markdown`/`banana`/`xyzzy123` all produced the full, unsuppressed baseline — only `quarto` was ever affected, and only because that happened to be both the test value AND the string used inside the stray parent config) and by moving the stray file aside and re-running, which restored the expected full-baseline result. The corrected re-run (config placed alongside, never above, the fixture) reproduced Session 64's own result shape: baseline 23 issues (15 false positives) down to 8 with the recipe applied, zero false positives, all genuine typos caught. | Whenever an empirical scratch-directory test result contradicts a prior session's own grounded finding, or a tool "just works" with less configuration than expected, treat it as a bug to root-cause in the TEST HARNESS first, not a discovery to report — the surprising result is more often a scratch-directory-hygiene artifact than a genuine tool behavior. For any CLI that auto-discovers config by walking ancestor directories, keep every scratch config file INSIDE (never above) the directory tree under test, including files written earlier in the same investigation — a stray file left in a parent scratch directory is picked up silently, and a documented "disable search" flag is not guaranteed to suppress it. |
| 73 | **Quarto's own `.qmd` reader (Pandoc's `markdown` format, NOT `gfm`/`commonmark`) resolves the setext-heading "`---` disambiguation dragon" the backlog item itself flagged as the hard part — but not the way CommonMark's spec describes it, and empirical grounding against the real CLI (both Quarto's bundled Pandoc and a separate system `pandoc` binary, cross-checked) surfaced 3 genuinely surprising rules no amount of spec-reading would have found (Session 66, implementing setext heading support in the outline, `BACKLOG.md` Polish/deferred item).** (a) **A 2+-line paragraph does NOT promote to a setext heading in Pandoc's `markdown` reader** — unlike `gfm`/`commonmark` (verified: identical input promotes under `-f gfm`/`-f commonmark` but stays a plain paragraph, underline included as literal trailing text, under `-f markdown`). This dissolved what looked like the biggest scoping decision (multi-line heading text) into a non-issue: single-line-only isn't a pragmatic simplification, it's the CORRECT, faithful behavior for `.qmd`'s actual reader. (b) **A setext underline immediately following an ATX heading line (no blank line) swallows the ATX line's literal text — INCLUDING its `#` markers — into the setext heading**, e.g. `"# ATX Heading\n---"` renders `<h2># ATX Heading</h2>`, not two separate blocks. This is REAL Pandoc behavior (reproduced with plain `pandoc -f markdown`, not a Quarto-specific artifact) but was deliberately NOT replicated: a pre-existing, currently-passing unit test (`qmd-model.test.ts`, "does not treat a `---` that is not at line 0 as front matter") already asserts the OPPOSITE (both stay separate ATX headings) for an unrelated reason, so replicating Pandoc's quirk here would have required breaking or rewriting established, unrelated test coverage for an extremely rare adjacency — the divergence is disclosed in both the model's docstring and a dedicated regression test, not silently absorbed. (c) **A lone bullet-list item (`-`/`*`/`+` marker) immediately followed by an underline gets NESTED inside the list** (`<li><h2>text</h2></li>`, marker stripped) — but an ORDERED-list marker (`1.`) or a block-quote marker (`>`) does NOT nest; both stay literal heading text with the marker included verbatim. A flat, list-unaware regex scanner (this model tracks no list/blockquote context, matching the pre-existing accepted CommonMark-indented-code-block gap) would silently produce a heading with a WRONG name (the literal `"- solo item"`, marker included) for the bullet-list case if left unguarded — caught via reasoning, then confirmed empirically, and fixed with a narrow guard (`BULLET_LIST_MARKER`) that declines (false negative) rather than mis-titles; the ordered-list/blockquote cases needed NO guard since the general mechanism already reproduces their literal-inclusion behavior correctly. All three findings came from directly testing edge-case fixtures against the real CLI before writing any implementation code (`ARCHITECTURE`/`DEVELOPMENT_WORKSTREAM`'s "grounding, not documentation-level verification" discipline, Learnings #6/#52/#62 lineage) — none are documented in CommonMark's own spec, since Pandoc's `markdown` reader is a DIFFERENT, older dialect with its own extension-gated behavior. | Before implementing ANY Markdown/CommonMark-adjacent parsing logic for a `.qmd` file, verify against Pandoc's actual `markdown` reader specifically (`pandoc -f markdown`, or `quarto render`/`quarto pandoc`) — NOT against the CommonMark spec, NOT against `gfm`, and not from general Markdown knowledge. The three readers genuinely disagree on block-level disambiguation rules (multi-line setext promotion, block-adjacency swallowing, list-nesting-vs-literal-inclusion), and `.qmd` always uses Pandoc's own `markdown` format. When a flat, context-unaware regex scanner (this model's whole architecture) would need to fabricate structural nesting to match a reader's real behavior (e.g. Pandoc's list-item swallowing), prefer declining/false-negative over emitting a structurally-wrong result with fabricated text — and write a same-file regression test locking in the deliberate divergence, not just a comment. |
| 74 | **A competitor-comparison document that re-verifies existing rows category-by-category will still miss "unknown unknowns" — a competitor's own current manifest is an exhaustive, structural source of truth that a prose/changelog-only research pass cannot substitute for (Session 67, refreshing `docs/POSIT-COMPARISON.md` against Posit's current extension).** `docs/POSIT-COMPARISON.md` had been spot-corrected through Session 65, but every prior touch re-verified rows that already EXISTED — nobody had ever diffed Posit's FULL current `apps/vscode/package.json` (every `contributes.commands`/`configuration`/`languages`/`menus` entry) against this doc's own coverage. Doing that exhaustive structural diff (rather than only re-checking known categories) surfaced 11 real gaps with literally zero prior row — cell-diagnostics forwarding, Format Cell, standalone `.dot`/`.mmd`/`.typst` language registration, a public extension API, Lua/Quarto-Extension authoring, and more — none of which a "re-verify what's already documented" pass could ever have found, by construction: you cannot re-verify a row that was never written. **A second, related finding from the same session's adversarial-refute-check pass (28 candidates, 3 independent refuters each): 10 of 28 "survived" only after correction, not rejection** — e.g. a claim of "9 Posit commands" was refuted for undercounting (the true figure was 10), and "moved to the Activity Bar" was refuted because that move was reverted 9 days later in 2022 — in every one of those 10 cases the refuters caught a real inaccuracy in the CLAIM's specific framing (a count, a URL, a "since when" date) while the underlying finding itself remained genuine and doc-worthy. Treating "refuted" as "discard" would have silently dropped 10 real findings; the correct read of an adversarial-refute result is the REFUTER's own corrected claim, not a binary keep/reject. | (a) When refreshing any competitor/feature-comparison document, don't just re-verify existing rows — diff the competitor's actual current manifest/changelog/contribution-points structurally (every command ID, config key, language registration) against the whole document's coverage, specifically hunting for capabilities with NO existing row at all; a category-by-category or row-by-row re-check structurally cannot surface something nobody thought to look for. (b) When adversarially refute-checking a batch of candidate findings, read the REFUTER's `corrected_claim`, not just its `refuted` boolean, before discarding anything — a refutation is very often "this framing/count/date is wrong, but the underlying gap is real," not "this is false," and discarding on the boolean alone silently drops true findings. |
| 75 | **VS Code's pull-based request-forwarding architecture (`vscode.executeCompletionItemProvider` etc. — this project's whole `src/providers/embedded.ts` design) has a hard, documented ceiling: it cannot serve diagnostics, and the reason is not a missing feature to work around but a fundamentally different delivery model (Session 68, planning `BACKLOG.md` item 10, code-cell diagnostics forwarding).** Diagnostics are push-based (a language server proactively publishes via a `DiagnosticCollection`, on its own schedule) — VS Code's own Extension API docs state directly that request forwarding "does not work with diagnostics errors... does not support diagnostic providers that can 'pull.'" A firsthand empirical Extension Development Host test (5 scratch tests, written/run/deleted, never committed) against VS Code's own built-in TS/JS language service confirmed this three independent ways: (1) a synthetic-scheme `TextDocumentContentProvider` vdoc — this project's EXACT existing pattern — opened via `workspace.openTextDocument()` but never shown, got ZERO diagnostics after 8s; (2) a REAL on-disk file, inside the actual workspace folder, opened the same unshown way, ALSO got zero — ruling out "just write to disk" as sufficient by itself; (3) only `vscode.window.showTextDocument()` (a genuinely visible, active editor tab) produced diagnostics (~400ms) — and the moment a second document became the active editor, the first tab dropped out of `vscode.window.visibleTextEditors` entirely, meaning there is no "shown once, then safely backgrounded" state via public API — the tab would need to stay ACTIVE, which cannot coexist with the user actually editing their real document. Independently, Posit's own PR prose (`quarto-dev/quarto` #980, public description text only, never their AGPL source) confirms the same wall from the *outside*: their own first vdoc-based prototype didn't get diagnostics either ("LSPs don't seem to want to give diagnostics for vdocs when they aren't in the workspace"), and their shipped fix was NOT a lighter workaround — it was abandoning "forward to the user's installed extension" entirely in favor of spawning and owning dedicated `vscode-languageclient` connections directly to specific servers (Pyrefly, Ark), a materially heavier architecture with its own disclosed cleanup hacks and a known cosmetic bug. `microsoft/vscode-jupyter` (MIT), independently researched, hit the identical wall solving an architecturally similar problem and also landed on "spawn and own the LSP client directly" (old) or VS Code's Notebook API's native LSP 3.17 document sync (new, inapplicable to a single-`TextDocument` file format like `.qmd`). Three independent sources — one official-docs citation, one empirical test suite, one real shipped competitor's own admission — converging on the same conclusion is strong enough evidence to make a scope-changing architectural call in the PLAN, rather than discovering the wall mid-implementation. | Before assuming an existing request-forwarding architecture (or any VS Code provider pattern) generalizes to a NEW capability, check whether that capability is pull-based (has an `vscode.executeXxxProvider` command) or push-based (a `DiagnosticCollection`/similar proactive-publish model) — the two are not interchangeable, and VS Code's own Embedded Programming Languages guide states this limitation directly for diagnostics specifically. When a competitor's own public PR/changelog prose describes an unexpectedly heavy architecture for something that sounds like it should be simple, take that as a signal to verify firsthand (a scratch, throwaway Extension Development Host test — write, run, observe, delete, never commit) before assuming a lighter approach will work; three convergent independent sources (official docs, your own empirical test, a competitor's own admission) is enough to change a plan's recommended architecture, not just enough to flag a risk. |
| 76 | **A plan's own internal forward-reference ("the exact edits are in §N") can be wrong even when the plan's substantive reasoning is sound — the referenced section itself still needs to be read, not assumed present (Session 69, closing `BACKLOG.md` item 10 under Option A).** Session 68's plan (`docs/planning/2026-07-10-code-cell-diagnostics-plan.md` §1) stated: "if A is chosen, §8 has the exact `docs/POSIT-COMPARISON.md`/`BACKLOG.md` edits ready to make." §8, on inspection, is the "Alternatives considered" table — a rejected-alternatives comparison, not edit text. No literal ready-to-use edits exist anywhere in the plan document; the claim was aspirational, not accurate. This cost nothing this session (§3's rationale was specific enough to derive the actual edits directly), but a session that trusted the forward-reference at face value — searching for "§8," expecting to copy-paste — would have found nothing there and had to improvise the exact same work anyway, just after a wasted round-trip. | Before treating a plan's own internal cross-reference ("see §N for X") as load-bearing — especially one promising ready-to-use content rather than just supporting analysis — read §N directly first. A plan can promise content in a section it never actually wrote, the same way an external citation can go stale (Learning #7/#10), just within a single document instead of across files; the fix is the same discipline, applied one level closer to home. |
| 77 | **When forwarding a request to a per-computation virtual document via `vscode.executeXxxProvider`, not every provider family tolerates a REUSED vdoc URI the same way — test each one's caching behavior independently rather than assuming the existing recipe generalizes (Session 70, planning `BACKLOG.md` item 11, outline granularity / in-cell code symbols).** This project's existing `src/providers/embedded.ts` forwards completion/hover/definition/signature-help against a STABLE per-(document, language) vdoc URI, rebuilt-per-request (`VirtualDocStore`) — and a firsthand empirical test this session confirmed `vscode.executeCompletionItemProvider` correctly reflects an edit on that reused URI (no staleness). But the SAME test pattern applied to `vscode.executeDocumentSymbolProvider` found the opposite: a fetch-count-instrumented `TextDocumentContentProvider` showed the content WAS re-fetched (count 1→2) on a second call to the same URI, yet the RETURNED SYMBOLS still reflected the FIRST call's content — even across a real 300ms wall-clock gap, ruling out a timing artifact. This is independently corroborated by a 2025 comment on `microsoft/vscode#108722`, found by a parallel research fork without being prompted with this specific finding, stating `executeDocumentSymbolProvider` "appears to hit an internal cache rather than freshly re-invoking the provider." Separately, the same session confirmed firsthand that `vscode.DocumentSymbolProvider` has no `onDidChange`/refresh event (unlike `CodeLensProvider`, `FoldingRangeProvider`, etc., confirmed directly against the current `vscode.d.ts`) — a long-standing, still-open VS Code API gap (`microsoft/vscode#108722`) — and that the sanctioned workaround for a live settings-driven refresh is to DISPOSE and RE-REGISTER the provider (confirmed independently via VS Code core's own notebook-outline source, which listens to the language-feature registry's `onDidChange`, and via Posit's own real, review-refined implementation of this exact feature, whose reviewers explicitly rejected a no-op-edit hack in favor of re-registration). | (a) Before reusing an existing request-forwarding vdoc-URI convention for a NEW provider type, empirically test THAT specific provider's caching behavior on a reused URI (an instrumented content-provider fetch-counter is a cheap, decisive probe) rather than assuming it behaves like the providers already proven in production — pull-based `vscode.executeXxxProvider` commands are not a monolithic family; some (completion, and by earlier findings hover/definition/signature-help) tolerate a stable reused URI, at least one (`executeDocumentSymbolProvider`) does not. (b) Before designing a live-refresh mechanism for any VS Code provider interface, check whether that specific provider interface exposes a refresh event (`onDidChangeCodeLenses` and siblings) — if it doesn't, dispose-and-re-register the provider registration itself, which fires the language-feature registry's own internal change event; this is VS Code's actual sanctioned pattern (confirmed via its own core source), not a workaround of last resort. |
| 78 | **`vscode.executeDocumentSymbolProvider`'s caching (Learning #77) is NOT specific to virtual/synthetic-scheme documents — it also stales a REAL on-disk document across an unrelated config change, which made this slice's OWN toggle untestable via the standard command API until re-registration was implemented (Session 71, implementing `BACKLOG.md` item 11 slice 1).** Session 70's plan grounded the caching finding only against a synthetic-scheme vdoc, so slice 1's design treated dispose-and-re-register as UI-refresh polish (plan §2.4) layered on top of an already-correct filter. Implementing test-first surfaced otherwise: an integration test that opened `test/fixtures/sample.qmd` (a REAL file, already queried once by an earlier test in the same suite run), flipped `quarto.symbols.showCodeCellsInOutline` to `false`, and re-queried via `vscode.executeDocumentSymbolProvider` got the STALE pre-toggle result (4 cell children, not 0) — even though the provider's own `provideDocumentSymbols` reads the config live on every call and was structurally correct. Only after adding the dispose-and-re-register listener (`vscode.workspace.onDidChangeConfiguration` → dispose + re-register the `DocumentSymbolProvider`) did the SAME test pass. The likely mechanism: this command's cache keys on document VERSION, not just URI+content — a config-only change never bumps a document's version (no text edit occurred), so VS Code has no signal to invalidate the cached computation for either a real or virtual document; only a fresh provider REGISTRATION (a language-feature-registry event) forces a re-query. | Before assuming a `vscode.executeXxxProvider` caching finding is scoped to the specific document type (virtual vs. real) it was empirically tested against, test it against a REAL, previously-queried document too — a config-driven (not text-edit-driven) change is the scenario most likely to reveal a version-keyed cache the original test didn't exercise. For ANY VS Code provider whose output depends on live `vscode.workspace.getConfiguration()` state rather than document content alone, dispose-and-re-register on the relevant config change is not optional UI polish — it may be required for basic command-API correctness, provable with a real `@vscode/test-electron` integration test (open the doc once, change config, re-query, assert non-stale) before assuming a live-read config check alone is sufficient. |
| 79 | **GUI screenshot automation (`screencapture`) is blocked in this session's sandboxed Bash execution context — no macOS Screen Recording (TCC) permission — even though launching a real, isolated Extension Development Host GUI window via the downloaded `.vscode-test` binary works fine (Session 71, verifying `BACKLOG.md` item 11 slice 1).** Following the `verify` skill's cold-start guidance, launched `.vscode-test/vscode-darwin-arm64-*/Visual Studio Code.app/.../bin/code` directly with `--extensionDevelopmentPath`/`--disable-extensions`/isolated `--user-data-dir`+`--extensions-dir` (a short `/tmp`-rooted path — a long `--user-data-dir` under this session's scratchpad hit a real ~103-char unix-socket-path limit, `EINVAL` on the IPC handle) — the process tree confirmed a genuinely live GUI instance (8 child processes: renderer, GPU, utility, network). `osascript` process-activation (`set frontmost of process ... to true`) succeeded with no error, but `screencapture -x` failed outright (`could not create image from display`) — an OS-level permission wall a script cannot route around, distinct from Session 12's own successful screenshot pass (Learning #19), which relied on the OPERATOR interactively pressing F5 and pasting screenshots via the chat UI's own clipboard-capture path (landing in `~/.claude/image-cache/`), never scripted `screencapture`. | A future session attempting an unattended/scripted F5 visual pass (vs. Session 12's human-driven, chat-pasted-screenshot approach) should expect `screencapture` to fail in this environment without a prior, manually-granted Screen Recording permission — don't spend time debugging `osascript`/process-targeting logic first, since that part works; the failure is the final capture call. If a short `--user-data-dir` isn't used, expect an `EINVAL` IPC-socket error unrelated to permissions — keep VS Code CLI scratch profile dirs under a short path (e.g. `/tmp`, not a long per-session scratchpad path). | 80 | **Once Screen Recording (Learning #79) is granted, scripting actual UI *interaction* (not just a screenshot) into a custom-launched `.vscode-test` Extension Development Host hits two FURTHER, independent obstacles — both silent, neither raising an error (Session 72, completing the `BACKLOG.md` Active item — the outline toggle's pixel-level F5 visual verification, `BACKLOG.md` item 11 slice 1).** (a) **The EDH process is not enumerable by `System Events`, even with Accessibility permission correctly granted.** `tell application "System Events" to get every process whose name is "Code"` — by name, by index, and by an exhaustive scan of `unix id of every process` for the EDH's own confirmed-live pid — only ever resolved to the regular `/Applications/Visual Studio Code.app` instance; the `.vscode-test` copy (a separate `.app` bundle at a nonstandard path, same likely bundle ID) never appeared, despite `ps` confirming it alive with 6 real child processes throughout. The fix: activate it by its exact POSIX bundle path — `tell application "/full/path/to/.vscode-test/vscode-darwin-arm64-*/Visual Studio Code.app" to activate` — which bypasses `System Events` process enumeration entirely and disambiguates from the already-running canonical copy of the same name. (b) **Frontmost focus does NOT persist between separate Bash tool calls.** An `activate` that succeeded (verified via `tell application "System Events" to get name of first process whose frontmost is true`) was reliably undone by the time the NEXT, separately-invoked `osascript` command ran — frontmost had silently reverted to `iTerm2` (the terminal hosting this session), with no error at any step, routing keystrokes like Cmd+Shift+E into iTerm2 instead of the EDH. Only chaining `activate; sleep; keystroke/click; sleep; screencapture` inside ONE bash invocation kept focus stable long enough to act. Blind coordinate-based `click at {x,y}` (a lesser-known but real `System Events` primitive, working even without addressing any specific process) was tried as a workaround before this was diagnosed and twice landed clicks in unrelated foreground apps (System Settings, a Safari tab) — risky in a desktop with many other windows open; asking the operator to bring the target window forward once, then driving it via keyboard shortcuts only, was safer and ultimately what worked. | Before scripting keystrokes/clicks into any GUI app launched from a nonstandard path (a `.vscode-test` EDH, or similar): (1) activate it via `tell application "<full POSIX .app path>" to activate`, not by process/application name, which is ambiguous against an already-running canonical copy and may not even enumerate the target at all via `System Events`. (2) Bundle the activate call and every subsequent interaction (keystroke, click, confirming screenshot) into a SINGLE shell/osascript invocation — never split "activate" and "act" across separate tool calls, since this harness's terminal host reliably reclaims frontmost focus in between, silently, with no error to catch. (3) Prefer keyboard-shortcut-driven interaction (Cmd+Shift+P + typed command + Enter) over blind coordinate clicks whenever multiple other windows/desktops are on screen — a coordinate click is only as safe as the last screenshot's window layout, which can be stale or wrong the moment focus has silently shifted. |

The fix was to redesign the test to EDIT the cell body between two `executeDocumentSymbolProvider` calls (mirroring `embedded.test.ts`'s own "reflects an edit on the next completion" pattern) — which reliably triggers a second computation and correctly proves the version-varying URI defeats the cache in the scenario that actually matters (a user typing inside a cell after the outline was already computed once). | Before writing an integration test that calls `vscode.executeXxxProvider` twice to prove a per-computation/per-request invariant (a fresh vdoc URI, a re-read config value, etc.), confirm what actually triggers a SECOND invocation of the target provider for that specific command family — for `executeDocumentSymbolProvider`, it is a real text edit (or a provider re-registration, e.g. Learning #78's dispose/re-register on config change), NOT a bare repeated command call on an unchanged document.
