| 82 | **Two FURTHER GUI-automation obstacles beyond Learnings #79/#80, one of which caused a real near-miss (Session 74, completing the pixel-level F5 visual pass for `BACKLOG.md` item 11 slice 2).** (a) **The isolated `.vscode-test` Extension Development Host and the operator's own real, already-running `/Applications/Visual Studio Code.app` share the EXACT SAME bundle identifier (`com.microsoft.VSCode`).** When both are alive simultaneously, `tell application "<full POSIX .app path>" to activate` (Learning #80's own documented fix) is NOT reliable disambiguation — AppleScript's `application` object model resolves by bundle ID, and repeatedly brought the operator's real window forward instead, even though `System Events` process enumeration correctly listed both as separate PIDs with distinct window titles. | 87 | **A BACKLOG item's own "reuses existing infrastructure" framing can be the plan's central wrong assumption, not a shortcut — verify by empirical census, not by re-reading the item's own prose (Session 79, planning `BACKLOG.md` item 14, `_quarto.yml` document links + filepath autocompletion).** Item 14's text said the feature "reuses existing `core/project-yaml.ts`/`core/yaml-context.ts` infrastructure" (the `project:`/`website:`/`book:` closed-schema scan built for Session 47's diagnostics feature). A 6-agent research `Workflow` — including a from-scratch Python traversal of the installed Quarto schema (`yaml-intelligence-resources.json`) counting every `path`/`maybeArrayOf:path`/`arrayOf:path`-typed field, independently re-verified by 2 adversarial verifiers — found that infrastructure resolves only 15 of 50 empirically-confirmed path-typed fields; the other 35 (`bibliography`, `csl`, `css`, `template`, `include-in-header`, and 30 more) live in the general document front-matter schema, a container that infrastructure never walks. Independently, grounding Posit's OWN shipped PR #906 via public-only facts (PR description/CHANGELOG/discussion — never their AGPL source) found their real implementation is NOT schema-driven at all — a whole-document heuristic ("fairly hackish parsing... then each match is checked to exist" against disk), confirming the same conclusion from the opposite direction. The operator confirmed (mid-planning `AskUserQuestion`) building the whole-document, existence-checked heuristic instead of the narrower schema-scoped reading of the item's own text. Also confirmed a genuine, disclosable POSITIVE finding in the same research pass: unlike the diagnostics feature (which needed raw `onDid*TextDocument` events + a manual filename gate + debounce + a generation counter because `DiagnosticCollection` has no selector-based routing), a `DocumentLinkProvider`/`CompletionItemProvider` CAN use a pattern-based `DocumentSelector` (`{pattern:"**/_quarto.{yml,yaml}"}`) and let VS Code itself route calls — genuinely simpler than the precedent it might have been assumed to inherit. | When a BACKLOG item's own text claims a new feature "reuses existing infrastructure," treat that as a hypothesis to verify empirically (a grep-based/schema-based census, or a live probe), not as ground truth carried into the plan — this project's own history (YAML diagnostics, onboarding walkthrough, outline granularity, notebook conversion, image paste, and now this item) shows "reuses existing infrastructure" items correct their own scope at planning time FAR more often than they confirm it. Don't assume a new provider type must inherit the wiring complexity of the most recently built similar-sounding feature — check whether VS Code's own selector/registration mechanics (pattern-based `DocumentSelector`) already solve the problem the older feature's event-driven workaround existed only because of a DIFFERENT API's limitation (`DiagnosticCollection` specifically, not `_quarto.yml`-scoping in general). |
| 88 | **A plan's own worked example can under-enumerate the real-world input shape space; the adversarial review of the SHIPPED slice is where the omission gets caught — run it even when every layer is green (Session 80, implementing `BACKLOG.md` item 14 Slice 1, the `_quarto.yml` `DocumentLinkProvider`).** The Session 79 plan's §2.5 dragon example showed sequence-item path values only as *bare scalars* (`- data/raw.csv`, `- "images/logo.png"`), so the first implementation's `- ` branch reused `valueSlotAfterColon` with the dash position as the value delimiter — correct for a scalar item, but it grabbed the WHOLE inline mapping as one token for the dominant real-world shape, `- href: page.qmd` / `- part: intro.qmd` (website navbar/sidebar `contents:`, book `chapters:` part lists). Every layer was green (695 unit / 271 integration / clean `.vsix`) because the fixture and tests, written from the same plan, only exercised the bare-scalar shape the plan enumerated — a closed loop of plan → tests → impl all sharing the same blind spot. A 24-agent adversarial-review `Workflow` run on the shipped slice (this project's standing post-implementation practice, e.g. the Session 47 YAML-diagnostics 26-agent review) surfaced the miss from FOUR independent finder dimensions (correctness/path-safety/test-faithfulness/plan-conformance), each re-deriving it by running the actual scanner, and 8 verifier votes confirmed it — proof the review catches what plan-derived tests structurally cannot. Root-cause fix: a `mappingColonIndex` helper that anchors the value at the YAML mapping colon (the first `:` followed by whitespace/EOL) for BOTH `key: value` lines and `- key: value` items, which also fixed a low-severity colon-in-quoted-key case (`"a:b": v`) for free. Two shapes were deliberately NOT fixed and filed as documented, fail-safe limitations: flow collections (`chapters: [a, b]` — the whole `[...]` stays one token, never split; block style is idiomatic in `_quarto.yml`, and the plan §2.5/§5.1 explicitly scoped the scanner to block style), and a pathological quoted key containing `": "` (astronomically rare, matches the existing `project-yaml.ts` `keySpanAt` naive-colon precedent). Both degrade to a MISSING link, never a wrong one — the existence-check safety design (plan §2.1) makes every scanner gap fail-safe by construction. | When implementing a parser/scanner from a plan, do not treat the plan's worked examples as the full input-shape inventory — enumerate the real-world shape space independently (for `_quarto.yml` sequence values: bare scalar `- x`, inline mapping `- key: x`, continuation-line mapping, flow collection), because plan → fixture → test written by one author share one blind spot and all pass green together. Run the standing adversarial-review `Workflow` on the SHIPPED slice even when every layer is green — it is specifically the step that catches a shape the plan-derived tests never exercised (here, 4 independent finder dimensions all found the same real defect). When a scanner's failure mode is fail-safe by an existence/validity gate downstream (as here), an un-handled input shape is a documentable limitation to defer, not necessarily a blocker — but only after confirming the downstream gate genuinely makes it fail-safe (a missing link, never a wrong one), not merely assuming it does. |
| 89 | **A new cursor-position slot detector needs BOTH bounds; when a sibling detector already exists in the codebase, mirror its contract instead of deriving a one-sided guard — and don't let a downstream consumer's incidental filtering hide a core-layer bug (Session 81, implementing `BACKLOG.md` item 14 Slice 2, the `_quarto.yml` filepath `CompletionItemProvider`).** `valueContextAt` (new, `core/project-links.ts`) was written with only a LOWER guard (`col <= colonAbs → null`, the key-position case that has its own gate-d test) but no UPPER bound, so a cursor PAST the value token — in trailing whitespace or a trailing inline comment (`chapters: intro.qmd  # note`, cursor in `# note`) — returned a context whose `token` captured the comment chars and whose `replaceRange` (via `endCol = Math.max(slot.endCol, col)`) overran the comment; accepting a filepath item would have corrupted the line. This exactly diverged from the codebase's OWN sibling detector, `yaml-context.ts`'s `frontMatterContextAt`, which guards `col >= valueSlot.startCol && col <= valueSlot.endCol` — a contract that was right there to mirror and wasn't. The 7-agent adversarial review (5 finder lenses × 2-skeptic refutation; 4 lenses clean) split on it: one skeptic REJECTED (VS Code's suggest widget computes the fuzzy-filter word from the item range start to the cursor, so the overrun token `intro.qmd  #` matches no filename and every item is filtered out of the widget — no user-visible corruption reachable through normal accept), one CONFIRMED (the pure-function bug + contract divergence are real and reproducible; manual Ctrl+Space reaches the provider). Arbitrated as FIX, not defer: core correctness and contract-consistency with an existing sibling must not depend on a downstream consumer's incidental filtering accident — "VS Code happens to hide it" is exactly the works-by-accident fragility strict TDD + adversarial review exist to remove, and the plan's §5.1 docstring for `valueContextAt` even listed "a comment" among the null cases (it caught a whole comment LINE but not a cursor in a trailing inline comment). One-line fix (`col > slot.endCol → null`), RED→GREEN, mirroring the sibling bound. | When writing a cursor-position context detector (completion/hover/signature), enumerate BOTH edges of the slot — cursor before the value start AND cursor past the value end — not just the key/value split; a one-sided guard passes every in-value test while silently mis-firing outside the value. Before deriving the guard, grep for an existing sibling detector in the same codebase (`completionContextAt`/`frontMatterContextAt`/`nestedKeyContextAt` here) and mirror its exact bounds — a divergence between two detectors of the same shape is a latent bug. And when triaging a split-verdict review finding, weigh a "the downstream consumer neutralizes it" refutation as evidence the impact is currently narrow, NOT as license to ship a core-layer function that is wrong on its own terms: fix the core, don't rely on the filter. |
| 90 | **Threading a new dimension (`{to}` format) through a lifecycle-owning manager can introduce a session-identity race that all-green happy-path tests miss; a per-`fsPath` handler is unsafe once the same key can be re-occupied by a DIFFERENT session in one process lifetime — the adversarial review of the shipped slice caught it (Session 82, implementing `BACKLOG.md` item 15, `quarto.previewFormat`).** Adding a per-format restart to `PreviewManager.openPreview` (dispose the old session, spawn a new one with `--to <fmt>`) reused the existing `child.on("close")` handler, which decided "the server died unexpectedly" via `this.sessions.has(fsPath)` and always called `this.disposeSession(fsPath)` — both keyed on `fsPath` ALONE. That invariant ("a session still present at this key ⇒ THIS child's server self-died") held while one document mapped to at most one session over its whole lifetime, but the restart broke it: after `dispose(A)` + `spawn(B)`, the dying old child `A` fires its still-registered `close` late, sees `sessions.has(fsPath)` true (because `B` now occupies the key), and disposes `B` — killing the just-requested preview. All 3 original integration tests were green because each started from `previewProcessCount()===0` and never re-invoked preview on an already-previewed document, so the entire restart branch (the slice's marquee behavior) was unexercised — a closed happy-path loop. A 21-agent adversarial review (5 finder lenses × 2-skeptic refutation) surfaced SIX real survivors that green tests missed: the HIGH session-identity race, a HIGH "restart branch untested" coverage gap, the MEDIUM flow-collection garbage-token bug (`format: {html: default}` — a valid YAML flow mapping — parsed as one opaque `--to {html: default}` token instead of `["html"]`, because the scalar branch fired before the block branch and `unquote` doesn't strip `{`/`[`), a MEDIUM untested `["html"]` fallback (a `:[]` mutation → silent no-op survived green), a MEDIUM weak gate-d discriminator (the sole fixture declared exactly `["html","docx"]`, byte-identical to a plausible hardcode, so a `parseDeclaredFormats(...)`→`["html","docx"]` literal severing the parser from the picker survived), and a LOW `starting`-guard-drops-differing-format-during-startup race. Fixes at the root: a `session`-identity guard (`this.sessions.get(fsPath) === session`) on the close/error/timeout handlers so a stale child's exit is a no-op; destructure flow collections in `parseDeclaredFormats`; a no-format fixture asserting the picker offers `["html"]` (which ALSO discriminates the hardcode — captured `["html"]` ≠ the `["html","docx"]` constant); and reordering the `starting` check BEFORE the restart-dispose so an in-flight differing-format request degrades to a benign drop instead of a teardown-then-nothing. **Honest verification boundary:** the session-identity race is real by code-reading (both skeptics confirmed) but TIMING-DEPENDENT — in this integration environment the old child dies fast (before the new session installs), so `disposeSession` no-ops and the race does not deterministically reproduce; break-reverting the guard did NOT turn the restart test red. So the guard is a correct-by-construction defensive fix, not a break-revert-proven one; what IS break-revert-proven is finding #3's deterministic coverage (deleting the restart-branch dispose → the "html server must be reaped" assertion goes red) and finding #4/#5's fallback (removing `["html"]` → the no-format test goes red). Distinguishing "correct by construction, race not reproducible here" from "break-revert-proven" in the write-up is the honest close-out, not claiming a red that never happened. Separately, item 15's own "moderate scope" BACKLOG framing understated `previewScript` (Posit's `quartoRenderScriptActive` context key needs content-scanning a `.py`/`.jl`/`.r` script for `# %%` percent-cells — no precedent in this codebase, where the only context key `quarto.inCodeCell` is cursor-driven); surfaced to the operator via `AskUserQuestion` (the item-14/Learning #87 pattern) → scoped this session to `previewFormat` only, `previewScript` deferred to its own session. Grounded the CLI mechanics firsthand and safely: `quarto preview <file> --no-browser --to <fmt>` verified by running it with `--timeout <n>` so the server SELF-EXITS (no orphan, no manual kill needed) — the way to empirically test a long-lived-server CLI command without leaving a process behind. | When extending a manager that owns per-key lifecycle state (one live process/panel/connection per document/uri) with a new operation that can REPLACE the entry for a key mid-lifetime (restart, re-open, switch-variant), audit EVERY late-firing handler (`close`/`exit`/`error`/timeout/`onDidDispose`) that was written keyed on that key alone: capture the specific `session`/`instance` object in the handler's closure and guard side effects on `map.get(key) === thatObject`, because "the entry still exists at this key" no longer implies "it is the same entry this handler belongs to." Run the standing adversarial-review `Workflow` on the shipped slice even when every layer is green — a "restart/replace" branch that works in the happy path but is never re-invoked by a from-zero test is exactly the marquee behavior the review catches (here 6 real survivors from an all-green slice). To test a CLI that spawns a long-lived server, use its own self-exit/timeout flag (`quarto preview … --timeout N`) rather than spawning-and-killing. And in close-out, distinguish a correct-by-construction fix for a non-deterministically-reproducible race from a break-revert-proven one — write which is which, never imply a red you did not observe. |
| 91 | **When grounding a "does the CLI accept this file?" detection predicate, enumerate EVERY engine's `claimsFile` path — not just the first one you find — and copy the CLI's regex only after checking it is not itself buggy; the adversarial review of a PLAN (not just code) caught both misses (Session 83, PLANNING `BACKLOG.md` item 15's second half, `quarto.previewScript`).** Quarto decides a `.py`/`.jl`/`.r` file's execution engine in `fileExecutionEngineAndTarget` (quarto.js:42199) by polling each engine's `claimsFile`. There are **two independent render-script paths**, not one: (A) **Jupyter percent scripts** — `isJupyterPercentScript` (quarto.js:37362), ext ∈ {`.py`,`.jl`,`.r`} (case-insensitive) + `/^\s*#\s*%%+\s+\[markdown|raw\]/.test(text)`, run by the jupyter engine (the julia engine :41498 reuses the same predicate for `.jl`); and (B) **knitr *spin* scripts** — `isKnitrSpinScript` (quarto.js:28691), ext `.r`/`.R` + `/^\s*#'\s*---[\s\S]+?\s*#'\s*---/.test(text)` (roxygen `#' ---`…`#' ---` header), run by the **knitr** engine. Session 82's handoff (and this session's OWN first draft) modeled only path A — a real, shippable false-negative for the common R spin workflow. **The path-A regex is also buggy:** it has no group, so `|` (lowest precedence) splits it into `(^\s*#\s*%%+\s+\[markdown)` OR `(raw\])` — the second branch is UNANCHORED, matching the literal substring `raw]` ANYWHERE (verified firsthand: `quarto render` accepts a code-only `.py` containing `arr[raw]`). A naive verbatim copy of that regex would false-positive on ordinary code — and, worse, silently hijack a content-driven `quartoRenderScriptActive` context key (and its `Ctrl+Shift+K` keybinding) while the user edits normal Python/R/Julia. The fix is the CLI regex with its one obvious bug fixed by grouping (`\[(markdown|raw)\]`), a deliberate + disclosed divergence that agrees with the CLI on all conventionally-structured scripts and diverges only on the buggy `raw]`-junk. Two firsthand-grounded bonuses: **jupyter absence does NOT block testing** — a knitr spin `.R` script (`quarto check knitr` OK: R 4.6.1/knitr 1.51) previews END-TO-END with a real `Browse at http://localhost:7454/` URL and NO kernel, so it is the kernel-free accept-path integration fixture; and `quarto preview script.py` with missing jupyter **fast-fails in ~1s** (verified: `spawnPreview`'s `child.on("close")` settles the promise — NOT a 60s `START_TIMEOUT_MS` hang), so a percent-script accept-path test does not hang, but it surfaces Quarto's own "exited before it was ready" error (a different string from the gate's reject message — assert on the gate string specifically). Also: this project has NO `quarto.preview` keybinding (Posit's "mutually-exclusive `Ctrl+Shift+K`" is Posit's scheme, adopting it ADDS a key to `quarto.preview`); and the extension's `activationEvents` (`onLanguage:quarto`/`onLanguage:yaml`) do not fire for a bare script, so a content-driven context key needs Posit-style `onLanguage:r` + `workspaceContains:**/*.{qmd,rmd}` + `workspaceContains:**/_quarto.{yml,yaml}` (still NOT covering a lone script outside a Quarto workspace — a disclosed limitation Posit shares). Plan: `docs/planning/2026-07-12-preview-script-plan.md`. | When planning/implementing any feature that must decide "would the underlying CLI/tool accept this input?": grep the tool's source for ALL detector/claim entry points (every `claimsFile`, every engine, every `is*Script`) and enumerate the COMPLETE surface before writing the predicate — the first path you find is rarely the only one. Copy the tool's regex only after reading it for bugs (unanchored alternations, missing groups, missing flags); if it is buggy, decide consciously between bug-for-bug fidelity and intent-faithful-with-disclosed-divergence, and add tests that DISCRIMINATE the two (a battery that passes for both proves nothing). Run the standing adversarial-review `Workflow` on a PLAN, not just shipped code — an independent completeness lens that enumerates the whole surface catches the second code path a first-path-scoped author misses. And re-verify every agent-raised finding firsthand before acting on it — the reviewer is a lead, not an oracle. |
| 92 | **A regex copied verbatim from a CLI can be a performance bug in an editor even when it is functionally correct — and an all-accept test battery cannot tell you the content half of a predicate is dead.** Two independent defects in one small pure detector, both invisible to a green suite. (1) **ReDoS by inheritance.** `src/core/render-script.ts`'s spin regex was copied from Quarto's `isKnitrSpinScript` (quarto.js:28695), including a redundant `\s*` between the lazy `[\s\S]+?` and `#'`. Redundant ≠ harmless: it is *ambiguous* with the lazy quantifier, so a FAILING match (a `#' ---` header opened and never closed — exactly what a user mid-edit has) backtracks **quadratically** in the longest contiguous whitespace run. 80k blank lines = **3.7 seconds** with the extension host blocked. Quarto gets away with it because the CLI runs it once per render in a Deno subprocess; VS Code runs it on a single shared UI thread. Deleting one `\s*` → 0.03 ms, provably same language. **The lesson is not "don't copy the CLI" — it is that a predicate's *cost model* does not transfer across execution contexts even when its *semantics* do.** The plan had even certified this regex as "cheap on every keystroke" *because* it was lazy — exactly backwards, and the next slice was designed to rely on that certification. (2) **The surviving mutant.** Every `.r`/`.R` case in the 12-test battery was an ACCEPT, so `SPIN_HEADER.test(text)` was asserted by nothing: mutating the impl to `return ext === SPIN_EXTENSION` ("every `.r` file is a render script") passed all 738 tests. A battery built by walking a plan's table of *positive* examples reproduces the plan's blind spots. For any `A && B` predicate, ask which test would fail if `B` were deleted — if none, `B` is untested no matter how many tests pass. | When copying a regex or algorithm from a CLI/batch tool into an editor extension (or any latency-sensitive, single-threaded host): re-derive its worst-case cost in the NEW context — an unclosed-delimiter/failing-match input is the one to time, not the happy path — and add a timing lock. When writing a test battery for a boolean predicate: for each conjunct, write the test that dies if that conjunct is deleted, and break-revert-prove it against that exact mutant. Both defects here were found by the standing adversarial-review `Workflow` on an all-green slice, not by the tests. |
| 93 | **A context key can only be true once the extension is ACTIVE — so it must never gate the UI path that CAUSES activation. And when a plan states both a gate and a mitigation that silently depends on that gate's absence, transcribing both ships the contradiction.** Session 85 (implementing `BACKLOG.md` item 15 Slice 2, the `quarto.previewScript` gating layer) shipped an all-green slice in which `quarto.previewScript` had become **completely unreachable** for its flagship use case — a lone `analysis.py` percent script in a non-Quarto folder. The chain: `quartoRenderScriptActive` is `setContext`'d only inside `registerPreviewFeature` (i.e. only *post*-activation); no `activationEvent` matches a lone `.py`/`.jl` (deliberately — activating for every Python file is too costly, Posit's judgment and ours); and **VS Code does not activate an extension in order to evaluate a `when` clause**, so an extension-owned key that was never set is unset → falsy → the menu entry is hidden. Gating the *palette* entry on that key therefore closed the last of the three invocation paths at once (keybinding false, editor-title hidden, palette hidden) — a strict **regression**, since one commit earlier the command had *no* palette entry, was thus shown unconditionally, and invoking it auto-activated the extension (`engines ^1.90.0` ≥ 1.74) and previewed the script. **The plan contained both halves of the contradiction**: §5.3 prescribes `palette when: "quartoRenderScriptActive"` *and*, four lines away, mitigates the activation gap with "the palette command still works (auto-activates on invoke)" — mutually exclusive, and the session shipped both, its own new test comment asserting the mitigation the manifest beside it had just destroyed. Found by 5 of 6 independent review lenses; neither skeptic could refute it. **Fix: gate the palette on a BUILT-IN context key VS Code can evaluate while we are inactive** (`resourceExtname == .py \|\| …`, precedent: `quarto.convertToQmd`) — preserving auto-activation-on-invoke *and* the operator's intent of not listing the command on a `.qmd`. **When to apply:** (a) before gating ANY menu/palette contribution on an extension-owned context key, ask "can this key be true before we are active — and is this entry the thing that activates us?"; if the entry is the activation path, gate it only on built-in keys (`resourceExtname`, `editorLangId`, `resourceScheme`). (b) Treat a plan's "this still works because X" mitigations as **claims to test against the gates the same plan prescribes**, not prose to transcribe — this is Learning #92's lesson recurring one slice later, and the session demonstrably *could* apply it (it caught, and escalated to the operator, that the plan's prescribed `ctrl+shift+k` is VS Code's built-in **Delete Line** — `editor.action.deleteLines`, `primary: 3113`, weight 100 vs an external extension's 400 — which would have hijacked that command in *every* editor) yet failed to apply the same skepticism to the palette clause in the very same manifest. Two adjacent prescriptions, one tested and one transcribed. |
| 94 | **A test double registered on the same axis the real dependency discriminates by cannot detect that the real dependency REJECTS that axis — and a 100%-green suite will hide a totally dead feature (Session 86, PLANNING item 16; discovered a SHIPPED defect, now BACKLOG item 18).** This project's embedded-language forwarding (Phase 6e completion/hover/go-to-def/signature-help, plus in-cell outline symbols and format-cell) routes requests through a **virtual document under a CUSTOM URI scheme** (`quarto-embedded`, `quarto-outline-symbols`, `quarto-format-cell`). Real language servers filter by **scheme** in their LSP `documentSelector` (typically `file:`/`untitled:`/`vscode-notebook-cell:`), so **no provider is ever registered for our vdoc**: `executeXxxProvider` returns nothing, with **no error and no warning**. Proven firsthand against real Pylance with a passing control in the same Extension Development Host run — real `file:` `.py` → **306 completions / 1 hover / 2 doc-symbols / 2 semantic tokens**, identical content on our vdoc → **0 / 0 / 0 / none** (visibility, an 8-second wait, and content-freshness all ruled out as confounds). The feature has been **dead in production for `{python}` since it shipped**, while every one of its tests passed. The reason is mechanical, not sloppiness: each stand-in is registered as **`{ scheme: <our-custom-scheme> }`** — a selector pinned to *precisely the axis* the real server discriminates on. Such a double asserts "a request routed through our scheme reaches a provider registered on our scheme," which is trivially true and says **nothing** about whether any *real* server registers there. Adding more stand-in tests can never detect this; only a test against the **real dependency** can, and it needs a **control** proving the real dependency was alive (otherwise "no results" is indistinguishable from "the server never started"). Generalization: whenever a double stands in for an external dependency, ask *what does the real thing discriminate on?* (scheme, content-type, auth scope, protocol version, file extension) — and make sure the double is **not** keyed on that same thing. If it is, the test is a tautology wearing a green checkmark. | Session 86 (planning item 16) — the spike that refuted item 16's premise and uncovered the shipped defect; `docs/planning/2026-07-12-embedded-lsp-scheme-and-semantic-tokens-plan.md` §3/§10.1 | When writing ANY test double for an external dependency (a language server, an API client, a CLI, a plugin host). Identify the axis the real dependency filters/dispatches on, and ensure the double does not key on that same axis. And ship at least ONE test against the real dependency, with a control that proves it was alive. |
| 95 | **Repairing a dead code path makes its guards live for the first time — audit them as NEW code, not as shipped code.** `format-cell.ts`'s `rangeWithinCell` had guarded the formatter's edits for many sessions and had never once rejected a real edit, because the forward it guarded was dead (no real formatter registered for our custom URI scheme, so `executeFormatDocumentProvider` always returned zero edits). The only thing that had ever exercised it was a stand-in emitting body-confined edits. The moment Session 87 repaired the forward, a real formatter answered — and the guard was wrong in the worst possible way: it filtered on `findCellAtPosition`, which is **INCLUSIVE of a cell's fence lines**, where its sibling in `embedded.ts` (`rangeInSameLangBody`) correctly used `embeddedCellAt` (**body-only**, `null` on fences and `#\|` lines). The vdoc blanks non-body lines to whitespace-only runs; a real formatter trims those; the trims landed on the fences; and the guard waved them through. Result, observed end-to-end against VS Code's own JS formatter: ``` ```{ojs}\nx   =   1\n``` ``` → `\nx = 1\n` — **both fences deleted, the user's cell destroyed.** Two guards written to mirror each other, one reached for the wrong primitive, and nothing caught it for many sessions because one of them was never exercised. | When a fix makes a previously-dead feature work: list every defensive/filtering/validation path downstream of it and treat each as unreviewed code. "It has been shipped for months" is not evidence it works if nothing real ever ran through it. Grep for a sibling implementation of the same guard and diff the primitives they use — a divergence there is the bug. |
| 96 | **A control must be POLLED, not sampled once — and its fixture must be something the real dependency can actually process.** Session 87's real-LSP harness twice reported a failure that was the *test's* fault, not the product's, and both would have been easy to mis-file as regressions. (a) The `{ojs}` no-regression test used a **bare assignment** (`x = "hello"`) — TypeScript cannot type an undeclared global in a `.js` file, so it returned 1 word-based item. It returned exactly 1 for a plain `.js` at the workspace root too, which is what proved the fixture, not the forwarding, was the problem; with `const x = "hello"` it returned **52 items through our vdoc, and 52 through the control**. (b) The JS control then failed intermittently because it *sampled once* while VS Code's TS/JS service starts **lazily** — the same discipline already applied to Pylance (`waitForPylance` polls) had not been applied to it. A single-sample control on a lazily-started dependency is a coin flip that reads as a product bug. | Whenever a test depends on an external service: (1) POLL a known-good control until it answers, never sample once — lazily-started servers are the norm, not the exception; (2) before believing a zero/low result, run the SAME fixture straight at the real dependency outside your code path — if it is also zero there, your fixture is wrong, not your code; (3) pick fixtures the dependency can genuinely resolve (declared, not implicit) or you will measure its limitations and call them your regressions. |
| 97 | **A break-revert battery only proves the lines it mutates. Enumerate the invariants, not the code you happened to think about.** Session 88 shipped `decodeTokens`/`encodeTokens` with a four-mutant break-revert battery (bitset copy / type filter / sort / `%5` guard) and called the verification strong. The adversarial review then proved by execution that deleting the decoder's per-line column reset — `char = deltaLine === 0 ? char + deltaChar : deltaChar`, **the single line that decides where every colour lands** — left all **777 unit + 304 integration tests green**. It survived because every multi-token stream in the suite happened to sit on ONE line, so `char` was always 0 when the line advanced and the two branches agreed; the real Pylance streams the feature exists to consume are never like that. The battery was not weak because it was small — it was weak because it was assembled from *the mutations the author had already thought of*, which is exactly the set the author's tests already cover. The fix is to derive the mutant list from the **behavioral invariants** ("tokens land at the right coordinates", "order is ascending", "unknown names never mis-colour") and confirm each has a test that dies without it — not from re-reading the diff. Note the shape: this is the same defect class as Learning #94 (a double keyed on the axis the real dependency discriminates by), one level up — there the *test double* agreed with the code about the wrong thing; here the *mutant battery* did. A suite can be green, and a battery can be "proven", and both can be measuring something other than the thing that matters. | Session 88 (item 16 Slice 1, semantic tokens): the review's headline finding, reproduced firsthand before being fixed | When claiming a break-revert battery as evidence: list the behavioral invariants the module promises FIRST, then show a dying test for each. If an invariant has no mutant, it has no test — however many tests exist. Especially load-bearing for pure translation/codec layers, where a coordinate or index bug is silent, plausible, and invisible to the type checker. |

| 98 | **A safety argument written in a doc-comment is not evidence — and the more confident it sounds, the less it was tested.** Session 89's 🐉8 fix canonicalized whitespace-only lines to `""` in `ensureVdoc`, and I wrote a three-bullet comment explaining why that was safe: *"Only whitespace-only lines are touched. A code line keeps its leading indentation byte-for-byte… No request or result has ever landed on a blanked line — that is what blanking is for."* Every clause was true of the *builders* and false of the function I had just written, which ran **downstream** of them and could not tell a blanked line from a blank **code** line. Blanked ⊊ whitespace-only, and the difference is where the user's cursor sits every time they press Enter inside a Python function. The full matrix passed — 803 unit, 314 integration, 12 real-LSP against real servers, all four consumers — because **not one fixture in the entire repository contains a whitespace-only line**. The break-revert battery passed too: it mutated the lines I had thought about. Only the adversarial review's completeness critic found it, by executing the function on a document I had not imagined. **The countermeasure is not "write better comments" — it is to notice that a safety argument is a claim about inputs, and to go find the input that violates it.** When you catch yourself writing "nothing ever X", grep the test corpus for X. If there are zero instances, you have not verified the claim — you have restated it. | Session 89 (item 16 Slice 2; the 🐉8 canonicalization regression, caught by the standing adversarial review) | Any time you justify a change with a stated invariant over inputs ("only blank lines", "never on a fence", "always inside a cell"). Before believing it: grep the fixtures for a case that would violate it. Zero hits means untested, not safe. This is Learning #94/#97 one level up — there the test double and then the mutant list agreed with the code about the wrong thing; here the *safety argument itself* did. |

| 99 | **When you layer a semantic feature over an existing one, the incumbent is EVIDENCE — read what it already gets right before "improving" it.** Session 90's D4 decision looked like arithmetic: our legend dropped 36% of real Pylance's tokens, so carry the foreign names and recover them. That answer is a **regression**. A `.qmd`'s `{python}` cell is *already* coloured by VS Code's bundled MagicPython TextMate grammar, so the semantic layer never paints a blank canvas — it paints **over** a grammar that is mostly right, and a carried-but-unstyleable name **overrides** TextMate rather than degrading to it. Carrying `magicFunction` would have turned `__init__` from #DCDCAA to #d2a8ff purple. The rule that fell out — **the triage rule** — is that a serving extension's own `contributes.semanticTokenScopes` entry for a type IS that author saying *"the superType default is the WRONG colour for this type"*; and because VS Code gates those entries on the **model's** languageId (`quarto`, never `python`), they are inert for us. So the set of names with **no** scope entry is exactly the set safe to carry bare — the same judgement the author already made, read back out of their manifest. Pylance ships an entry for `selfParameter`/`clsParameter`/`magicFunction`/`builtinConstant` + six punctuation types and **none** for `module`, which is also the one name MagicPython gives no scope at all. Generalizes past this feature: **before adding a layer, resolve what the existing layer already produces for the exact inputs you intend to override.** Two corollaries, both of which bit: the default dark theme in VS Code 1.126 is **"Dark 2026"**, not Dark+/Dark Modern — and Dark+ colours the decisive scopes *identically*, so validating there hides the whole problem; and a theme's colours must be resolved through VS Code's **theme trie** (deepest matching scope wins, over the fully-resolved `include` chain — 118 rules, not the 53 in the leaf file), not a naive prefix match, which is how three of this session's own hexes came out wrong. | Session 90 (item 16 Slice 3, D4) | Whenever a new layer (semantic tokens, diagnostics, inlay hints, decorations) sits ON TOP of an existing one. Resolve the incumbent's actual output first; "we currently drop N%" is a measure of *coverage*, never of *harm*. |
| 100 | **A legend is not a promise of EMISSION. An agreement between two things you control proves nothing about the wire.** Session 90 carried `intrinsic` into `OUR_LEGEND` because it passed Learning #99's triage rule perfectly (Pylance styles it nowhere). Real Pylance **never emits it**: its walker maps `DeclarationType.Intrinsic` to `{type: 15 (variable), modifiers: 512 (builtin)}`, and token type 18 has **zero** emission sites in every shipped bundle — it exists in the advertised legend only because the legend is generated from an enum. So the carry added a **dead legend entry and a dead `semanticTokenScopes` rule** — precisely the defect the session's own new test file declared it existed to prevent. It could not catch it, and the reason is the transferable part: that test pinned *scope-rules == legend*, **an invariant between two artifacts the author wrote**, which is always satisfiable by writing both consistently wrong. The unit test "proving" the carry fed `pylanceStream(4, 0, 3, 18, 0)` — a stream real Pylance **cannot produce** — a fabricated RED wearing the costume of evidence. Only the real-LSP gate could settle it, and it had been pointed at `module` and not at `intrinsic`, on the strength of a docstring saying "same rule, same reasoning." **When an external system is the authority on a fact, a test that never asks it is decoration.** Ask which of your assertions could still pass if the external dependency were replaced by a stub of your own imagination — those are the ones that prove nothing. (This is Learning #98's sibling: #98 is "a safety argument is a claim about inputs — go find the input that violates it"; #100 is "a claim about a DEPENDENCY's behaviour must be settled BY the dependency.") | Session 90 (item 16 Slice 3 — caught by the standing adversarial review, 11th consecutive slice, not by the author) | Any time you encode a fact about an external system (a server's legend, an API's response shape, a file format's field) into your own code or fixtures. Pin it against the REAL system, and be suspicious of any test whose fixtures you hand-authored from a spec rather than captured from the wire. |
| 101 | **A per-key guard cannot cover a global-teardown race — the teardown has no key to invalidate, and the racing operation may not be registered under any key yet.** Session 88 fixed the document-close vdoc-strand race with a PER-DOCUMENT epoch: `disposeVdocs(docUri)` is handed the exact key that closed and bumps *that* epoch, so an in-flight `ensureVdoc` snapshotting `epochOf(key.docUri)` before its awaits sees it move. The BACKLOG *and* Session 90's handoff framed the sibling **deactivate** race as the same fix — verbatim, *"Fix is one line (bump every owner's epoch in `disposeAllVdocs`)."* That is wrong, and the RED test proved it by stranding a real `vdoc-mit.*.py`: `disposeAllVdocs` gets no `docUri`, and the racing `ensureVdoc` has not yet reached its `live.set`/`filesOf`, so it is in NO map to enumerate — a per-owner sweep over `docFiles` cannot reach a forward that has registered nothing yet. The correct primitive for a GLOBAL teardown is a single monotonic **global** generation, snapshotted before the awaits and re-checked after: it invalidates every in-flight operation at once, keyed or not. The corollary that made it robust: put the bump **synchronous and FIRST**, before any await, because the teardown is itself fire-and-forget — `deactivate()` runs `void disposeAllVdocs()` and never awaits it, so the guard's correctness must not depend on the teardown's own async work completing. Two independent signals said "trivial" (a one-line filed fix; a green build), and both were wrong about the shape; only writing the failing test first surfaced it. | Session 91 (the `disposeAllVdocs` deactivate-strand fix; the filed "one line, per-owner" framing refuted by the RED test, then confirmed correct by a 9-agent review with 5/6 lenses empty) | Any time you extend a per-entity concurrency guard (per-document, per-request, per-connection epoch) to a whole-system teardown (deactivate, shutdown, dispose-all). Ask: what key does the teardown bump, and is the racing operation guaranteed to be under that key by the time it resumes? If either answer is "none"/"no", reach for a single monotonic global generation, bumped synchronously before any await — not a per-key one. Do not trust a filed "one-liner" for a concurrency fix until a RED test has shown the exact strand. |
| 102 | **The filed/"obvious" fix direction for a hard integration bug can be empirically wrong — and a firsthand spike against the REAL dependency beats source-reading and prior-art prose combined; the fix may live in a mechanism class nobody's framing considered.** For the `diagnosticMode:"workspace"` vdoc Problems-panel leak, "relocate the vdocs out of the workspace" was favored by the BACKLOG item, by Posit's own PR #980 prose (*"LSPs don't want to give diagnostics for vdocs when they aren't in the workspace"*), by a source-level Pyright read, AND by this plan's own first draft. A single `test:lsp` run under `QMD_LSP_DIAGMODE=workspace` **refuted it** — the OS-temp-relocated vdocs leaked identically, because Pyright injects tracked membership at `didOpen` time (`service.ts`), which is location-independent, so `_shouldCheckFile`'s `isTracked && workspace-mode` branch fires regardless of where the file lives. The fix that shipped came from a THIRD mechanism class the "config-inject vs own-a-client vs close-the-model" framing never contained: **in-content suppression** — a file-level `# type: ignore` on the vdoc's already-blanked line 0 (coordinate-safe because line 0 is never a code body line), confirmed firsthand to fully suppress the leak, preserve completion (n=273), and emit no line-0 semantic token. It dominates config-injection (no user-config write, covers the OS-temp fallback a settings glob misses) and disposal-timing (full, not partial; zero concurrency blast radius). Two process points made the difference: (1) the spike was run on the REAL extension lifecycle, not a hand-rolled stand-in — the hand-rolled retype-to-plaintext probe *cleared* the leak (it is Posit's own clearing mechanism) and did NOT reproduce the natural steady state; (2) the standing adversarial review was run on the PLAN, not just code — it caught that the first-draft recommendation (a partial, high-blast-radius `deleteQuietly`→`WorkspaceEdit` disposal-timing change) was inverted, and surfaced the in-content class. | Session 92 (the diagnosticMode:workspace leak PLAN; relocation refuted firsthand, candidate `# type: ignore` confirmed, first-draft `D` recommendation refuted by a 13-agent plan review) | Whenever a hard integration/lifecycle/LSP bug arrives with a filed or "obvious" fix direction: **measure the obvious fix firsthand against the real dependency before a plan commits to it**, using the real code lifecycle (not a stand-in that may accidentally trigger the fix). Enumerate mechanism CLASSES, not just options within one class — for a "generated files pollute a tool" problem the classes are: change the tool's config, change where the files live, change the files' lifecycle/open-state, and **change the files' content**. Run the standing adversarial review on a PLAN's recommendation, not only on code — a planning error propagates into every executor session. |
| 103 | **A plan is verified evidence for the mechanism CLASS, but its exact gate predicate and its completeness claim are only as strong as the inputs its spike could falsify — re-derive both at implementation.** Session 92's plan for the `diagnosticMode:"workspace"` vdoc leak was thoroughly grounded (relocation refuted firsthand; `# type: ignore` confirmed to suppress), yet two specifics were subtly off, and the contract re-verification + the runtime RED→GREEN each caught one. (1) **The gate.** The plan said inject the mute when `keep.size > 0`; re-reading the builder's OWN invariant showed the correct predicate is *non-whitespace body content*, because `keep` adds blank body lines too — so `keep.size > 0` injects into an all-blank-body python cell and breaks the pinned `embeddedLanguagesIn(text) ⟺ buildVirtualContent(text,L).trim() !== ""` equivalence (the existing invariant test even goes RED against a `keep.size>0` gate). The tell was in the plan itself: §4.3 flagged "the invariant test must be updated" — a load-bearing predicate the prose under-specified. (2) **The scope.** The plan's spike measured `[]` and read as "full suppression", but its fixture contained no SYNTAX errors; the implementation's real-Pylance RED→GREEN showed `# type: ignore` mutes Pyright's *type/name/import* diagnostics but NOT *parse/syntax* errors (a mid-typing `os.` still leaks on its vdoc) — a residual the plan's inputs could not have surfaced (this is Learning #98 applied to a PLAN's completeness claim: a claim exercised only on inputs that cannot violate it is restated, not verified). Neither finding refutes the plan's mechanism (in-content suppression is right); both refine its boundary, which is exactly implementation's job. | Session 93 (candidate G implementation; the gate refinement caught at Orient's contract re-verification, the scope residual caught by the workspace-mode `test:lsp` gate) | When executing a plan: (a) re-derive every load-bearing gate/predicate from the TARGET code's own invariants at Orient — not from the plan's prose — especially any predicate the plan flags with "update the invariant test"; (b) treat a suppression/transform's COMPLETENESS as unproven until a runtime gate exercises the input classes the plan's spike omitted (here: syntax errors, which the plan's fixture never contained). The plan proves the class; the implementation proves the boundary. |
| 104 | **Break-revert on an UNCOMMITTED change: back up the file (`cp`), never `git checkout` — the latter discards the very work you are testing.** Mid-implementation, to prove a gate was load-bearing, I reverted just the gate with a throwaway `perl` edit and then ran `git checkout <file>` to "restore" — which discarded my ENTIRE uncommitted L1 implementation (the injection + its constant), not merely the temporary break, because `git checkout -- <path>` resets the file to its **committed** version and an uncommitted change has no committed version to return to. Had to re-apply the whole change from context. The safe patterns: `cp file /tmp/backup` before the break and `cp /tmp/backup file` to restore (used for every subsequent break-revert this session, cleanly); OR checkpoint-commit the base change FIRST, after which `git checkout HEAD -- <file>` correctly restores the committed fix. | Session 93 (lost the uncommitted injection to a `git checkout` during a break-revert; re-applied, then switched to file backups) | Any mutation/break-revert test run to prove a line is load-bearing while the base change is not yet committed. `git checkout -- <path>` on a file with uncommitted changes is destructive — back up with a file copy, or checkpoint-commit the base first. |
| 105 | **A filed "cheap fix: gate on `vscode.languages.getLanguages()`" can rest on a false premise, because VS Code core registers many languageIds via built-in *language-basics* extensions (grammar + config, NO server).** The S89-filed fix for passive `{r}`/`{julia}` semantic-token vdoc minting was "gate `embeddedLanguagesIn`'s targets on the languageId being registered via `needsLanguageExtension`/`getLanguages()`." It cannot work: VS Code ships built-in language-basics for `r`, `julia`, `python`, AND `javascript` (`<VSCode.app>/Contents/Resources/app/extensions/{r,julia,python,javascript}`, v1.128.0), so `getLanguages()` returns all four in EVERY standard VS Code — extension or not — and `needsLanguageExtension` returns `false` for the whole mapped set. A RED integration test whose PREMISE was asserted (`assert(!getLanguages().includes("r"))`) fired on the first run and disproved the fix before any production code was written; inspecting the VS Code app bundle's built-in `extensions/` dir confirmed the root cause. Corollary: `getLanguages()` distinguishes "languageId known to VS Code" from "unknown", NOT "a language SERVER is installed" from "only the built-in grammar" — and there is no public API to detect whether a *provider* (completion/hover/semantic-tokens) exists for a language without first handing VS Code a real document of that language (a `provideDocumentSemanticTokensLegend` that returns `undefined` is NOT a reliable "no provider" signal — S89 measured the built-in JS service returning an undefined legend *transiently while it has a provider*). Latent sibling: `providers/embedded.ts`'s own `needsLanguageExtension`-gated "install an extension" hint therefore never fires for r/julia either. | Session 94 (investigating the S89 passive-minting MEDIUM; the filed `getLanguages` fix refuted by a RED premise assertion + app-bundle inspection; item downgraded MEDIUM→LOW, not implemented — the correct fix is an unavoidable-first-mint lifecycle change) | Before implementing any fix that treats `getLanguages()`/`needsLanguageExtension` as "a server for this language exists" — verify first (a RED test with an *asserted* premise, or `ls <VSCode.app>/…/app/extensions`) whether VS Code bundles built-in basics for that languageId; if it does, `getLanguages()` is vacuous as a server-presence gate. Sibling of Learning #101: a filed one-liner fix's PREMISE is itself a hypothesis — assert it in the RED test so a wrong premise fails loudly before you build on it. |
| 106 | **A behavior-preserving optimization has no new OUTPUT to red-first, so TDD it against the CONTRACT it must preserve — and prove the perf property itself firsthand.** Memoizing the shared `scanRegions` (a semantic-token pass re-scanned 2+2N times, N=embedded langs; BACKLOG `:118`) changes no output, so a naive "write the failing output test first" doesn't apply. Three artifacts stood in for it: (a) **contract tests** that pin what the memo must NOT break — aliasing-independence (mutate a returned array, assert a later call is unaffected) and full-text keying (prime doc A, query a same-`.length` prose doc B, assert no collision) — each **break-revert-proven to discriminate** (a no-`.slice()` memo turns the aliasing tests red and even cross-pollinates a sibling test via the shared cache; a length-keyed memo turns the keying test red); (b) **firsthand instrumentation of the perf property** — a temporary `globalThis` scan counter + a `QMD_NOMEMO` bypass showed the exact 2+2N→1 collapse (10→1 scans on a 4-language doc), added then REVERTED before commit (Learning #104's file-backup discipline, sibling of S94's MOCHA_GREP); (c) the full existing suite as the correctness net (825 green). **A single-entry shared memo introduces an ALIASING hazard fresh-per-call computation never had — and `.slice()` isolates only the array SPINE, not its elements.** The standing review (10 agents) confirmed exactly one real LOW: my docstring over-claimed "a caller can never corrupt a later call's view" (false for element mutation — `cell.code=` would leak cross-call), and one contract test was a tautology (`findX(t) toEqual findX(t)` holds for any deterministic fn). Fixes: state the true contract (spine copied & freely reorderable; elements shared & immutable — the contract every consumer already honored, even typed `runCells(cells: readonly Cell[])`), and strengthen the tautology to `.not.toBe` identity. **Separately, the wide-blast-radius due-diligence integration run discovered the suite had been silently RED for two sessions from NO source change**: `@vscode/test-electron` floats to the latest VS Code (no version pin), so an auto-update 1.128→1.129 changed the built-in tokenizer's semantic tokens, and S93's "reasoned unaffected → skipped the integration run" + S94 being docs-only meant nobody caught it. The javascript-only failing test (S93's `# type: ignore` is python-only) proved it environmental, not source. **[CORRECTED by Session 96 / Learning #107 — this causal story is WRONG. The failures are version-INDEPENDENT (they reproduce identically, 311/4 with the same 4× `@0:0`, on BOTH 1.128.1 and 1.129.0), so it is not tokenizer drift; and the cited "javascript-only" test actually contains a `{python}` cell (`pyCalls===1`). True cause: S93's `# type: ignore` on a python vdoc's line 0 meets a stand-in that emits a token per non-blank line. The "byte-identical on clean HEAD" observation stands; the 1.129 attribution does not. See #107.]** | Session 95 (memoise `scanRegions`; TDD'd via preserved-contract tests + firsthand scan-count instrumentation; standing 10-agent review found 1 real LOW — docstring over-claim + a tautological test — both fixed; discovered 4 pre-existing integration failures from an unpinned VS Code test version) | (a) When the deliverable is a behavior-preserving optimization: TDD the CONTRACT it must preserve (break-revert-proven to discriminate) and prove the perf property firsthand with throwaway instrumentation, rather than manufacturing a contrived output test. (b) When you introduce shared/cached state behind a pure API, state the immutability contract HONESTLY — `.slice()` copies the spine, not the elements; don't claim isolation you don't provide. (c) Run the integration suite before trusting green even when you "reason" your change is unaffected; and pin the external test toolchain version (`@vscode/test-electron`) — a floating dependency turns "I changed nothing" into a false premise. |
| 107 | **An inherited root-cause diagnosis is a hypothesis until you reproduce it against a controlled variable — and a "discriminator" cited in a handoff can itself rest on a misread. Re-derive it from code + a version-pinned experiment before fixing, especially when the FILED FIX doesn't touch the mechanism it names.** S95 filed the red integration suite (`BACKLOG:119`) as "VS Code 1.128→1.129 built-in-tokenizer drift" and proposed pin-and/or-update-expectations. Both load-bearing premises were false: (a) the cited discriminator — "test 4 is javascript-only, so S93's python-only `# type: ignore` can't explain its `@0:0` token" — was a misread; test 4's fixture is an `{ojs}`+`{js}`+`{python}` mix (`pyCalls===1`), so it DOES build a muted python vdoc; (b) the failures are version-INDEPENDENT — the unfixed suite fails identically (311/4, same 4× `@0:0`) on 1.128.1 (the very version the expectations were written against) AND 1.129.0, which no "1.129 emits a new token" theory survives. True cause, re-derived from code: since S93 `buildVirtualContent` injects `# type: ignore` on a python vdoc's **line 0**, and the Slice-2 stand-in `tokensForNonBlankLines` emits one token per **non-blank** line, so it spuriously tokenizes the mute comment; with NO coordinate remap (vdoc line N is .qmd line N — `providers/semantic-tokens.ts:234`) it lands at `.qmd` line 0. Production was always correct — real Pylance emits no token for the comment, pinned in `real-lsp.test.ts:469`. The fix was to make the stand-in FAITHFUL (skip the mute line), not the filed pin/update (pinning was still done, as independent hygiene, but it greens nothing). **Tells the diagnosis was off, visible before any code:** the filed fix (pin/update expectations) addressed neither the injected comment nor the stand-in that tokenizes it — when the proposed remedy doesn't name the mechanism, distrust the mechanism; and the "discriminator" was a fixture the filer never re-read. Sibling of #101/#105 (a filed one-liner's PREMISE is itself a hypothesis), applied to an inherited *diagnosis* rather than an inherited *fix*. | Session 96 (fixed the 4 RED "multi-language merge (Slice 2)" integration failures; refuted the S95 filed diagnosis by reproducing the failure identically on the pre-drift VS Code 1.128.1; corrected `BACKLOG:119`, Learning #106(c), and the S95 handoff's causal claim) | When inheriting any filed root-cause diagnosis (handoff, BACKLOG, prior learning): before implementing its fix, (1) re-read the actual fixtures/code the diagnosis rests on — a cited discriminator can be a misread; (2) reproduce the failure against a CONTROLLED variable (here: run the same unfixed suite on the OLD toolchain version) to falsify or confirm the causal story; (3) treat a filed fix that doesn't touch the named mechanism as a signal the diagnosis is wrong, not a task to execute. |
| 108 | **For a semantic-token FIDELITY fix, "carry the foreign name" is only correct when the token is currently the WRONG colour vs a real `.py` — a token that already MATCHES the `.py` needs no fix, and carrying a name the serving extension itself declines to style moves you AWAY from `.py`. Resolve every colour through the EFFECTIVE theme trie firsthand, last-wins.** BACKLOG:127 (the modifier axis) named two instances; firsthand grounding (a real-Pylance probe + theme resolution) split them in OPPOSITE directions. **`typeHintComment` was GENUINE:** Pylance emits `class.typeHintComment` inside a legacy `# type:` comment (verified on the wire), and clearing the modifier repainted a comment interior as a live type (`entity.name.type` #4EC9B0 teal vs the `.py`'s #8b949e comment colour) — a real wrong colour, fixed by carrying the modifier + mirroring Pylance's own `*.typeHintComment` → `comment.typehint.type.notation.python` rule `quarto`-scoped (same shape as the `module` type-axis fix). **`builtin` (the item's HEADLINE) was REFUTED:** Pylance ships no `function.builtin`/`variable.builtin` scope rule, so a real `.py` shows `print`/`__name__` in the plain `function`/`variable` colour (#d2a8ff / #ffa657) — EXACTLY what our bare-modifier `.qmd` already shows; carrying `builtin` + a scope rule would repaint them #DCDCAA/#79c0ff, *diverging* from the `.py` even in the DEFAULT theme (the S90 `selfParameter` trap on the modifier axis). **Second, load-bearing sub-lesson (adversarial review caught it):** VS Code applies same-specificity TextMate rules **last-wins**; a resolver that takes first-wins returns the BASE theme's hexes, not the effective override — my first-pass probe cited #DCDCAA/#9CDCFE/#6A9955 (base) instead of #d2a8ff/#ffa657/#8b949e (effective 2026-dark), a confidently-wrong fact that reached the docstrings before the review flagged it (Learning #7/#107). The colour-fidelity target is theme-INDEPENDENT at the SCOPE level (map to the exact scope the `.py` resolves through and you match in every theme), but any cited HEX must be resolved through the effective trie with correct precedence. | Session 97 (fixed `typeHintComment`, refuted `builtin`; standing multi-agent adversarial review — correctness lenses confirmed the decision, a quality lens caught the hex-citation error + a tautological refutation assertion, both corrected) | On any semantic-token fidelity item: (1) the target is "match a real `.py`" — resolve BOTH the current and the proposed scope for the token and only fix if they differ AND the current one is wrong; a name the serving extension declines to style itself (no `semanticTokenScopes` entry) is one a real `.py` shows bare, so carrying it diverges; (2) resolve every hex through the effective theme's include chain with last-wins precedence, never a single file or first-wins; (3) prove emission on the wire for BOTH carried and refuted names (a refutation assertion on your OWN re-encoded output is tautological — decode the CONTROL against the server's legend). |
| 109 | **Grounding a platform defect on the path your TEST takes is not grounding it on the path your CODE takes — enumerate which BRANCH each real input traverses before concluding coverage.** Session 99 characterized `BACKLOG:125` (semantic-token legend/stream provider divergence) about as hard as a claim can be characterized: 9 agents over the shipped 1.129.0 bundle, verbatim minified bytes for every claim, an *executed* reconstruction of VS Code's own `orderedGroups`/`rdn`/`mki`/`CU`, and finally a genuine RED in a real Extension Development Host (`function.declaration@1:0` where the answering provider meant `variable.readonly@1:0`). Every one of those agreed, and the conclusion drawn from them — "if VS Code fixed the document path, our two-command fetch would be sound as written" — was still **FALSE**. `javascript` has **no document semantic-tokens provider at all** in stock VS Code: the built-in TS extension registers ONLY `registerDocumentRangeSemanticTokensProvider` (measured: 0 doc registrations vs 1 range), so `hki(i,t)` is false for every `.js` vdoc (`lang-map.ts` maps ojs/js → javascript/.js) and **both commands already fall through to the RANGE registry** — a different code path with its own divergence, and a **BROADER** one: `ZNt` swallows per-provider errors (`catch(c){Fs(c),a=null}`), so a top range provider that THROWS diverges too, where the doc path's `mki` does `if(a.error)throw a.error` and degrades safely through our try/catch. The grounding was exhaustive on the axis under test and blind to the axis half our languages actually use — **because every grounding prompt named the two DOCUMENT commands**, so all 9 agents read that path perfectly and not one asked "which path does an `{ojs}` cell take?". The exhaustiveness *manufactured* the false closure: the item was one edit from being closed `[x]` over a live, uncovered path. Caught only by the standing review's SCOPE/residual-coverage lens — the one lens pointed at what the work did NOT cover rather than at whether what it covered was right. Sibling of #10 (a diff-scoped review misses what the change made stale elsewhere) and #99/#107/#108 (an inherited framing is a hypothesis), but the new failure mode is **self-inflicted**: the wrong premise was mine, freshly minted from correct evidence. | Session 99 (grounded + PINNED the document axis of `BACKLOG:125` in a real EDH, refuted both proposed fix directions; the standing 6-lens adversarial review's scope lens refuted the session's own "sound as written" conclusion and its plan to close the item — item kept OPEN with the range axis recorded as the remaining work) | Whenever you characterize an external system's defect and are about to conclude COVERAGE (close an item, declare an axis done): enumerate your REAL inputs (here: the 4 embedded languages) and trace which BRANCH of the external system each one actually takes — provider present? fallback? none? A fallback branch is a different code path with a different bug, and a fan-out whose prompts all name one path will confirm that path beautifully and never look at the other. Pair every "is what I found correct?" lens with a "what did I NOT look at?" lens; only the latter can catch this. |

| 110 | **The KIND of provider a test double registers is a branch-selecting axis — a stand-in of the wrong kind silently relocates the code under test onto a path production never takes, and the suite stays green while the real branch is never exercised.** Learning #94 found this on the *scheme* axis (every stand-in registered on our custom URI scheme — the exact axis real servers discriminate by — so a 100%-green suite hid a totally dead feature). This is the same failure on the **provider-kind** axis, and it hid the opposite thing: not a dead feature, but an untested branch. The Slice-2 javascript stand-in registers `registerDocumentSemanticTokensProvider`; stock VS Code has **no** javascript document provider (the built-in TS extension registers ONLY `registerDocumentRangeSemanticTokensProvider` — 0 doc / 1 range), so `hki(i,t)` (`s.has(o)`) is TRUE in the test and FALSE in production. Every `{ojs}` test therefore ran the DOCUMENT path while every real `{ojs}` cell runs the RANGE path — a different registry, a different picker, and a divergence bug the doc path does not have (`ZNt` swallows per-provider errors and its `uki` record has no error field, where the doc path's `dki` carries one and `mki` rethrows: a crashing server is a safe no-op on one path and a WRONG COLOUR on the other). The stand-in was not *wrong* — a JS extension registering a document provider is legitimate — it was merely of a kind the default install does not have, and nothing in the suite could notice. **The generalisation:** when a platform offers >1 way to serve the same request (document vs range; push vs pull; sync vs async), the double's KIND is a hidden fork in the code under test. Enumerate what the REAL dependency registers before choosing the double's kind — and if the real one is absent (as here), the absence is load-bearing and must be preserved in the fixture, which is why the range pins need their own `describe` with NO document provider. **The second half is the trap I fell into WHILE writing the first:** during review I adopted a proposed "two-read race" — our fetch issues two independent `executeCommand`s and each re-reads the registry (`_updateScores` per invocation, no shared snapshot), so a registration between them would diverge legend/stream with no decline, no throw and no tie group — and I wrote it into six files as *measured*. It is FALSE, and the only evidence I had was our own S89 retry note ("the LEGEND command returns `undefined` while the TOKEN command already answers") reasoned BACKWARDS: legend undefined => the two reads saw different registries. That note is **prose, not a measurement artifact** — an inherited diagnosis (#107) that I never re-measured, cited to support a claim about a path whose mechanics point the OTHER way (one range provider registered but not ready => legend DEFINED via `if(r.length===1)`, which never calls the provider, and tokens UNDEFINED via `ZNt`'s `tokens:null` — the mirror of what the note says). A positive-controlled probe settled it in minutes: the sequential recipe `await legendCmd(); registerB(); await tokensCmd()` DOES diverge (so the probe is not blind), while our real `Promise.all` shape with B registered at every earliest moment (same tick / microtask / `setTimeout(0)` / `setImmediate`) gives `bCalls=0` every time — the ext-host send chain is synchronous, so both messages queue in ONE tick on a FIFO channel and each handler finishes its registry read before its first await. **The generalisable rule: "there is no shared snapshot" is not "there is an observable interleaving." A race needs a WINDOW, and a window needs an await — find the await before you believe the race.** And a prose sentence in your own source is an inherited diagnosis, not evidence: if a claim is empirically checkable in minutes with a positive control, checking beats arguing from bytes, and beats citing your own changelog. | Session 100 (PINNED the RANGE axis of `BACKLOG:125`, both legs, in a real EDH, and CLOSED the item on a structural coverage argument; the standing review proposed a two-read race, the session adopted it into the record, and a positive-controlled probe then REFUTED it — the session's own claim, killed by its own experiment) | Choosing a test double for a platform request: first ask what the REAL dependency registers (KIND, not just selector) — `grep` the shipped extension. If production has no double of that kind, that absence selects the branch and must be reproduced in the fixture. And before recording any race/interleaving claim: name the AWAIT that opens the window, then prove it with a probe carrying a POSITIVE CONTROL — and never promote a prose sentence in your own repo to "measured". |

| 111 | **A filed fix is a hypothesis, not a design — run it before you believe it, and re-derive the mechanism from the event loop rather than from the item's own vocabulary.** `BACKLOG:102` filed both a mechanism ("when an untitled-doc forward **wins** the deactivate **race**") and a fix ("move the resets OUT of the `if`, and have `disposeAllVdocs` observe `fallbackDirPromise` … so a raced deactivate still `rmdir`s"). Implemented verbatim, the fix **did not work**, and the word "race" was wrong twice over. (1) **It is deterministic.** `disposeAllVdocs` reaches its fallback check after `await Promise.all(all.map(deleteQuietly))`; when nothing is registered — exactly the untitled-first-forward case — that is a *microtask*, and microtasks fully drain before the loop's poll phase can deliver `mkdtemp`'s libuv-threadpool completion. The check therefore *always* sees the unset value: not "sometimes loses a race" but "leaks every time" (2/2 unstubbed runs). Reading "race" as "occasional" also mis-sizes severity in the *safe* direction, which is the kind of error that survives review. (2) **The filed fix was insufficient, and only running it revealed why:** the directory still survived, EMPTY (`survived=true contents=[]`) — the in-flight forward writes its file before the non-recursive `rmdir` lands, `rmdir` fails ENOTEMPTY, and the forward's own epoch guard then deletes the file, leaving precisely the empty directory the fix existed to reclaim. The real fix needed a third change the item never contemplated (bail *before* writing a file you are about to delete). Sibling of #107/#108/#109/#110 — but where those say "do not inherit a predecessor's *diagnosis*", this says **do not inherit a predecessor's *remedy*** either: a fix that has never been executed is a guess with a confident voice. Corollary, learned the same session: when the observation contradicts your model, say so in the comment. The first draft of that guard explained itself with an unverified promise-resume-order theory ("this forward attached first, so it always resumes first"); a probe contradicted it, so the shipped comment states what was *measured* (cleanup is unreliable without the guard, reliable with it) and explicitly marks the interleaving as uncharacterized. **When to apply:** any session implementing a fix a previous session *filed*. Implement it, run it, and treat both its mechanism and its remedy as claims under test — and if it passes for a reason you cannot name, say that in the code rather than inventing one. |
| 112 | **Delegating a break-revert experiment mutates the tree you are reviewing — never hand a sub-agent the working copy you intend to ship.** A 6-lens adversarial review was told to prove test discrimination by reverting each source change and re-running. One agent deleted the load-bearing pre-write guard and never restored it; the parent noticed only because `git diff --stat` shrank from 70 to 59 lines mid-review. Two compounding harms, and the second is worse than the lost code: a *different* agent then reviewed the corrupted tree and confidently reported "REFUTED — the finding reviews code that does not exist in the diff … no hunk touches `ensureVdoc`", which was true only because a sibling agent had deleted that hunk seconds earlier. A concurrent fleet does not share a mental model of "the diff", so one agent's experiment silently becomes another's ground truth, and a **false refutation is indistinguishable from a real one** in the report. Also observed: an agent's probe created `<tmp>/quarto-mit-no-such-dir-121b`, which made a new test's "guaranteed nonexistent" path exist and flipped its precondition — fixed by making the path a per-run UUID. **When to apply:** any review or verification fan-out over a dirty working tree. Give agents a read-only remit, or `isolation: "worktree"`, or a copy — and if an experiment must mutate, keep it in the parent. Verify tree integrity by checksum *before* triaging any finding: findings computed against a corrupted tree must be discarded, not reasoned about. |
| 113 | **A claim inherited from your OWN subagent is still an inherited claim — ground your remedy, not just your premises (the #111 trap, one level down).** Learnings #107–#110 say do not inherit a *predecessor's* diagnosis; #111 extends it to a filed *fix*. This is the next level down: S102 grounded `BACKLOG:182`'s premises firsthand and killed the item's headline evidence — then wrote a **dragon of its own** ("drop the trailing `-` and `mkdtemp`'s suffix fuses onto the PID digits, so the name parses as PID 548278") and labelled it **Measured**. It is false. A grounding agent's *prose gloss* — true of the bare `parseInt`-style parser in its own throwaway probe — was transcribed into a claim about the plan's **anchored regex**, which the probe never ran. One command refuted it: `/^quarto-mit-vdoc-([0-9]+)-[A-Za-z0-9]{6}$/.exec("quarto-mit-vdoc-548278u7zTF")` → **null**. The real failure mode was the *opposite class*: a silent, total, safe-direction leak (the sweep reclaims nothing, ever), not a wrong-PID delete. Three independent review lenses caught it; the author did not. **The asymmetry that makes this a trap:** you feel *maximally* grounded right after a firsthand grounding pass — which is exactly when a plausible sentence from your own fleet's prose slides in unexamined, because it arrived wearing the same evidence-shaped clothes as the measurements around it. A subagent's *measured output* and its *narrative summary of that output* are different artifacts with different reliability, and the summary is where the errors live. **When to apply:** whenever a claim reaches your artifact from a subagent rather than from your own hands — a probe result, a review finding, a "measured" figure in a fleet report. Ask which parser/regex/binary the number was actually produced against, and whether that is the one you are shipping. If a claim carries a label like "Measured", the label is a promise you must be able to re-execute *in the artifact's own terms* — if you cannot re-run it in one command, it is not measured, it is repeated. Corollary, learned the same session: adversarial review is worth running against your **own** draft, not just your predecessor's — and its *refuted* findings can still be the most valuable thing in it (S102's PID-namespace finding was refuted on its "this is routine" premise, yet the guard was adopted anyway once the release status showed the grammar locks at first publish — the refutation improved the reason, not the decision). |
| 114 | **State a safety invariant in the direction it actually fails, or the sentence that licenses the design becomes the design's biggest lie.** `hostDiscriminator`'s docstring justified a 24-bit host tag with *"a collision costs only a missed reclaim (a leak — the safe direction), never a wrong delete, because the PID liveness check still has to agree."* Every clause is backwards. A **collision** makes `parsed.host === ours`, so G0 **fails open** and G2 then evaluates a foreign-namespace PID → `ESRCH` → **deletes a live window's unsaved source**; the liveness check is not a backstop against a collision, it is the *mechanism* of the damage. The **leak** I described comes from a hostname *change* (DHCP/rename → tags differ → skip) — a different event entirely, which I conflated with the collision. Measured after the fact: `hostDiscriminator("node7582.cluster.local") === hostDiscriminator("node8137.cluster.local") === "477c36"`; ~3% collision odds at 1000 hosts. **The code was correct throughout** — this was purely the *comment*, which is exactly what makes it dangerous: it priced the residual risk at zero in the one artifact a future session would read to decide whether 24 bits is enough. **When to apply:** for any guard, write down both failure directions explicitly (what makes it fail OPEN vs fail CLOSED) and name which event causes which, before claiming a cost. A "never" in a safety comment is a claim that only needs one counterexample to be false — and four independent review lenses found this one. Note the shape: I wrote this in the same commit that cited Learning #113 ("a claim from your own subagent is still inherited"), while inheriting an unverified sentence from my own reasoning. |
| 115 | **`grep -rn` silently skips any file containing a NUL byte — so a grep-based inventory can be sound in method and blind in fact.** `core/embedded/vdoc-path.ts` uses a literal NUL as `vdocKeyString`'s field separator (`.join("\0")`), which makes git render it `Bin 5465 -> 9023 bytes` (0 insertions/0 deletions — the diff is unreviewable) and makes `grep -rn TEMP_DIR_RE src/` return **nothing** while `grep -ran` finds it. It is the only such file in the repo, and it owns `TEMP_DIR_RE`, `isOurVdocFileName` and `VDOC_DIR_SEGMENTS` — **the ownership gates on both delete loops**. My Phase 0 re-verification of the plan's §7 grep inventory was therefore unsound; its conclusions happened to be right only because the symbols genuinely did not exist yet. **When to apply:** this project mandates a grep-based evidence inventory for every deletion/migration-shaped plan (`SESSION_RUNNER.md`), and that method has a silent blind spot. Before trusting any `grep -rn` sweep over `src/`, run `git ls-files | xargs grep -lP '\x00'` once to learn which files the sweep cannot see, and use `-a` for those. A zero-hit grep is only evidence if the file was actually read. Related: the same class of vacuous success bit me twice more this session — `grep -an PATTERN src/` (no `-r`) exits 0 having searched **nothing**, which reads exactly like "the false claim is gone", and a break-revert that trips `TS6133` fails the *build* so no test can redden (Learning #38d, met from the other side: "NOTHING FAILED" meant the probe was broken, not the guard undiscriminated). |
| 116 | **A test whose discriminating power depends on an unasserted precondition degrades into a tautology the moment that precondition changes — assert the premise, and skip loudly rather than pass vacuously.** The 🐉1 test (`isProcessDead(1) === false`) is the suite's *only* discriminator of the `catch { dead }` inversion, and it works solely because `kill(1,0)` throws `EPERM` for a non-root user. Measured: **as root it does not throw**, so the shipped and the naive implementations both return `false` and the assertion passes while proving nothing (and Windows has no PID 1 at all).
