The fix is not a better fixture but an honest one: probe the premise, assert the discriminating case only when it holds, and `skip()` otherwise — because "we could not run the discriminator" and "the discriminator passed" must never look alike in a test report. **When to apply:** whenever a test's value rests on an environmental fact (a uid, a platform, a signal's permission semantics, a suite ordering), assert that fact in the test. This is Learning #109's shape one level up: #109 was a fixture that never reached the guard; this is a fixture that reaches it but stops discriminating. Corollary found the same session: my "old PID-less grammar" decoy was a shape **no version ever wrote** — I copied it from the plan's §4.2 example list instead of from the code, so the label was false and the real legacy shape (`quarto-mit-vdoc-BLjvTa`, verified against directories still on disk) was pinned by nothing. |
| 117 | **When the fix RE-MINTS a resource under a fresh identity, the test must observe the resource the fixed code produced — not the one it replaced, which the fix has made vanish.** Phase 2's defect: `vdocDirFor` returns a memoised `mkdtemp` dir even after the OS/user deleted it, and the next `vscode.workspace.fs.writeFile` silently re-creates that path at `0777 & ~umask` (0755) instead of a private 0700 dir. The fix `stat`s the memo and re-mints a **fresh** `mkdtemp` on `ENOENT` — so the directory the second forward uses has a *different path* than the first. The obvious test ("capture the dir, delete it, forward again, stat the captured dir") is a trap the plan's own prose walked toward: under the fix that captured path stays deleted, so the `stat` throws `ENOENT` and the test errors rather than observing the 0700 it exists to prove; under the bug it observes 0755. It discriminates, but its *pass* leg proves nothing. The honest test asserts on `path.dirname(secondForward.fsPath)` — the dir the user's source **actually landed in** — which is 0755 under the bug (re-created in place) and 0700 under the fix (fresh mint). Same shape as this project's other observation traps (#109 fixture never reaches the guard; #116 fixture reaches it but stops discriminating): here the fixture observes the *wrong object*. **When to apply:** any fix that repairs a fault by producing a new instance (re-mint, re-open, re-allocate, reconnect) rather than mutating the faulted one — assert on the identity the FIXED path yields, obtained from its own return value, never a handle captured before the fix ran. Corollaries this session: (a) the plan's specified `stat`-then-re-mint is a check-then-act on module state across two awaits, so the re-mint needs the **identity-CAS** (`if (fallbackDirPromise === memo)`) the existing `.catch` already models, or two concurrent forwards each mint and orphan the loser's dir (the BL-102 leak class — a dragon the plan did not name); (b) Phase 1 had already **falsified this item's filed cause** — the self-skip + ESRCH-only liveness guard mean our own sweep can no longer delete a live dir, so the trigger is OS/user deletion, not "a reclaim racing a live forward" — a reminder that in an incrementally-shipped plan each phase can invalidate the *next* phase's stated rationale without touching the defect, so re-ground the cause per phase, not once per plan. |
| 118 | **To de-binary-fy a text file that holds a meaningful control-char string literal, replace the raw byte with its ESCAPE SEQUENCE — byte-identical at runtime, plain text in source — not a new "printable" character.** `vdocKeyString`'s field separator was a raw NUL byte (`0x00`), both in the `.join("<NUL>")` and quoted inline in the docstring above it; that made git render the whole file `Bin … 0 insertions` and `grep -rn` silently skip it, so an inventory over `src/` was blind to the delete-loop ownership grammar the file owns (the exact hazard **#115** warns about — BACKLOG:184). The fix writes the separator as `"\0"` (backslash-zero) in source: the emitted byte is unchanged (the key is an in-memory `Map` key only — one call site, never persisted — so the change is provably inert), but the source is now printable and greppable. **Reject the item's own prescription to "change the separator to a printable character":** that re-prices the collision risk instead of removing it — you must then prove the new char can never occur in any field value (URI/languageId/ext), a strictly harder proof than "the byte is identical." Two tests carry it: (a) a repo-hygiene test scanning `src/**/*.ts` for any NUL byte — this is the genuine RED→GREEN (RED with the raw NUL present, GREEN after) *and* a durable regression guard, because the defect is a property of the source bytes, not of any behaviour; (b) an exact-equality pin on `vdocKeyString` output that also asserts `key.includes("\\0") === false`, which catches the classic `"\0"`→`"\\0"` typo (a real behaviour change — a 2-char separator) that the distinctness/forge tests would pass right over. The pin is GREEN-on-add (a refactor guard, not a RED→GREEN); prove it load-bearing by break-reverting the separator, exactly as this project break-revert-proves any guard. **When to apply:** any file git/grep treats as binary because a source literal embeds a raw NUL or other control byte; escape-in-source is the inert route and an exact-byte pin is what keeps a later "cleanup" from silently un-escaping it. |
| 119 | **When the deliverable is a comment that CORRECTS a false comment, the replacement is the single highest-risk place to introduce a NEW false-or-overclaiming comment — so adversarially refute your OWN replacement prose against the MEASUREMENT, not against plausibility.** This project's signature failure is a false comment shipped by the very author citing the learning against it (Learnings #114, #116). Session 106's whole deliverable (Phase 3 of the OS-temp sweep plan, BACKLOG:182/183) was fixing two such comments — the refuted *"self-healing: `ensureVdoc` notices the model is gone and re-mints"* → the measured *"harmless because the model outlives the file."* I ran a 3-lens adversarial refutation (mechanism / overclaim / code-consistency) on my *own* rewrite before commit; all three returned "not refuted," but two independently flagged that naming **"completions"** generalized past the measurement: plan §3.5 exercised only an **in-process** `executeHoverProvider`, whereas Python completions route through **out-of-process Pylance**, whose file-watcher was the plan's explicitly UNMEASURED caveat. The rewrite was "not false" but "beyond measured" — and the correction for an overclaim must not overclaim in the opposite direction. Tightened to *"requests are served from the open in-memory document"* (the measured mechanism — `didOpen` with no `didClose` until eviction), naming no unmeasured feature. **When to apply:** whenever a session's deliverable is correcting a comment or doc claim, treat the replacement with the same suspicion as the original — refute it against what was actually measured, and prefer the narrow measured mechanism over a plausible-sounding generalization. Two corollaries proven here: (a) when re-editing a comment whose line location is cited elsewhere (the plan's re-pinned `:551-553`), **preserve the line count** so the citation you just fixed does not re-rot; (b) re-pinning bare line-number citations only postpones the rot (this plan's numbers have now rotted three times) — anchor the reader to a greppable phrase ("re-grep the phrase, not the number"), not just a fresh number. |
| 120 | **A handoff's fix PRESCRIPTION is an inherited claim, not evidence — and when it contradicts the code's own documented rationale for a sibling mechanism, that contradiction is the tell that the prescription is wrong.** BACKLOG:103 was the after-dispatch deactivate race: a forward dispatched *entirely after* `disposeAllVdocs` bumps the once-per-session `disposeAllEpoch` snapshots the final value, slips both re-checks, and re-registers a vdoc holding the user's source into the cleared maps. The S105/S106 handoff asserted, in firm prose, *"the latched `deactivated` flag is REFUTED; needs a per-document latch."* That prescription was **unsourced** (nowhere in PROJECT_LEARNINGS, the plan, or any review — only in the handoff's own next-steps) **and self-contradictory**: `embedded-vdoc.ts`'s own `disposeAllEpoch` doc comment already explained the guard is GLOBAL *precisely because* an in-flight/after-dispatch forward may be "for a document that has not yet reached its `live.set` — so it is in no map for a per-owner sweep to enumerate." A per-document latch has that exact blind spot. Grounding firsthand (a RED integration test observing a real stranded `vdoc-mit.*.py`, then a 5-lens + completeness-critic adversarial review) confirmed the GLOBAL latch is correct and per-document is insufficient — the handoff was wrong. **The tell wasn't "unsourced" alone** (facts can be right without a citation); it was that the prescription contradicted the load-bearing rationale the code already documented for the sibling guard it was extending. **Three corollaries proven here.** (a) **A persistent module-global latch and the test-isolation a shared long-lived fixture needs are ONE deliverable, not two.** The item filed the "tests call the process-global `disposeAllVdocs()` mid-suite" coupling as a *benign quality nit*; adding a latch that the one long-lived Extension Development Host inherits turned it into a HARD prerequisite — without an `afterEach(resetDeactivation)` reset seam, 5 downstream tests cascaded RED (the latch poisoned every later `ensureVdoc`). That predicted-then-observed cascade is itself the proof the reset is load-bearing. (b) **Closing a backlog item does not just flip `[x]` — it must correct any now-false premise the filed text still carries** (the cross-reference-completeness discipline, `SESSION_RUNNER.md` framework Learnings #7/#8): the completeness critic caught that the item's "would apply symmetrically to `disposeVdocs`" was demonstrably false (the per-document-close sibling is unreachable — a closed doc gets no forward, and semantic tokens' retry timer is cleared by `provider.forget()` in the same close handler — and self-healing — a re-registration is reclaimed at session-end `disposeAllVdocs`). Left uncorrected, the "fixed" record would keep a false claim, this project's signature failure (Learnings #114/#116). Pinned the load-bearing "a closed document receives no forward" assumption in a `disposeVdocs` comment so a future no-`forget()` provider timer can't silently reopen it. (c) **Name what the fix's own wiring cannot test.** The production `activate()`→`resetDeactivation()` line is load-bearing for re-enable-without-reload yet drives no test (`ext.activate()` is idempotent on the shared host); deleting it fails zero tests. Pinned that coverage boundary in a code comment at the call site rather than pretending the green suite covers it. **When to apply:** whenever a handoff (or a backlog item) hands you not just facts but a *design prescription* — verify the prescription against the code's existing documented rationale before adopting it; if they conflict, re-derive from the code. And when a fix adds persistent global state, treat the test-isolation it forces and the now-stale filed premises it exposes as part of the same deliverable, not follow-ups. |
| 121 | **`npm audit`'s `fixAvailable` is the `--force` resolution (often a semver-major jump to *latest*, or even a DOWNGRADE) — not the minimal correct fix. Derive the real target from each advisory's own patched range, prefer the smallest bump + narrowest `overrides`, and gate on `npm audit` (seconds) before the full matrix (minutes).** BACKLOG:176 cleared 7 dev-only advisories (4 mod / 2 high / 1 crit) the right way. `npm audit`'s machine output pointed at `vitest@4.1.10` (latest, a 2→4 jump) and — for the mocha→serialize-javascript chain — `mocha@8.1.3`, a *downgrade to an older mocha with an older serialize-javascript*. Both are `npm audit fix --force` artifacts, not the minimal fix. The method that found the real targets: read each advisory's `via[].range` (the TRUE vulnerable range, e.g. `vite <=6.4.2`, `vitest <3.2.6`, `serialize-javascript` RCE `<=7.0.2` + DoS `<7.0.5`) and the transitive parent's *declared* dep range, then pick the smallest version / narrowest override that lands everything above those ranges. That gave **`vitest ^3.2.7`** (a 2→**3** jump, not 4 — its `vite: ^5||^6||^7` range lets npm resolve the patched `vite@7.3.6`, and it pins `vite-node@3.2.4`, both above the vulnerable ranges; 3.2.7 also clears the `<3.2.6` UI advisory) and **`esbuild ^0.28.1`**. **Two corollaries proven here.** (a) **When a vulnerable package is transitive and even the LATEST parent still pins the vulnerable range, an `overrides` pin is the only non-downgrade fix — and it is safe precisely when the code path is never invoked.** Even `mocha@11.7.6` still declares `serialize-javascript ^6.0.2`, so *no* mocha bump clears that advisory; `overrides: { "serialize-javascript": "^7.0.7" }` does, and also clears mocha's own advisory (whose only vuln IS that transitive dep). The pin crosses mocha's declared `^6.0.2`, but that is inert here — verified, not assumed: no `.mocharc*`, no `--parallel` in any script, so serialize-javascript (mocha's parallel-reporter-only dep) is never executed; the 333-test integration suite passing unchanged confirms it. So the mocha MAJOR bump the "hygiene" framing tempts you toward is pure added risk for zero audit benefit — keep it pinned. (b) **A dev-dependency audit fix is runtime-verified for free by an integration suite that loads the actual BUNDLE, so it is not FM #24 (build-passes-ship-it).** The two upgrades verify on different surfaces: the unit runner (`vitest run`, 834 tests, count-unchanged = no silent test-drop) exercises the *runner* bump; the integration suite (`@vscode/test-electron`, 333 tests in a real Extension Development Host that loads `dist/extension.js`) exercises the *bundler* bump's output at runtime. Separately re-verify the standing "dev-only never ships" invariant after the bump (empty runtime `dependencies`, no `src/` imports, whole-token grep of the bundle → 0, `vsce ls` → 0 `node_modules`) — a toolchain change is exactly when a tool name could leak into the shipped artifact. **When to apply:** any "clear the `npm audit` advisories" session — do not trust `fixAvailable`; compute minimal targets from per-advisory ranges, iterate install+audit cheaply until `total: 0`, then run the full matrix ONCE, and re-verify the ship-invariant. |
| 122 | **To split, merge, or reorder items in a backlog whose cross-references are LINE NUMBERS, preserve every citation by rewriting the cited item's line IN PLACE (as a same-line pointer) and APPENDING new items at the END — never inserting among cited lines — then re-verify each citation MECHANICALLY.** This project's `BACKLOG:NNN` scheme is line-number-based, with (as of S109) 17 distinct cited lines across `src/`, `test/`, and docs; decomposing one item must not shift any cited line or ~30 references silently rot. The transform proven on BACKLOG:64 item 17 (a 4-way bundle → items 17a–17d): (1) rewrite item 17's single physical line (line 64) IN PLACE as a same-line pointer that keeps its number and meaning (`BACKLOG:64` still resolves to item 17, now "SPLIT — see 17a–17d"); (2) APPEND 17a–17d at the END of the file, below the highest cited line (185), never inserted among cited lines. Verify by measurement, not by eye: `git diff --numstat` showed `5  1  BACKLOG.md` (a same-line rewrite adds AND deletes exactly one line; the other 4 are the appended items), and every distinct cited line — `git grep -hoE 'BACKLOG:[0-9]+' -- ':!BACKLOG.md' \| sort -u` — was `sed -n` spot-printed post-edit and still resolved to the SAME item it did before (64 → the item-17 pointer, 185 → the EPERM item, 120 → the pre-existing blank line, etc.). Two tooling facts proven here: (a) the dashboard's done-counter regex is `^\s*[-*]\s*\[x\]` (matches `-`/`*` bullets only), so a numbered `NN.` pointer line is invisible to it and will not misreport as done, while the appended items must be `- [ ]` (open, not counted) — this project also deliberately keeps many shipped `- [x]` sub-items in BACKLOG for history, so a non-zero "done-unmigrated" signal is expected, not a defect; (b) grep the ACTUAL item line, never a predecessor handoff's enumeration — S108's summary conflated a sibling item's sub-feature ("R-package workspace-symbol exclusion", item 11's sub-feature (c) at line 146) into item 17's bundle; grounding against line 64 caught it. **When to apply:** any grooming/triage session that splits, merges, or reorders items in a line-number-cited backlog (or edits any file whose cross-references are line-based) — rewrite-in-place + append-at-end, then mechanically re-verify each citation still resolves. This formalizes the long-standing "`BACKLOG:NNN` is a LINE NUMBER — rewrite in place, append at the END" gotcha into a named technique with a verification recipe. |
| 123 | **A backlog item that bundles several same-*named* commands can still span two different *mechanisms* — verify each member firsthand against the code and the comparison doc before treating the whole item as uniform "thin wiring," and split the outlier into its own item rather than force it.** BACKLOG:64 item 17c bundled three Posit "new X" discoverability commands under one "thin command/menu contribution over existing `newDocument`, **not** new core work" framing. Grounding against `docs/POSIT-COMPARISON.md` (Session 67) and the code showed only TWO fit that framing — `quarto.newPresentation` (untitled `.qmd`, `format: revealjs`) and `quarto.fileNewDocument` (the File▸New variant of `newDocument`, `format: html`) both reuse `quarto.newDocument`'s existing untitled-text-buffer path — while the third, `quarto.newNotebook`, creates an `.ipynb`: a DIFFERENT mechanism (notebook creation via `openNotebookDocument("jupyter-notebook", NotebookData)` + a raw front-matter cell), i.e. exactly the "new core work" the item said it wasn't. Surfaced the mismatch to the operator, who scoped the two `.qmd` presets to this session and split `newNotebook` to new item 17e. **Two techniques proven.** (a) **Parameterize a pure builder by a new argument whose DEFAULT equals the old hard-coded value** — `buildNewDocumentContent(title, format: NewDocumentFormat = "html")` — so every existing single-arg caller is byte-identical (no caller churn; 834 unit stay green) AND the new behavior gets a clean runtime RED: a two-arg unit call runs under vitest's type-stripping transpile (esbuild strips types, never type-checks) and fails with an AssertionError (`format: html` vs `revealjs`) — the genuine RED-for-the-right-reason, not the arity/`TS6133` type-error Learning #115 warns masquerades as RED. `check-types` is briefly red on the arity until GREEN; that is expected, and the failing *test* (not the type-checker) is the RED gate. (b) **When the LOGIC is TDD'd in `core/` but the adapter+manifest layer is declarative wiring (a `registerCommand` + a `package.json` contribution with no branch to unit-test), break-revert is the faithful-verification (gate d) for that layer** — disabling both new `registerCommand` calls reddened exactly the registration/behavior integration tests (`command 'quarto.newPresentation' not found`, 3 failing) while the manifest-reading `file/newFile` menu test correctly stayed GREEN (it asserts on `ext.packageJSON.contributes.menus`, not runtime), proving each test discriminates on the wiring it targets. **When to apply:** implementing any multi-command / multi-member backlog item — enumerate each member's actual mechanism (not its name) against the code and comparison doc before committing to "one thin slice"; if a member needs a different mechanism, split it. And when adding a preset over an existing pure builder, default-parameterize to keep callers inert and win a real RED. |
| 125 | **When a feature's correctness rests on an external tool's behavior, verify it firsthand by reading that tool's ACTUAL shipped source, and machine-verify the exact direction the feature relies on — an inverse-direction test plus round-trip reasoning is weaker.** Item 17e (`quarto.newNotebook`) needed a Jupyter `raw` front-matter cell. BACKLOG:190 said "verify firsthand, do not assume" the `raw` cell ↔ `NotebookCellKind.Code`+`languageId:"raw"` mapping. The strongest firsthand source is the built-in extension's shipped bytes in the installed app: `/Applications/Visual Studio Code.app/Contents/Resources/app/extensions/ipynb/dist/` — `notebookSerializerWorker.js` `S(t)` (serialize: a cell with `kind!==Markup` and `languageId==="raw"` → `{cell_type:"raw"}`; the dispatch is `I(t,e)= t.kind===1?markdown : t.languageId==="raw"?raw : code`, so a `Code`(=2)+`"raw"` cell always hits `raw`) and `ipynbMain.node.js` `F1` (deserialize: `cell_type:"raw"` → `new NotebookCellData(Code, source, "raw")`). That same file also gives the **authoritative new-notebook pattern to mirror**: `ipynb.newUntitledIpynb` seeds `NotebookData.metadata = {cells:[],metadata:{},nbformat:4,nbformat_minor:5}` per call (the serializer's top-level `P(t)` reads those keys; the seeded `cells:[]` is overwritten by the real cells). **Direction gap the adversarial review caught:** my first serializer guard opened a hand-written raw-cell `.ipynb` and asserted it loads as `Code`+`"raw"` — the DESERIALIZE direction, i.e. the *inverse* of the SAVE the feature depends on. Round-trip reasoning ("serializers are symmetric") is acceptable but indirect; the direct machine proof is a `WorkspaceEdit` + `vscode.NotebookEdit.replaceCells` that sets the adapter's exact cells on a file-backed notebook, `await notebook.save()`, then `readFileSync` + `JSON.parse` and assert `cells[0].cell_type==="raw"`. Both directions now run in the EDH. **Break-revert trap (reinforces #115):** to prove a `registerCommand` is load-bearing, break the command-ID **string** (`"quarto.newNotebook"`→`"…_BROKEN"`) — it compiles clean and yields a genuine runtime RED (command-not-found) on exactly the command-dependent tests, while a serializer/contract guard with no command call stays green (gate-d discrimination). Commenting out the import or the call instead throws **TS6133** ("declared but never read") — a compile error that stops the whole suite, the masquerade #115 warns against, not a test RED. **When to apply:** any feature depending on VS Code built-in behavior (read `…/app/extensions/<ext>/dist/`, don't assume) — and any serializer/round-trip feature: machine-verify the direction the code actually uses (save-and-reread), not just its inverse; break the string literal, never the import, for a command-registration break-revert. |
| 124 | **A stateful module singleton set by the running extension is UNREACHABLE from an integration test that imports the same module from `src/` — the extension runs the esbuild bundle (`dist/extension.js`) while the test imports the tsc-compiled copy (`out/src/…`), so they are two different module instances.** Item 17a's passive cell-background decorator kept an `activeDecorator` singleton (set by `registerCellBackgroundFeature`, called from `activate()`) so a test-only `refresh…ForTesting()` helper could observe applied ranges. It failed with "feature is not registered" on the clean run: `activate()` set the *bundle's* singleton, the test read the *compiled* copy's (never set) one. STATELESS exports (`cellBackgroundRangesFor`) are instance-agnostic and test fine across the boundary; a mutable module observer is not. **Corollary trap I hit:** in the earlier break-revert run that same test *also* failed, and I mis-read it as my intentional wiring-break reddening the test — but it fails REGARDLESS of the wiring, so it was never a load-bearing test of the `activate()` wiring (a false-positive RED; verify a break reddens for the RIGHT reason before trusting it as gate-d evidence). **Fix:** the test registers its own decorator in *its* instance (a fake `{subscriptions:[]}` `ExtensionContext`, disposed in `finally`) and exercises the apply path end-to-end there; the `activate()` wiring itself is runtime-verified (it ran in every test's `before()` EDH activation without error), NOT asserted — like the `resetDeactivation()` wiring (S107). **Second technique — gate d for a passive decorator with no VS Code read-back:** VS Code exposes no API to read applied `TextEditorDecorationType` decorations back, so faithfully test the observable surfaces — the document→ranges bridge and the enabled/languageId gating against real docs+config (break-revert-proven), and drive the apply path so `createTextEditorDecorationType`+`setDecorations` are proven not to throw on real editors — and disclose the rendered pixels as runtime/eyeball-only. This is **NOT FM #24** when the runtime path actually runs (activate executed the feature in the EDH; `setDecorations` ran with real ranges): distinguish "the runtime path never runs" (build-passes-ship-it) from "the path runs but its visual output isn't machine-observable" (honest disclosure). **When to apply:** any integration test that needs to observe module-level state the running extension mutates (register your own instance; don't read the bundle's) — and any decoration/visual feature (test the bridge+gating+apply-path; eyeball the pixels). |

| 127 | **A pure-logic generalisation that swaps a specific literal matcher for a capturing one can silently regress multi-token inputs the existing suite never contained — "no behavior change" is a claim about ALL inputs, so verify it adversarially against the general case, not just the green suite.** Item 17d follow-on (A) generalised the notebook callout renderer from a `.callout-note` literal regex to a capturing `/\.callout-([a-z]+)/` + a `CALLOUT_TITLES` map. The green suite proves the tested cases, not the class of inputs it never contained. |
| 128 | **When you DEFER part of a parsed syntax, make the parser IGNORE the deferred forms cleanly — a partial parse that half-reads deferred syntax can actively corrupt the IN-SCOPE behavior, which is worse than merely not supporting the deferred feature.** Item 17d follow-on (C), S115, added generic Pandoc fenced-div rendering (`::: {.className}` → `<div id/class>body</div>`) and explicitly DEFERRED `key=value` attributes. Fix: strip `key=value`/`key="value"` segments BEFORE scanning id/class, so the deferred syntax is safely ignored, not half-read. A 5-lens adversarial `Workflow` panel found this (MEDIUM) plus two LOW Pandoc-fidelity gaps in the same hand-rolled parser (several ids kept the FIRST, pandoc keeps the LAST; interior dots in `.a.b`/`#id.class` were truncated at the first dot) — all THREE verified FIRSTHAND against the installed `quarto render`/pandoc 3.1.1 before fixing (`<div style="padding: .5em">` with NO class; `<div id="b">`; `<div class="a.b">`), and the panel's ground-truth claims were correct on every count (reinforces #127 — the same file's attribute matcher has been the source of both sessions' only real defects). | 129 | **"Derived firsthand" is only as complete as the CANDIDATE LIST you probed — a membership/passthrough set derived by probing the reference tool is silently incomplete if the probe's input space is; and when a reference tool ships a decode step, reuse the host library's equivalent rather than hand-rolling it.** Item 17d follow-on, S116, made the notebook-renderer emit generic-div `key=value` attributes with pandoc's HTML5 `data-` prefix rule (a known-attribute passthrough set) + bare-word `::: foo`. "Derived firsthand" felt authoritative but was only as complete as the probe's inputs; re-deriving against a COMPREHENSIVE candidate list (all HTML global/element attrs + every event-handler family + RDFa) gave the definitive 214 (0 wrongly included). **(2) a hand-rolled decode that was wrong AND carried a false "matches pandoc" docstring.** The quoted-value reader unescaped only `\"`/`\'`/`\\` and claimed "any other `\x` stays literal, matching pandoc" — but pandoc's litChar unescapes `\` before ALL ASCII punctuation (`\.`→`.`) and decodes character references (`&amp;`→`&`, `&#65;`→`A`); markdown-it stores literals, so renderAttrs then DOUBLE-escaped (`&amp;`→`&amp;amp;`). Fix: capture the raw value and decode it through the host's `state.md.utils.unescapeAll`, which reproduces pandoc's litChar EXACTLY (verified firsthand across backslash-punct, entity, numeric, and `\"` cases) — reuse the reference-equivalent decoder, don't reinvent it. **(3) a guard silently dropped by a same-session restructure.** Increment 1's bare-word branch had a `!startsWith("{")` guard; increment 4's restructure of `parseDivAttrs` dropped it, so `::: {.callout-note}x` (a complete block glued to trailing junk) became a `<div>` with a literal class `{.callout-note}x` instead of falling through. | 130 | **A forward LOOKAHEAD scan that classifies lines must model EVERY line-classification the recursive parser makes downstream, or the lookahead silently disagrees with the recursion — and the disagreement can be a NEW regression the "fix" itself introduces.** S117 fixed the HIGH nested-same-length fenced-div bug (from #129's panel) in `notebook-callout.ts` by adding NESTING DEPTH tracking to `calloutRule`'s closing-fence scan. Firsthand pandoc 3.6.3 grounding first corrected the BACKLOG's own suggested fix ("push a level for a nested opener of length ≥ the current opener") — pandoc's closer length is INDEPENDENT of the opener's (a `::::` div closes on a bare `:::`, verified), so the pre-existing `< markerCount` gate was ALSO non-faithful and had to become `< MIN_MARKERS`; keeping it would have mis-tracked mixed-length nesting. RED→GREEN on the same-length case, then 7 more grounded nesting tests (6 genuine REGRESSION-catchers, proven by stashing the fix and re-running: same-length braced/bare-word, closer-length independence, callout-in-generic, generic-in-callout, triple nesting). **Then a 5-lens adversarial `Workflow` panel caught a regression the depth fix ITSELF introduced:** the scan walked every line flatly, so a `:::`-looking OPENER inside a ```` ``` ````/`~~~` fenced code block (a `::: {.x}` shown as EXAMPLE code) was counted as a nested opener → the div's real closer was consumed as a depth decrement → the div auto-closed at block end and swallowed following content. Adjudicated FIRSTHAND (pre-fix vs post-fix vs pandoc): pre-fix MATCHED pandoc, post-depth-fix BROKE it — a true regression, because the new depth counter now cares about opener-looking lines that the OLD first-closer scan had harmlessly ignored. Fix: the scan tracks fenced-code state (mirroring markdown-it's fence rule — ≥3 ` or ~, indent <4, closer same-char/≥length/trailing-spaces, backtick info-no-backtick), treating everything inside a code block as literal; this ALSO fixed the pre-existing bare-`:::`-inside-code early-close (one root-cause fix, both directions). **The panel's other 20 cases (empty `::: {}` and paragraph-glued openers nested in a div) I adjudicated firsthand as PRE-EXISTING and self-consistent, NOT regressions** — for same-length fences the empty-`{}` case is byte-identical pre/post, and `isDivOpener` is deliberately consistent with what the plugin RENDERS as a div (making it match pandoc's broader degenerate-opener set would de-sync depth from the rendered structure). Held 1-and-done by fixing only the introduced regression and filing the pre-existing consequences to BACKLOG. | When adding depth/nesting/lookahead tracking to a scan that runs BEFORE a recursive parse: enumerate every way the recursive parser classifies a line (fence openers, fence CLOSERS of independent length, code blocks, paragraph-interrupt, the plugin's own opener set) and make the lookahead agree with ALL of them — a scan that models only the case in the bug report introduces regressions on the cases it forgot (here: fenced code). Ground the closer/opener semantics firsthand against the reference tool (the BACKLOG's own suggested fix was subtly wrong on closer-length independence). Run an adversarial panel AFTER the fix and adjudicate each finding firsthand pre-fix-vs-post-fix-vs-reference: a divergence that is IDENTICAL pre and post is pre-existing (out of scope, disclose); one where pre-fix matched the reference and post-fix diverges is a regression YOU introduced (must fix). Keep the lookahead's opener predicate consistent with what actually renders, not with the reference's superset. |
| 131 | **When a feature "moves" a value from one render context to another, an escaping/fallback rule that was implicit in the OLD context must be RE-ESTABLISHED explicitly in the NEW one — and grounding the reference tool tells you exactly which rules those are.** S118 added custom callout titles to `notebook-callout.ts`: a `title=` attribute or a leading `##` heading becomes the callout title. | 136 | **A completion enum is NOT a validation acceptance set: `SchemaField.values` being non-empty does NOT mean the value set is CLOSED. Quarto's `valuesOfSchema` (`src/core/yaml-schema.ts:638-697`) populates `values` for COMPLETION — it flattens `anyOf` and pulls `string.completions` in — so a field can carry a non-empty `values` yet still accept ANY string. Two open families: `anyOf`-with-a-free-arm (`output` = `anyOf[boolean,"asis",string,object]` → `output: banana` renders exit 0) AND bare `string:{completions:[…]}` (`engine`, `documentclass`, `pagestyle`, `linkstyle`, `animation-hook` → `engine: banana` renders exit 0). Naive `value ∉ values ⇒ flag` false-positives on all of them — the cardinal sin for a false-negative-only feature. **The design fix:** add a derived `valuesClosed` bit (mirroring the existing `ClosedKeySet.closed` precedent) computed by a sibling of `valuesOfSchema` that returns closed=false for ANY reachable free arm — a `string` node is ALWAYS open, INCLUDING `string:{completions}`; only bare/object `enum` and `boolean` are closed. **The process lesson (the humbling part):** my FIRST-draft plan listed `string.completions` as a *closed* arm, AND the census probe I wrote to size the dragon (`admitsFreeScalar`) ALSO treated completions as closed — so the grounding tool inherited the exact bug it was meant to expose, and the initial census (22 open) under-counted. A 16-agent adversarial review `Workflow` (5 lenses, each finding verified firsthand with renders) caught it as a CONFIRMED CRITICAL; I confirmed firsthand (`engine: banana`/`animation-hook: myhook` → exit 0), reclassified `string` as always-open, and re-derived the corrected census (45/219 open, committed as a re-runnable script). Also grounded empirically (superseding a stale prior-plan claim): a wrong CLOSED-type value is render-FATAL (Error) in BOTH front matter and `#|` cell metadata; booleans accept exactly the six YAML-1.2 spellings `true\|True\|TRUE\|false\|False\|FALSE` UNQUOTED (quoted or `yes`/`on` → rejected), while string enums accept quoted-or-unquoted members case-sensitively — so the matcher must treat booleans and enums differently. | When planning ANY validator that reuses a data structure built for a DIFFERENT purpose (completion, display, indexing): the reused field's semantics may not be the validation contract — here "offer these" ≠ "only these are legal." Derive the actual closed/exhaustive signal separately, default to NOT-flag for anything not positively proven closed, and ground it firsthand against the real tool (render the wrong value, don't trust the docs' simplified enum). And treat a grounding PROBE with the same skepticism as the plan: if the probe encodes the same classification the plan does, an adversarial re-derivation (or an independent reviewer that RE-RUNS it) is what catches a shared blind spot — a probe that agrees with your plan is not independent confirmation. |
| 137 | **Closed-set VALUE validation has more cardinal-sin false-positive shapes than a principled rule predicts — render the specific quote/case/enum edges, don't trust the derivation.** Shipping Phase 1 of value-validation (BACKLOG #43), a mandatory adversarial review (`quarto render` 1.7.33 firsthand) caught **two false positives my strict-TDD battery AND the S123 plan's principled rule both missed**: (a) an **enum whose members include YAML booleans** (`message: {enum:[true,false,"NA"]}`, `results`) accepts the six boolean spellings — `#\| message: True` renders **exit 0** — so `acceptsBoolean` must key on "the enum contains a boolean member," NOT on the schema node being a boolean *type* (the plan's "enum ⇒ case-sensitive" framing implied `acceptsBoolean:false`, which flags `message: True`); (b) a **quoted value + trailing inline comment** (`#\| fig-align: "center" # note`) renders exit 0, but the cell-option value slot keeps the comment for quoted values (`slotsOf` strips comments only when UNQUOTED), so a naive `unquote` (strip iff first==last==quote) can't unwrap it → membership fails → flag. Fix (a): `closednessOfSchema` enum branches set `acceptsBoolean = enum.some(v => typeof v === "boolean")`. Fix (b): quote-aware `unquote` — take content up to the matching close quote, ignore trailing junk (accepts a benign false negative on malformed `"x" garbage`, fine under false-negative-only). Both are exactly Learning #54a ("value equality is where quoting/casing/whitespace hide false positives — careful TDD alone was not sufficient"): I had probed quote/case in the battery but not *enum-members-that-are-booleans* or *quoted+comment* specifically. | Building any closed-set VALUE validator (Phase 2 front-matter values; numeric/nested v2): enumerate the OPEN shapes (anyOf-free-arm, `string:{completions}`, enum-of-booleans-needs-acceptsBoolean, quoted+comment) and RENDER each specific edge firsthand before shipping; run the mandatory adversarial `Workflow` review — it caught 2 criticals here that TDD + a grounded plan both missed. Also: a diagnostic feature must slice keys/values from the SAME snapshot text `findCellOptionLines` saw, NEVER `document.lineAt()` after the schema `await` (a mid-await edit doesn't bump the generation guard → `lineAt(staleLine)` throws / slices shifted content; mirror the `yaml-diagnostics.ts` sibling, which builds ranges purely from snapshot numbers). |
| 138 | **A NEW value-extraction SURFACE that reuses a proven matcher can still ship surface-specific cardinal-sin false positives the matcher's guards never had to cover — render the shapes unique to the new surface, and give a line-by-line front-matter scanner MULTI-LINE-value state.** Shipping Phase 2 of value-validation (BACKLOG #43 — top-level front-matter values), the matcher (`isWrongValue`) and the closedness model were REUSED unchanged from Phase 1, so the only new code was the enumerator (`findFrontMatterValueLines`) + wiring — yet the mandatory §9 adversarial review (4-lens/13-agent `Workflow`, each finding `quarto render`-verified, 9/9 CONFIRMED / 0 refuted) caught **two NEW cardinal-sin false positives that Phase 1's single-line `#|` surface simply never exercised**: (a) a **YAML node property on a value** — anchor (`toc: &a true`), tag (`toc: !!bool true`), alias (`toc: *a`) — all render **exit 0** (quarto resolves the node) but the enumerator slices `&a true`/`*a`/`!!bool true` into `rawToken` and the shared matcher, seeing a scalar that is neither a boolean spelling nor an enum member, flags it; (b) a **multi-line FLOW collection** (`mymeta: {` / `toc: yes,` / `x: 1}`) renders exit 0, but the line-by-line enumerator re-parses the column-0 continuation line `toc: yes,` as an independent top-level mapping and flags `toc`. Fix (a): extend the shared matcher's non-scalar skip guard from `/^[[\]{}|>]/` to `/^[[\]{}|>&*!]/` (a token carrying a node property can't be reduced to a plain scalar → flag nothing; false-negative-safe, and single-sourced so Phase 1 `#| echo: &a true` is protected too). Fix (b): the enumerator tracks flow depth (net `{`/`[` vs `}`/`]`, entered only by a value STARTING with `[`/`{`) and skips continuation lines while inside a multi-line value — quote-naive (the plan forbids YAML-parsing; an over-count only OVER-skips = safe FN), with the existing indent check backing it up for indented continuations; block scalars (`|`/`>`) need no tracking since their content is indented and already skipped. I pre-identified (a) myself during a design firsthand pre-check before the review returned; the review independently confirmed it and added (b). | When extending a validator to a NEW value surface that reuses an existing matcher: do NOT assume the matcher's guards (built for the old surface's shapes) cover the new surface — enumerate and RENDER the YAML shapes the new surface can host that the old one couldn't (front matter can host anchors/aliases/tags and multi-line flow collections that single-line `#|` options cannot). A whole-document line-by-line front-matter scanner needs explicit multi-line-value state: a column-0 line inside an unclosed flow collection is a continuation, NOT a top-level mapping — without that state it will flag continuation keys on a doc quarto renders clean. Run the mandatory adversarial `Workflow` — it caught 2 criticals here that strict TDD + the plan's dragons both missed, exactly per Learning #54a/#137. |
| 139 | **A behavior-preserving extraction of a lifecycle/state-machine is faithful when the guards move VERBATIM into the injected callback and the factory becomes a pure writer — bracket it with a design audit before and an adversarial diff review after, and never let a host-untestable behavior ship as a FALSE GREEN.** Extracting the ~75-line `DiagnosticCollection` skeleton both diagnostics features copied (BACKLOG:47) into `createDebouncedDiagnosticsFeature({collectionName, gate, compute})`, the correctness-critical part was the Session-47 per-URI **generation guard** (a slow first schema-load resolving after a newer edit must discard itself). The faithful factoring: the factory bumps the generation **synchronously in `refresh` before `await spec.compute`** and does `if (result === null) return; collection.set(uri, result)`; `compute` returns `Diagnostic[] | null` and carries the guard checks (empty-path `isCurrent() ? [] : null`; post-await `document.isClosed || !isCurrent() → null`) **relocated verbatim** from the old `refreshDiagnostics`. Two subtleties a Plan-agent design audit surfaced and the impl honored: (i) the factory needs NO post-`await` re-check — only a **microtask** separates compute's return from the write, and generation bumps happen only in `refresh`'s synchronous prologue reached via **macrotasks** (VS Code events / the debounce `setTimeout`), which cannot interleave a microtask drain; (ii) `document.isClosed` must stay INSIDE compute's non-empty branch — hoisting it into the factory would add an `isClosed` gate to the empty fast-path the original deliberately lacks (dropping a `[]`-write for a closed-but-current empty doc). Behavior-preservation was PROVEN, not asserted: both existing integration suites unchanged and green (the RED-if-broken net), plus a post-impl adversarial diff review that returned no regression across 8 checks — the same "apply adversarial refutation to your own work" bracket used for value-validation, now on a refactor. **The false-GREEN catch:** the synthetic-suite's onDidClose/D4 test PASSED without a close handler, because closing an untitled doc makes VS Code auto-clear its diagnostics — so an "after close, zero diagnostics" assertion proves nothing. I DROPPED it and documented the gap (the two real suites already record the identical host limitation) rather than shipping fake coverage; the genuinely testable half (`cancelPending` cancels a prior pending timer) is covered by the debounced-coalescing test instead. | When extracting a shared lifecycle/concurrency skeleton into a factory taking injected callbacks: relocate the concurrency guards VERBATIM into the callback and make the factory a pure writer (single write site, strict `=== null` sentinel so an empty valid array still clears); prove faithfulness with the pre-existing suites staying green + an adversarial diff review, not just a fresh unit test. Bracket a low-reversibility/high-compounding refactor with a design audit (before coding) and an adversarial review (after) — a Plan agent for the design, a review agent for the diff. And treat any integration assertion that passes against a KNOWN-broken stub as a false GREEN to investigate (here: untitled-doc dispose auto-clears diagnostics) — drop it and document the host gap rather than shipping coverage that proves nothing. Build a factory's own RED-first tests via a SYNTHETIC feature (a stand-in gate+compute on untitled/plaintext docs the shipped features ignore, asserting by a unique diagnostic `code`) so the extracted machinery is tested directly, not only through its real callers. |
| 140 | **Planning-first + reading the implementations SHRANK a deferred "largest new pure-core surface" to mostly-wiring — and the mandatory adversarial review of the PLAN (before any code) caught a CRITICAL false positive in its one genuinely-new algorithm.** Planning the v2 **nested** front-matter VALUE validation slice (BACKLOG:46, `docs/planning/2026-07-20-nested-frontmatter-value-validation-plan.md`), the S123 plan's §4.3 had deferred it with the estimate "the largest new pure-core surface … a forward-scan analog of `nestedParentPath`." Reading the code firsthand (Learning #6, decisively) showed that framing was too pessimistic: the nested-path **resolution** already exists (`frontMatterKeys(parentPath)` resolves `["execute"]`/`["format"]`/`["format",fmt]`/`["format",fmt,opt]` — `yaml-schema.ts:507-549`, the exact lookup the completion provider inverts at `providers/yaml.ts:102-114`), the **path computation** already exists (`nestedParentPath`, cursor-anchored but reusable per-line in a forward loop — the precedent `mappingContainerKey` already set), and **nested closedness** is already derived correctly by the reader (`objectChildren`→`annotateClosedness`; a grounding `Workflow` compiled `parseSchemaIndex` against the installed 1.7.33 schema and found `format.<fmt>.*` closedness has zero divergence from firsthand renders). It also corrected a handoff imprecision: nested is NOT "a 3rd `createDebouncedDiagnosticsFeature` caller" (S126's phrase) — it is the SAME `.qmd`/`quarto-value` surface, so it EXTENDS `computeValueDiagnostics` with a third loop, not a new feature. **The humbling part (Learning #138 at the PLAN stage):** a 5-lens adversarial review of the plan — each finding `quarto render` 1.7.33-verified — returned execute/format closedness CLEAN and citations CLEAN, but caught a **CRITICAL + HIGH false positive in the one new algorithm, the flow-depth guard**: (a) an **anchored/tagged flow opener at depth** (`format.html.foo: &a { x: 1,` then `toc: yes }`, renders exit 0) bypasses a first-char `/^[[{]/` arm because the token starts with `&`, so the flow continuation `toc: yes` gets flagged; (b) a **quote-naive counter UNDER-counts** at depth (`foo: {` / `a: "}",` / `echo: maybe,` — the quoted `}` drops `flowDepth` early → `echo` flagged, exit 0). The top-level enumerator's "quote-naive over-count is a safe FN" reasoning (Learning #138b) is FALSE at depth because there is no column-0 backstop. Fix folded into the plan: a **quote-aware, node-property-aware, net-based** `flowScan` (arm on net>0 over the whole token, count only outside quotes). The review also disclosed a MEDIUM coverage gap (other closed containers `crossref:`/`website:`/`brand:` — `crossref.chapters: banana` renders exit 1 — are unreachable via `NESTED_CONTAINERS={execute,format}`; a safe FN, now named in §4.3). | When a prior plan DEFERS a slice with a scary cost estimate ("largest new surface"), re-READ the implementations before trusting it — a deferred estimate is an assumption (Learning #6), and reusable resolution/traversal/derivation infra often already exists (here the completion path's lookup, inverted, WAS the validator). | 141 | **A "skip continuation lines" guard for a line-local YAML scanner must cover EVERY line-spanning value form YAML has — flow collections `{}`/`[]`, block scalars `\|`/`>`, AND quoted scalars `"…`/`'…` — not just the forms the plan enumerated; a scanner that tracks only brackets misses a `key: "text` that WRAPS, and at DEPTH (no column-0 backstop) that continuation line reads as a real nested mapping and its `closed-sibling: text` gets flagged while quarto folds the whole span into ONE accepted string — a cardinal-sin false positive.** Implementing the nested front-matter VALUE slice (BACKLOG:46, S128), L2's `flowScan` guarded the plan's three §7.1 FLOW shapes (same-indent continuation, anchored opener, quoted brace) and `nestedParentPath` protected block scalars, but NOTHING covered a **multi-line QUOTED scalar** — the flow-bracket counter never arms on an unterminated quote (no `{}`/`[]` in it). The **mandatory §9 adversarial review (a 4-lens `quarto render`-verified `Workflow`) caught it as a CONFIRMED CRITICAL FP**, render-verified for double quote, single quote, and both `execute:`/`format:` levels: `format.html.title: "Start of title` / `echo: false is the default"` renders **exit 0** (quarto folds it into one string) yet the code flagged `echo`. The UNQUOTED sibling renders exit 1 (a real key — flagging is correct there), which is exactly why the quoted case is the trap. **Fix:** unify the continuation tracker into ONE state machine `scanFlow(s, depth, quote)` that carries BOTH the flow-bracket depth AND an open-quote char across lines — they nest (a bracket inside a quote is literal; a quote persists across lines) — and skip a line when `flowDepth > 0 \|\| openQuote !== null`. **The humbling part:** this is Learning #138 AGAIN, one layer down — a NEW value SURFACE ships ≥1 review-caught surface-specific FP — and BOTH the plan's §7 dragons AND my own parallel firsthand FP-sweep (tabs, flow variants, quoted-KEYs, per-format scoping, body-fence) probed everything EXCEPT a multi-line quoted VALUE; only a fresh adversarial review lens, hunting from outside my implementation frame, found the one shape I'd normalized away. | When writing ANY line-local (non-YAML-parsing) scanner that must skip the continuation lines of a multi-line value: enumerate ALL of YAML's line-spanning forms (flow collection, block scalar, quoted scalar) UP FRONT and unify them into one carried state machine — do NOT guard only the forms a plan or a prior FP happened to name (the omitted form is where the cardinal-sin FP hides). Render a wrapped quoted scalar whose continuation looks like a sibling key (`key: "x` / `closed-sibling: y"`) BEFORE writing the scanner. And run the mandatory adversarial review even when you've already hunted FPs yourself — a fresh lens outside your implementation frame catches the shape you normalized away (here a multi-line quoted VALUE, after I'd probed quoted KEYs and flow values but not this). |
| 142 | **`quarto render` rejects a bad value at TWO different layers, and a value-validation feature must mirror ONLY the quarto YAML-SCHEMA layer — never the downstream pandoc layer — so "renders exit 1" / "renders exit 0" is the WRONG grounding criterion; "does quarto's schema reject it" is the right one.** Planning the NUMERIC value slice (BACKLOG:47, S129), grounding showed `fig-width: wide` → exit 1 with **"Validation of YAML front matter failed … which must instead be a number"** (quarto's own schema layer — the message family the enum/boolean slices already mirror; also spelled "…cell metadata…" for `#|` options), but `toc-depth: 2.5` / `columns: 2.5` / `dpi: .inf` → exit 1 with **"pandoc … Error in $: parsing Int failed"** — a DOWNSTREAM, format-specific pandoc-writer error that fires AFTER quarto's schema passed. Quarto's schema types ALL of these `"number"` (there is **no `"integer"` leaf** in the 1.7.33 resource) and accepts any numeric literal — int, float, sign, scientific, `1_000`, `0x1A`, `.inf`, `.nan` — so the matcher must accept them too; the float-on-conceptual-integer rejection belongs to pandoc, is format-dependent, and has no schema signal to key on. **Two consequences the plan encodes:** (a) a fixture of "valid" values must be grounded on **quarto-schema-accepts** (no "must instead be a number"), NOT on "renders exit 0" — a schema-valid literal can still pandoc-reject (`dpi: .inf` → exit 1 pandoc), so putting it in a valid fixture and re-rendering misleads the executor; keep exotic literals on genuinely-float fields (`fig-width`). (b) The safe "is-a-number" predicate is a regex `R` that is a verified STRICT SUPERSET of quarto's schema-accept set (built by 60 firsthand `quarto render` probes), so anything `R` fails, quarto also rejects → zero FP; forms where quarto is stricter than `R` (`+.5`, uppercase `0X…`, `1_`) are left as deliberate safe FNs. Also: a QUOTED number (`fig-width: "6"`) IS schema-rejected (flag it), like a quoted boolean. | When building or planning validation that mirrors an external tool's acceptance: identify EVERY layer the tool rejects at (here quarto-schema vs pandoc), mirror ONLY the layer whose contract you actually reproduce, and ground "valid" fixtures on that layer's accept — not on overall exit code. When the tool's type is a permissive superset of a stricter downstream check (schema `number` ⊃ pandoc integer), build your matcher as a verified SUPERSET predicate of the layer you mirror (false-negative-only), never as the intersection with the downstream layer. |
| 143 | **A new VALUE-VALIDATION dimension can EXPOSE a latent enumerator false positive that was previously MASKED because the affected keys were OPEN — so the mandatory §9 review of a value slice must re-probe the SHARED enumerators, not just the new matcher logic.** The numeric slice (BACKLOG:47, S130) added `scalarType:"number"` to ~35 keys (`columns`/`fig-width`/`fig-height`/`layout-ncol`/`daemon`…). (2) A quote/flow continuation-skip guard belongs in ONE shared scanner used by every value-line enumerator; three near-identical scanners is three chances to fix only some (S128 fixed one of three). | Reviewing/shipping any value-validation slice that widens the validated key set: adversarially re-probe every SHARED value-line enumerator (top-level / nested / cell) for multi-line quoted/flow/block continuation FPs the new keys now reach — the FP hides in the enumerator, masked until the key stops being OPEN. Keep the continuation-skip guard in ONE shared `scanFlow`. |

Reuse a hardened shared enumerator rather than writing a new one — it is why this slice had no FP to find. |
| 146 | **This extension has ZERO runtime dependencies (esbuild bundles `src/` only), so every `npm audit` finding is a DEV-TOOLCHAIN vulnerability that never ships in the `.vsix` — clear it with the non-breaking `npm audit fix` (never `--force`) and verify via the compile+unit+integration+PACKAGE matrix, NOT a runtime F5.** Clearing GHSA-3jxr-9vmj-r5cp (`brace-expansion` DoS, the sole High-severity dashboard flag, S133): `npm ls brace-expansion --all` showed two installs — 5.0.6 via `@vscode/vsce`→`minimatch@10.2.5` and 2.1.1 via `mocha`→`minimatch@5.1.9`; `npm audit --json` reported `fixAvailable: true` (a boolean, meaning a semver-compatible non-breaking bump — an *object* would have meant a breaking major, needing `--force` and a real regression risk). `npm audit fix` bumped both (5.0.6→5.0.7, 2.1.1→2.1.2) touching **`package-lock.json` only** — confirm `git diff --name-only` does NOT include `package.json` (transitive-only = zero API-surface change). The severity label ("high") is the advisory's, not this repo's exposure: `package.json` `dependencies` is `(none)` and `grep -rl brace-expansion dist/` is empty, so the vuln lived purely in the test-runner glob + packager glob and never reached a user. **The faithful verification (gate d) is the toolchain matrix, because that is exactly what the changed packages run in:** `npm run compile` (bundle unaffected — sanity), `npm test` (mocha's `minimatch`→`brace-expansion@2.1.2` does test globbing), `npm run test:integration` (383, real host), and `npx @vscode/vsce package` (vsce's `minimatch@10.2.5`→`brace-expansion@5.0.7` does file globbing → the `.vsix`). A runtime F5 proves nothing here — the extension bundle doesn't contain the package. `.vsix` is gitignored; delete the verification build after. | Resolving an npm-audit finding in this repo: check `fixAvailable` is a boolean (non-breaking) before `npm audit fix`; NEVER `--force` without a plan-mode decision. Confirm the diff is `package-lock.json`-only (transitive). State the real exposure honestly (zero runtime deps → dev-only, not the advisory's raw severity). Verify by running the toolchain matrix that actually exercises the changed packages (test + package), not a runtime smoke test. One advisory = one deliverable; do not batch unrelated bumps. |
| 147 | **The `_quarto.yml` project-config surface and the `.qmd` document surface resolve against DIFFERENT schemas through DIFFERENT code paths — so the S132 "reuse the already-annotated `.children`" trick does NOT transfer to project-config value validation; the project surface resolves key NAMES only.** Planning value validation for `_quarto.yml`'s `project:`/`website:`/`book:` container children (S134, `docs/planning/2026-07-21-quarto-yml-value-validation-plan.md`). So this slice needs THREE genuinely-new pieces (a project-scoped, super-aware child-FIELD resolver reusing `annotateClosedness`/`annotateScalarType`/`valuesOfSchema`; a scanFlow-aware bare-YAML value enumerator; a thin filename-gated feature) — heavier than any prior value slice, though it still reuses the shared `isWrongValue`+`valueMessage`. Grounding (parser-mirroring harness with super-merge + `quarto render` 1.7.33): the depth-1 closed surface is MODEST — 16 (container,child) positions (project 1, website 6, book 9 via super base-website+csl-item-shared, manuscript 0), ~5 high-value string enums. Two cardinal-sin subtleties grounded firsthand: **(a)** `project.type` is `{string:{completions}}` → schema-OPEN; `project.type: banana` exit-1s DOWNSTREAM (`Unsupported project type`), NOT at `readAndValidateYamlFromFile` — so mirroring only the schema layer (Learning #142) means NOT flagging it, and NOT curating it closed (that would flag a value the schema accepts). **(b)** the multi-line-quoted-continuation FP (Learning #143) is LIVE because the slice adds a NEW enumerator (a `draft-mode:`-looking line inside a quoted `title:` renders exit 0). The mandatory 4-lens plan-review `Workflow` came back 3 SOUND / 1 SOUND_WITH_FIXES (2 LOW) + 1 doc-drift LOW (all 3 re-verified firsthand + folded: `project.preview.*` is a cheaper deferred depth-2 target reachable via super/ref; `execute:` in `_quarto.yml` is schema-validated today with no editor feedback and its children are already document-reader-resolvable; `book.type` is a 45-value not 44-value CSL enum). | When a feature spans two surfaces that look similar (both YAML, both "container children"): read BOTH resolution paths firsthand before assuming reuse — a shipped mechanism on surface A may not reach surface B if they use different schemas/resolvers (here: names-only vs annotated children; `resolveObjectProperties` lacks `super`). Ground the closed surface with a parser-mirroring harness that replicates the ACTUAL resolution (including super chains the shared resolver skips), then confirm each against `quarto render`. A NEW enumerator reopens the scanFlow FP surface (unlike S132's reuse) — make it scanFlow-aware and treat it as the primary §9 risk. Count enum sizes programmatically, not by hand (44 vs 45 slip). |
| 148 | **A new value-line enumerator DOES reopen the scanFlow FP surface (unlike S132's allow-list-only reuse), but modeling it on the already-hardened `findNestedFrontMatterValueLines` keeps it clean — and a super-merge child resolver's precedence choice (own-wins) is a cardinal-sin risk ONLY if some own-vs-super name collision has DIFFERING closedness, which instrumenting the real schema rules out.** Implementing `_quarto.yml` project-config value validation (S135, the value family's SIXTH slice and FIRST on the `_quarto.yml` surface; plan `docs/planning/2026-07-21-quarto-yml-value-validation-plan.md`). The plan's three-piece design held exactly, with two implementation choices worth recording. **(1) The super-aware reader was built by THREADING each property's schema through the EXISTING `resolveClosedKeysObject` super-merge (extend `ClosedKeySet` with a `properties: Map<name,schema>`, own-wins on merge), NOT a parallel walk** — a single resolution feeds both `projectKeys` (reads `.names`, byte-identical) and the new `projectFields` (annotates `.properties` via the reused `valuesOfSchema`/`annotateClosedness`/`annotateScalarType`). This is the least-risk way to add a second consumer to a shared resolver: keep the existing output field, add a parallel field built in the same loop, regression-guard the existing one. **(2) The own-wins precedence question turned out to have a PROVABLE safety argument:** the §9 super-resolution lens instrumented the real 1.7.33 schema and found exactly 2 own-vs-super name collisions across all three containers (book `title`/`description`, both KEPT=book-schema-own / DROPPED=base-website), both OPEN=OPEN — so own-wins can never FLIP a child's closedness, meaning the merge-precedence choice cannot produce a cardinal-sin FP regardless of which side wins. When a design has a "which wins?" merge choice, check whether the collision set even contains a closedness disagreement before agonizing over correctness. **(3) The new enumerator reopened the scanFlow FP surface** (contra Learning #145's S132-clean case — that slice added NO enumerator), so the multi-line-quoted-continuation FP (Learning #143) was genuinely LIVE. But modeling `findProjectConfigValueLines` on the already-hardened `findNestedFrontMatterValueLines` (reuse the shared `scanFlow`, arm `flowDepth`/`openQuote` on each emitted value's whole token, skip continuation lines) made it inherit the proven guard — both my firsthand sweep (12 exotic shapes) and the independent 4-lens `Workflow` (37 shapes) found ZERO cardinal-sin FPs, CONVERGING. **(4) Block scalars (`|`/`>`) are handled by a DIFFERENT guard than scanFlow** — scanFlow is blind to block scalars, but block-scalar content is always indented MORE than the container-child indent, so the `indent !== containerIndent` skip catches it (verified firsthand: `title: \|` + a deeper `draft-mode: hidden` renders exit 0 and is not flagged). Two guards cover the continuation surface: scanFlow for quotes/flow-collections, the indent guard for block scalars. **(5) A TAB-indented file is a non-defect edge** the review surfaced: the feature flags but quarto fails at the PARSE layer (`tab characters must not be used in indentation`), not the schema layer — NOT a cardinal-sin FP (quarto never accepts that value; the value is independently invalid; matches the document-surface features on malformed input). Grounding tools (rebuild per slice): `scratchpad/pf-harness.cjs` (esbuild-bundle `parseSchemaIndex` over the installed schema → prints `projectFields` per container = the exact 16 closed positions, the fastest wrongly-closed-child check) and `scratchpad/flag-harness.cjs` (the FULL diagnostic path — enumerator + `projectFields` + `isWrongValue` — over any `_quarto.yml`, for A/B vs `quarto render` exit codes). | Implementing a value slice that adds a NEW value-line enumerator: model it on the hardened `findNestedFrontMatterValueLines` and reuse the shared `scanFlow` — do not write a fresh continuation scanner. Adding a second consumer to a shared resolver: extend its output with a parallel field built in the same walk, keep the existing field byte-identical, regression-guard it. Before worrying about a merge-precedence ("own wins?") choice, instrument the real data to see whether any colliding name even has a closedness disagreement — if none do, the choice is safety-neutral. Remember two continuation guards, not one: scanFlow (quotes/flow) AND the indent guard (block scalars). Build the pf-harness (real-schema `projectFields`) and flag-harness (full path) and A/B every closed position + exotic shape against `quarto render`. |
| 149 | **A cross-surface reuse can be CHEAPER than a prior planning session estimated — verify the "harder tier" firsthand before scoping around it; and a numeric-VALUED enum is a cardinal-sin trap the shared string matcher can't handle (an off-list-garbage grounding sweep will MISS it — only numeric-coercion probes catch it).** Planning DEPTH-2 value validation for `_quarto.yml` project-config containers (S136, `docs/planning/2026-07-21-quarto-yml-depth2-value-validation-plan.md`). **(1) S134 deferred depth-2 in two tiers — (i) `project.preview.*` "cheap, super-reachable", (ii) `navbar/sidebar/search` "harder, via anyOf" — but firsthand grounding DISSOLVED the split:** the EXISTING shared `resolveObjectProperties` (via `objectChildren`, already anyOf-aware, already used by the document surface) resolves ALL 55 closed depth-2 grandchildren with ZERO mismatches vs a super+anyOf union resolver (`scratchpad/rop-compare.cjs`). No `super` is needed at depth-2 — the CONTAINER-level super merged the depth-1 children already, and each child's grandchildren come from its own `object`/`anyOf`. So the reader is a ≈2-line `objectChildren` reuse (populate `SchemaField.children`), identical cost for both tiers — S134's "anyOf is harder" cost model was wrong. Surfacing this to the operator (grounded) BEFORE writing the plan flipped the scope from a 3-boolean subset to the full 55+ position surface at the same reader cost. **(2) The MANDATORY 4-lens `quarto render`-verified review (`wf_c99d35f6-b02`) returned fp-cardinal UNSOUND (HIGH) — a real cardinal-sin FP my own firsthand grounding MISSED:** `google-analytics.version` (enum[3,4]) is the ONE numeric-VALUED enum in the surface. Quarto coerces YAML numerics, so `version: 3.0`≡`3`/`+4`/`04`/`4.0`/`3e0` all render exit 0, but the shared string-membership `isWrongValue` flags `3.0` (`"3.0" ∉ ["3","4"]`) = a cardinal-sin FP on a value quarto ACCEPTS. Re-verified firsthand: real `isWrongValue("3.0", {values:["3","4"],valuesClosed:true})` ⇒ true, and `quarto render version: 3.0` ⇒ exit 0. My §2.1 sweep only tested OFF-LIST garbage (`version: 5`), never a coercible numeric form — so the FP was invisible. **Fix: the reader leaves any numeric-member closed enum OPEN (a safe FN); this is a PRE-EXISTING latent property of the shared matcher** — `aspectratio` (enum[43,169,…]) is an all-numeric closed enum on the DOCUMENT surface today (`scratchpad/numenum.cjs`) — so a detection-preserving general fix (numeric-aware enum membership) is filed as a separate cross-surface deferred item. **(3) `annotateScalarType` also flags numeric-TYPED grandchildren** (`search.limit`/`collapse-after`, `scalarType:"number"`) which the plan's inventory had omitted (found by the completeness lens) — correct behavior, but 4 positions + fixtures owed. **(4) A depth-1/depth-2 name collision is a cardinal-sin FP, not just an FN:** `book.type` is the closed 45-value CSL enum while `book.cookie-consent.type: express` is valid — bare-name resolution would flag `express` (∉ CSL) = FP; by-path resolution (already mandated) prevents it. | When a prior planning session deferred work as "harder, needs X": verify X firsthand before scoping around the estimate — a shipped mechanism (here `resolveObjectProperties`, anyOf-aware) may already cover it, collapsing the cost. When grounding a closed-enum surface for cardinal-sin safety, DO NOT only test off-list garbage — for any numeric-VALUED enum, probe YAML-coercible numeric forms (`N.0`, `+N`, `0N`, `NeM`); quarto coerces them and a string matcher will FP. Run the MANDATORY adversarial review even when your own firsthand sweep feels complete — the fp-cardinal lens caught a HIGH the author's grounding missed. Re-verify every review finding firsthand (real matcher + `quarto render`) before folding, never trust the agent. |
| 150 | **A multi-line-quoted/flow value's OPENING line is a DISTINCT false-positive surface from its continuation lines, and a scanFlow continuation guard that arms AFTER emit protects only the continuation — never the opener.** The opener carries an unresolvable token (`location: "nav\` — an unterminated double-quoted scalar continued by an escaped newline), which `isWrongValue`/`unquote` cannot reduce to a plain scalar, so it is matched against the closed enum and flagged — yet quarto FOLDS `"nav\<nl>bar"` to `navbar` and renders exit 0 (a cardinal-sin FP). This was PRESENT AT DEPTH-1 (shipped in S135) and inherited unchanged by the depth-2 slice; **both S135's own L4 §9 review AND this session's author firsthand sweep tested continuation-line FPs but neither tested an opener-with-a-CLOSED-field FP** — only the fresh independent adversarial Workflow caught it (2nd consecutive session the panel caught a HIGH two firsthand passes missed, cf. #149). Fix: a value that opens an unterminated quote/flow must NOT be emitted for validation (its folded result is unknowable) — skip the opener but still arm the continuation guard; over-skipping a multi-line value is the safe FN direction. When to apply: for ANY value enumerator that arms a scanFlow continuation after emitting, the emitted opener itself is unvalidatable — guard it; and treat a prior session's "clean" review + your own author sweep as NON-substitutes for a fresh independent adversarial panel. |
| 151 | **When you port quarto's YAML numeric coercion to a JS matcher, the JS parser and the YAML number grammar DISAGREE on digit-group underscores — so a naive parse-and-compare ships a cardinal-sin FP.** Planning the general numeric-member-enum matcher fix (`isWrongValue` should validate a closed enum whose members are YAML *numbers* — `google-analytics.version` `[3,4]`, `aspectratio` `[43,169,…]` — by PARSED VALUE so quarto's coercion `3.0`≡`3`/`+169`≡`169` stops being flagged): `NUMBER_LITERAL` (the value-check regex, a strict superset of quarto's number set) MATCHES YAML underscored literals like `aspectratio: 4_3` (≡ 43, which quarto COERCES and renders exit 0), but JS `Number("4_3")` is `NaN` — so a `Number(token) !== member → flag` branch flags a value quarto ACCEPTS. FN-safe fix: parse with `Number()` (NEVER `parseFloat`/`parseInt` — `parseFloat("0x2b")`=0 would FP hex), and treat `NaN → do NOT flag`. **BOTH my firsthand `quarto render` grounding AND the plan's own truth table missed the underscore case** — I had grounded `1_0` (=10, out-of-set, renders REJECT), which made flag-on-NaN look correct; only the fresh independent adversarial plan-review Workflow caught it (the THIRD consecutive session — cf. #149/#150 — where the panel caught a HIGH cardinal-sin FP two firsthand passes missed). The same panel also corrected two over-strong universals I'd written: a "no mixed enum exists anywhere" claim that held only for REACHABLE surfaces (`brand-font-weight` is a mixed enum in the raw schema, merely unreachable at depth≤2), and a surface enumeration that missed `aspectratio`'s nested `format.beamer.aspectratio` reachability (a SECOND live FP path). When to apply: (1) any time a JS matcher reproduces a YAML-coercing numeric comparison, ground the JS parser against the grammar's exotic forms (underscores, hex, leading-dot, `.inf`/`.nan`) — `Number()` and a permissive regex DIVERGE; default `NaN→don't-flag`, and put the guard IN the algorithm/tests, not in prose. (2) A cardinal-sin-risk plan warrants an independent adversarial panel even after thorough author grounding — three sessions running, the panel beat two firsthand passes; state safety invariants as REACHABILITY claims, not universals. |
| 152 | **Replacing a VALUE-keyed heuristic with a TYPE-keyed one can EXPOSE a test-fixture-fidelity bug the value-keyed version silently tolerated — re-check that every fixture mirrors the real schema's member TYPES, not just its stringified values.** Implementing the S139 numeric-member-enum matcher fix as one strict-TDD vertical slice (L1 `SchemaField.numericMemberEnum` + a `Number()`/NaN-safe `isWrongNumericMember` branch [INERT] → L2 `numericMemberEnumOfSchema` annotation keyed on raw JS member types → L3 DELETE the S137 `openNumericMemberEnum` guard → L4 §9 review). The S137 guard keyed on the STRINGIFIED `field.values` (`.some(v => NUMERIC_LITERAL.test(v))`), so its unit fixture defined `google-analytics.version: {enum: ["3","4"]}` — JS **strings** — and the guard still fired (`"3"` matches the numeric regex), so the test passed on a fixture that did NOT mirror the real schema (which uses JS **numbers** `[3,4]`). The new type-keyed `numericMemberEnumOfSchema` (`typeof v === "number"`) correctly did NOT set the bit on the string fixture, turning the guard-deletion test RED for the RIGHT reason and forcing the fixture to be corrected to `[3, 4]` — a latent fidelity bug the value-keyed predecessor had masked for two sessions. **Second: when a planning session's own §9 panel has ALREADY caught and folded the cardinal-sin HIGH (S138 caught the NaN/underscore trap, #151), the implementation-session panel comes back CLEAN** — this slice's independent 4-lens panel (`wf_00c4cf2f-334`) AND the author's own firsthand FP battery (wide numeric forms, both positions) + guard-safety scan (all 225 project grandchildren, exactly 2 with the bit, `brand-font-weight` unreachable) CONVERGED on zero code defects. The panel is not ceremony even when it comes back clean: it independently re-grounded the fp-cardinal + guard-deletion invariants firsthand, and its doc-drift lens caught a stale test comment. **Third: deleting the S137 stopgap was a NET SIMPLIFICATION that both RESTORED lost coverage (`version: 5` now flagged) AND fixed ≥3 live shipped FPs (`aspectratio: 169.0`/`+169`/`0169`), on both the document and project surfaces, from ONE annotation choke point** (`annotateClosedness` → all three call sites) — the cross-surface fix reaches every surface for free. When to apply: (1) when you swap a value-keyed predicate for a type-keyed one, audit every existing fixture for TYPE fidelity to the real schema — the old predicate may have been green on a type-unfaithful fixture; a RED that appears "wrong" may be the type-keyed version correctly exposing the fixture bug. (2) A planning-session §9 panel that front-loads the cardinal-sin catch pays off at implementation: expect (and still run) a clean confirming panel + author grounding. (3) Prefer deleting a local stopgap over extending it when a shared-core fix supersedes it — net-negative diff, restored coverage, and fewer surfaces to keep in sync. |
| 153 | **Ground a plan's REACHABILITY / "no-X-exists" safety claim against the real SCHEMA's shape — not a proxy artifact (a curated reader, a completion set). Inferring a universal from a flat proxy is an over-generalization the safe-FN conclusion may SURVIVE, but the stated REASON will be wrong — and the reason is what tells the executor what actually guards the false positive.** Planning the S140 `execute:`-value slice, I wrote (§2.4/§4.3) that `execute:` has "no object-valued child in 1.7.33" — inferred from `CURATED_EXECUTE_KEYS` being flat scalars — and concluded depth-2-under-execute "does not occur legitimately". The MANDATORY §9 review's design-inventory lens grounded the SCHEMA directly (`document-execute.yml`) and found `jupyter`/`julia`/`knitr`/`server` ARE object-valued (`execute:\n  knitr:\n    opts_chunk:\n      fig.width: 7` -> quarto exit 0). The safe-FN held — but because those object children are ABSENT from the curated reader (which carries no `.children`), so PATH-based resolution of any depth-2 execute line returns `undefined` -> skip — NOT because depth-2 can't occur. Stating the wrong guard would have misled the executor about what to protect (and what a future `.children` addition would break); the fix corrected §2.4/§4.3 to the real guard + added an L3 depth-2 `knitr.cache` name-collision regression fixture. Two smaller review catches the SAME session are the same class: a `scalarType` line-cite outside the grepped range, and an L1 dormancy "proof" grep that can never match (the emit site writes `container: currentContainer`, a VARIABLE, not the string literal I grepped for) — each a claim I could have self-verified with one grep before the panel. A sibling of Learning #6 (plan from the implementation, not a description) and #151 (state safety as a verified REACHABILITY, not a universal). | When a plan asserts a safety property as "X cannot occur" / "no Y exists", ground it against the real schema/source, not a curated/completion proxy, and write the guard as "guarded by <verified mechanism>", not an inferred universal. Self-grep your own §5 file:line cites and §-verify commands before the adversarial panel — a cite/verify slip the panel catches is one you should have caught. |
| 154 | **A regression LOCK only locks if its inputs would make the DEFECT fire — assert BOTH sides of a forked invariant, and choose a value the bug actually mis-handles; a lock that passes equally with OR without the bug locks nothing.** Shipping S141's `execute:`-value slice, the central dragon (S140 #1) was that the value enumerator and the unknown-KEY enumerator SHARE a container predicate, so adding `execute` to the shared `PROJECT_CONFIG_CONTAINERS` would make the KEY feature flag unknown execute keys — a cardinal-sin FP (quarto ACCEPTS `custom-thing: whatever`, exit 0). The fix forks a value-ONLY `isValueContainer`/`VALUE_CONTAINERS` used solely in `findProjectConfigValueLines`, leaving the KEY set byte-unchanged. Two lock-design refinements made the regression tests actually bite: (1) **Assert BOTH sides of the fork.** A test that only asserts `findProjectConfigKeyLines("execute:…") === []` passes even if NEITHER enumerator saw execute — it never proves the predicates DIVERGE. The lock ALSO asserts the VALUE enumerator DOES see execute (`.map(v=>v.container) === ["execute","execute"]`), so it turns red the moment someone re-merges the predicates (widening the KEY set OR narrowing the value set). (2) **Pick a value the bug mis-handles for a collision lock.** The depth-2 name-collision safe-FN (`execute.knitr.cache`, resolved BY PATH → `knitr` ∉ `CURATED_EXECUTE_KEYS` → undefined → skip) must be locked with `knitr.cache: banana` — a value the BUGGY bare-name resolution WOULD flag (`banana` ∉ the closed depth-1 `execute.cache` enum). A depth-1-VALID value (`knitr.cache: true`) passes identically under BOTH correct path-based AND buggy bare-name resolution — locking nothing. (3) A "valid" (zero-OUR-diagnostics) fixture's contract is "zero of OUR diagnostics", NOT "renders clean": a deliberate safe-FN line (`knitr.cache: banana` — quarto exit-1, we silent by design) legitimately lives in the valid fixture, documented; the feature's contract ≠ quarto-cleanliness. The mandatory 4-lens §9 review (`wf_208f743b-ced`, 220+ probes) + a 37-case author FP battery both returned 0 cardinal-sin FPs — the clean-panel-after-front-loaded-catch pattern (the plan front-loaded dragon 1 into the KEY-isolation lock; cf. #152). | When writing a regression LOCK for a forked invariant or a path-vs-bare-name resolution: (a) assert BOTH sides so re-merging the fork turns it red — one-sided assertions pass vacuously; (b) choose lock inputs the DEFECT would actually mis-handle (a value invalid for the colliding sibling), not ones both correct and buggy code accept; (c) a "valid"/zero-diagnostics fixture may hold a documented safe-FN line quarto rejects — its contract is your feature's silence, not quarto-cleanliness. |
