| 155 | **A maintenance handoff's "genuinely harder" forward-pointer estimates EFFORT from the feature's SHAPE, not from whether the machinery already exists — ground the REUSED reader/matcher on a SIBLING surface before trusting the estimate; "harder" often means larger FP surface / different offline behavior, not new core logic.** S141's handoff named `format:`-value validation in `_quarto.yml` "genuinely harder (per-format nested keys, reader-derived closedness, top-level scalar-vs-container ambiguity); its own plan." All three flags were real, but firsthand grounding DE-RISKED the slice to the same "surface plumbing" shape as the S141 execute slice, one nesting level deeper: the `.qmd` document surface ALREADY value-validates `format → <fmt> → <option>` via the identical reader path `frontMatterKeys(["format", fmt])` → `perFormatOptions(fmt)` + `isWrongValue` (shipped + integration-tested, `yaml-value-diagnostics.test.ts:240-284`), so the `_quarto.yml` slice is ZERO new reader/matcher/message logic — just a value-side `format` container in `VALUE_CONTAINERS` + a feature resolver branch. The three "harder" aspects each resolved to already-handled (depth-2: the S137 depth-2 enumerator; reader-derived closedness: the reader already derives it, verified) or a deliberate FN (the scalar `format:` name is unvalidated on BOTH surfaces by construction — its enum is injected post-`annotateClosedness`, so `valuesClosed` stays unset). What genuinely differs from execute is narrower than "harder": depth-2 needs a resolver branch resolving via the READER path (NOT `resolveProjectValueField`'s `.children` descent — the format-name fields don't carry the options as children), and format is a safe FN OFFLINE (its `CURATED_FORMAT_OPTIONS` fallback is un-annotated, unlike `CURATED_EXECUTE_KEYS`). TWO firsthand grounding modes cross-checked 1:1 gave the FP-safety proof: `quarto render` 1.7.33 (what quarto ACCEPTS/REJECTS at the schema layer) AND a fresh esbuild-bundled `parseSchemaIndex` harness against CURRENT source (what the READER annotates — `valuesClosed`/`acceptsBoolean`/`scalarType`/`values`). The reader-harness is higher-fidelity than `quarto render` alone for the FP question because it shows the matcher's actual INPUT (e.g. `code-fold → [true,false,show]` proves the anyOf(bool+enum) resolves, so `code-fold: show` won't FP); do NOT trust an inventory sub-agent's claim about a load-bearing annotation — verify it with the compiled reader (and adversarially: the plan-review `Workflow`'s enumerator-reality lens firsthand-confirmed the depth-2 emit I had only structurally argued, by temporarily adding `format` to `VALUE_CONTAINERS` and reverting). | When planning a value-validation (or any reader-driven) slice on a NEW surface: FIRST ground whether the reader+matcher already validate that shape on an EXISTING sibling surface (run its proof test + a compiled-reader harness); if they do, the slice is surface plumbing regardless of a handoff's "harder" framing. Cross-check `quarto render` (accept/reject) against a fresh `parseSchemaIndex` harness (the reader's per-field annotation) 1:1 — that pair is the strongest false-positive-safety evidence, and the harness catches an under-annotation the CLI alone cannot attribute. Verify any load-bearing sub-agent claim firsthand (a temporary reverted probe for enumerator behavior; the compiled reader for annotation). |
| 156 | **When a GO-LIVE edit WIDENS a recognized-set (adds a name to a container/keyword set), the pre-existing tests that used that name as a NEGATIVE ("unknown"/"not recognized") example go RED — and that RED is the go-live SIGNAL, not a regression; reconcile them by swapping the example to a still-negative value, never by deleting the assertion. And the strongest, cheapest L3 pre-check is a compiled feature-SIM over the EXACT fixture files, which proves the precise flag count + spans headlessly before the slow integration run.** Implementing S143's `format:`-value slice (add `"format"` to the value-only `VALUE_CONTAINERS` + a feature resolver branch), the L2 go-live had a subtlety the plan flagged only as "the `:218` RED baseline": TWO shipped unit tests (`project-yaml.test.ts` "skips…unknown containers" and "returns [] …no project/website/book") used a `format:` block as their example of an UNRECOGNIZED value container. The moment `format` joined `VALUE_CONTAINERS`, both went RED (the enumerator now emits the format lines) — exactly the go-live confirmation, distinct from the NEW driver test's RED→GREEN. The correct reconciliation preserves each test's INTENT (skip unknown containers) by swapping `format:` → a still-unrecognized name (`metadata:`), NOT by weakening the assertion to accept the new emit; a commented note records the swap. This is the widen-a-recognized-set dual of Learning #154's fork-lock: adding a member to a positive set silently invalidates negative-example tests keyed on that member's absence — grep the test corpus for the newly-added name BEFORE go-live and pre-identify which negative examples will flip (here also `:34`/`:110`, which test the KEY enumerator and CORRECTLY stay green — format never joins the KEY set). Second, the make-or-break FP-safety proof was a compiled feature-SIM (`esbuild`-bundle `findProjectConfigValueLines` + `parseSchemaIndex` + `isWrongValue`, replicate the feature's exact container-routing) run over the two REAL fixture files: it printed "INVALID → 6 FLAGS at spans […]" and "VALID → 0 FLAGS (15 emitted lines all skip)" BEFORE the ~1-minute integration run, so the integration test confirmed a result already proven, rather than discovering a miscount. The mandatory 4-lens §9 review (`wf_cbfd34f2-147`: fp-cardinal 65-value accepted battery / container-isolation / surface-parity / doc-drift) + the author's 27-value FP battery both returned 0 cardinal-sin FPs — the clean-panel-after-front-loaded-catch pattern continuing (the S142 planning panel front-loaded the FP surface; cf. #152/#154); the panel's doc-drift lens enumerated the exact BACKLOG/POSIT-COMPARISON lines to reconcile at close-out (Learning #10), and its §5-citation audit confirmed every UNCHANGED-reader-file anchor still exact = gate (a) held. | (1) Before a go-live that ADDS a member to a recognized container/keyword/enum SET, grep the whole test corpus for that member name and pre-identify every NEGATIVE-example test keyed on its absence — expect them to go RED at go-live (that RED is the signal), and reconcile by swapping the example to a still-negative value with a note, never by weakening the assertion. Distinguish which sibling tests CORRECTLY stay green (a different set/predicate). (2) For any diagnostics slice, build a compiled feature-SIM (bundle the enumerator + reader + matcher, replicate the feature's routing) and run it over the ACTUAL fixture files to prove the exact flag count + spans headlessly BEFORE the real-host integration run — the integration test should confirm a known result, not discover the count. |
| 157 | **When you build a validator that MIMICS a tool's behavior, ground its rule against the exact LAYER whose output you reproduce — a tool often has multiple layers that DISAGREE, and modeling on the wrong one ships cardinal-sin false positives that firsthand grounding of the wrong layer will "confirm."** Planning S144's format-NAME validation (flag `format: banana` matching `quarto render`), I extracted quarto's acceptance rule from `parseFormatString`/`breakFormatString` (quarto.js:41317/41365) and built a two-path predicate (base-token + last-hyphen-suffix). It passed my firsthand `quarto render` battery — but `parseFormatString` is the RENDER-DISPATCH layer, reached only AFTER schema validation; the layer that emits "Validation of YAML front matter failed" (what the extension actually mimics, like every prior slice's `readAndValidateYamlFromFile`) is a DIFFERENT function, `makeFrontMatterFormatSchema` (quarto.js:16697-16748), an anyOf of per-name regexes `^(.+-)?<name>([-+].+)?$` + `^.+\.lua$` — strictly more permissive and DISAGREEING with parseFormatString (parseFormatString accepts `foo-bar`; the schema rejects it). The two-path predicate shipped 2 cardinal-sin FP classes into the plan draft: (A) custom `.lua` writers (`format: my-writer.lua` — schema-ACCEPT via `^.+\.lua$`), (B) `<ext>-<builtin>-<mod>` where the built-in sits mid-name (`foo-html-smart`, `nature-pdf-draft` — the regex accepts a built-in bounded by `-`/`+` ANYWHERE, but my predicate only inspected the first token + the last-hyphen suffix). The MANDATORY 4-lens §9 review (`wf_fc737cbf-672`) caught it — 3 of 4 lenses independently REFUTED the predicate with firsthand `quarto render` evidence, converging on the one root cause — and PROVED the fix (mirror the schema regex union: `/^.+\.lua$/.test(name) || [...B].some(b => new RegExp(`^(.+-)?${esc(b)}([-+].+)?$`).test(name))`, B = raw pandoc/formats.yml 67 + {md,hugo,dashboard,email} = 71), which I firsthand-re-verified → 0 divergences over the 32-case matrix + the 51-case review battery. Why two firsthand passes missed it: my modifier probe used the `+`-delimited `foo-html+smart` (works under BOTH predicates because `lastIndexOf('-')` still precedes the base), never the `-`-delimited `foo-html-smart`; and I never probed `.lua` at all — I grounded the CASES my (wrong) mental model predicted, not the cases that would DISTINGUISH the layers. The review also caught that my "`[html, pdf]` is a valid multi-format list" claim contradicted my OWN GROUNDING.md (which recorded `[html, banana]` → SCHEMA-REJECT; the flow-list form is schema-invalid — the valid multi-format syntax is the MAPPING form), and that my FN-lock inventory undercounted the breaking tests by one (a hard `strictEqual(diags.length, 5)` in addition to the `!includes(6)` I'd named). This is the THIRD consecutive family cluster (cf. #151, #153) where the §9 panel beat two thorough firsthand passes on a cardinal-sin-risk claim — the panel is not ceremony; it is the control that catches the mis-grounding an author's own model blinds them to. Sibling of #153 (ground the safety claim against the real schema, not a proxy) applied to LAYER selection rather than artifact selection. | When building a validator that mimics a tool: (1) identify the EXACT layer whose output you reproduce (here: the schema validator emitting the user-visible error, NOT the downstream dispatch/execution layer) and ground the RULE against THAT layer's source — greping a plausible-looking function is not enough when the tool has multiple layers that disagree. (2) Design the grounding battery to DISTINGUISH candidate rules, not merely confirm the one you already believe (probe `-`-delimited AND `+`-delimited; probe the classes your model says "can't happen" like `.lua`) — a battery that only exercises cases your model predicts will "pass" a wrong model. (3) Run the mandatory §9 adversarial panel even after thorough author grounding, and firsthand-verify each finding + the proposed fix before folding — three sessions running, the panel caught a cardinal-sin the author's firsthand passes missed. (4) Self-grep your own draft for internal contradictions (a claim vs your own grounding file) before the panel (Learning #7). |
| 158 | **When you mirror a validator whose regex is BUILT FROM A STRING (`regexSchema("…")` / `new RegExp("…")`), copy the COMPILED pattern's semantics — not the source string's apparent intent: a `\.` written in the source is NOT a literal dot in the compiled regex, because the STRING escape collapses `\.` to a bare `.` (a wildcard) before RegExp ever sees it.** S144's §9 review had already corrected the format-NAME predicate to mirror the SCHEMA layer `makeFrontMatterFormatSchema` (Learning #157) — an `anyOf` of `^(.+-)?<name>([-+].+)?$` + `^.+\.lua$`. Implementing it (S145), I copied the plan's `.lua` rule verbatim as the regex LITERAL `/^.+\.lua$/` (a real literal dot). But quarto's actual schema is `regexSchema("^.+\.lua$")` — the argument is a STRING, and in a JS string literal `\.` is not a defined escape, so it evaluates to `.`; the value handed to `new RegExp` is `^.+.lua$`, whose dot is a WILDCARD. Quarto therefore ACCEPTS any name of ≥2 chars followed by `lua` (`foolua`, `aalua`, `abclua`, `htmlua`), NOT only a literal `.lua`. My literal-dot predicate flagged every one of them → a cardinal-sin FALSE POSITIVE. My L1 unit battery AND both feature-sims missed it because I only ever tested `.lua` names WITH a dot (`my-writer.lua`, `foo.lua`) — never a dot-less `Xlua`. The MANDATORY 4-lens §9 review (`wf_f9e2f6f5-ae4`) caught it: the predicate-parity lens built its reference from the SAME string-literal semantics quarto uses (explicitly "NOT a regex literal"), fuzzed dot-less lua names, and grounded on real `quarto render` — `foolua`/`aalua` pass the schema layer (fail only later with a non-schema "Unknown format"), while `lua`/`plua`/`alua`/`Xlua` (≤1 char before `lua`) are schema-REJECTED, pinning the boundary at exactly `^.+.lua$` (≥2 chars). I firsthand-re-verified every case (12 quarto renders at the boundary), fixed the regex to the wildcard `/^.+.lua$/` RED→GREEN (6 dot-less cases), and ran a 50-name render-vs-predicate battery → **0 cardinal FPs, 0 divergences**. (Two of the 4 lenses failed on a StructuredOutput retry cap; I discharged fp-cardinal firsthand via that 50-name battery and doc-drift via a whole-corpus grep.) This is the FOURTH consecutive family cluster (cf. #151/#153/#157) where the §9 panel beat thorough firsthand author passes on a cardinal-sin — and the sharpest: it beat #157's OWN correction, which fixed the LAYER but still transcribed that layer's regex-SOURCE dot as a literal. Sibling of #157 (right layer) at one level deeper: right layer, right regex SOURCE-vs-COMPILED semantics. | When mirroring a tool's regex-based validation: (1) if the tool builds its regex from a STRING (`regexSchema`/`new RegExp("…")`), mentally (or actually) run the string through JS string-escape collapse FIRST — `\.`→`.`, `\d` stays, etc. — and mirror the RESULTING compiled pattern, not the source text; a `\.` in a `new RegExp("…")` string is a wildcard, in a `/…/` literal it is a literal dot. (2) Fuzz the predicate against inputs that OMIT the delimiter you assumed mandatory (a dot-less `foolua`, not just `foo.lua`) — the FP lives exactly where the assumed-required character is absent. (3) Keep running the mandatory §9 panel even after a PRIOR §9 correction — a fix at layer N can still be wrong at layer N+1; four sessions running, the panel caught the cardinal-sin the author's firsthand passes missed. |
| 159 | **A battery's SIZE is not its COVERAGE — the axis you hold fixed is the axis you cannot audit; and to find where a mimicked tool disagrees with you, diff against the tool's OWN error text, not just its exit code.** Planning the `_quarto.yml` document-key value slice (S146), I built a 936-shape false-positive battery (13 fields × 72 exotic YAML value tokens) and rendered all 614 shapes the planned code would flag: 614/614 genuinely rejected, 0 FPs — which felt like proof. It was not. Every shape varied the **value** and held the **key and separator** fixed, so an entire FP class was structurally unreachable no matter how large the sweep got: `toc:: true` is quarto-VALID (its key is `toc:`, unknown-but-accepted on an OPEN key set → exit 0) while every enumerator here splits at `indexOf(":")` and flags the bogus token `: true` — live today on the shipped `.qmd` and `execute:` surfaces. The mandatory §9 panel found it by varying the axis I had frozen. Separately, the technique that DID find a defect on my own was diffing the reader's derived enum against **quarto's own `which must instead be …` clause** parsed out of all 170 rejections: three fields list `null` as valid that our `values` omits (`valuesOfSchema` drops a literal `null` enum member while `closednessOfSchema` still marks the field CLOSED), so `auto-play-media: null` is flagged though quarto accepts it — an FP live on shipped code that four prior sessions in this family never saw, because an exit-code-only battery can only confirm "the values I thought of are rejected", never "the values the tool accepts are all in my set". **Apply:** (1) before trusting a battery, list the axes of the input space (key, separator, value, line shape, document shape) and confirm each is varied — a fixed axis is an unaudited axis, and state which axes a sweep covered when you report its size; (2) when mimicking a tool that emits a *reason*, parse that reason and diff it against your model's expectations — it converts a one-directional check (does it reject what I flag?) into a two-directional one (does it accept anything I flag?); (3) when reusing an existing guard "verbatim" on a NEW position, re-derive whether its assumptions still hold there — reusing `scanFlow`'s whole-token arming at column 0 would have made an ordinary `title: Don't Panic` swallow the rest of the file, because at column 0 the blast radius of a mis-armed continuation guard is the whole document, not one container. |
| 160 | **A derived bit that LOOSENS a check is not a copy of the sibling bit that TIGHTENS one — the fold operator inverts, and the completeness standard is parity with the GATE that reads the bit, not with the data format.** Shipping the null-arm fix (S147, prerequisite P of the S146 plan), the new `acceptsNull` sat beside three existing annotators (`valuesClosed`, `numericMemberEnum`, `scalarType`) that all prove a RESTRICTION, so the obvious move was to mirror one arm-for-arm. Two things do not carry over. (a) **The `anyOf` fold inverts.** The siblings fold with AND (every arm must be closed / numeric); an ADMISSION folds with OR — one null-admitting arm makes the whole field admit null. Copying the sibling's AND would have left the field flagging a value quarto accepts, i.e. reproduced the exact cardinal-sin FP the slice existed to remove. Same for the risk polarity: over-setting `acceptsNull` is a safe false negative, while a MISSED arm is the false positive — the opposite of `valuesClosed`, where over-setting is the danger. (b) **"Walk every arm" needs a target.** The mandatory §9 review found two null-bearing shapes the walk does NOT handle (a bare `null` `anyOf` member, `epub-subdirectory`; and the `{null:{…}}` type wrapper, `out-width`) and correctly returned CLEAN anyway — because the bit is only ever READ behind the `valuesClosed` gate, and `closednessOfSchema` returns OPEN for both shapes, so the matcher returns before consulting it. The right completeness standard was therefore parity with the **gate's own walk**, not with the DSL's whole null vocabulary. I verified that firsthand rather than accepting it, including the adversarial variants where such an arm sits beside only-closed arms (the sole way the claim could fail), and recorded the reasoning in the function's doc comment so the next reviewer does not re-litigate it. **Corollary that cost a real count:** an indirection arm you do not traverse silently turns an "exhaustive" scan into a lower bound. S146's `nullscan.ts` reported "exactly 3 fields in all of Quarto 1.7.33" because its walk tested `node.ref` as an object when the DSL makes it a **string** — so it never resolved into `definitions.yml` and missed a 4th null-admitting name (`output-file`). The miss was invisible precisely because the 4th resolves OPEN and so was never a false positive; nothing downstream disagreed. | When adding a derived annotation bit to a family of existing ones: first ask whether it RESTRICTS or ADMITS, and re-derive every fold operator and the risk polarity from that answer instead of copying a sibling. Then set the completeness target from the gate that READS the bit, not from the raw schema. And whenever a scan's output is a COUNT used as a blast radius, prove its arm coverage against the consumer's own walk — especially every indirection arm (`ref`/`$ref`/include), whose type in the DSL (string vs object) is exactly what a hand-written walk gets wrong. |
| 161 | **A grammar rule that is correct for DIAGNOSTICS can be wrong for COMPLETION — and a "judge this token" predicate is not the same as a "find the right token" scan. Both mistakes hide behind a passing suite.** Shipping the key/value-separator fix (S148, prerequisite P2 of the S146 plan), I made the same category error twice. (a) I put the separator guard inside `topLevelSlots`, the grammar SHARED by the value enumerators and the completion providers. For diagnostics `key:value` is not a mapping, so never flag it; but for completion it is a user MID-TYPING, and `providers/yaml.ts` deliberately offers the value with a prepended space to repair it. Two integration tests caught the nested half — the top-level half had NO test and would have shipped silently. (b) The guard asked `isMappingSeparator(line, indexOf(":"))` — is the FIRST colon a separator? — when the real question is WHERE the separator colon is. On `a:b: "text` a LATER colon is the separator, so the line IS a mapping whose value opens a multi-line scalar; treating it as a non-mapping skipped the `scanFlow` arming and flagged the folded continuation line on a document `quarto render` exits 0 on. I had probed the arming question and convinced myself it was "structurally safe" — but only tested lines where NO colon is a separator (which quarto rejects), never the later-colon case that makes the document VALID. The mandatory §9 review found it from two independent lenses; independent skeptics confirmed rather than refuted. **When to apply:** (1) before putting a rule in a shared grammar helper, enumerate its consumers and ask whether they want the SAME answer — a diagnostic and a completion routinely do not, and the completion side is the one with thin test coverage; (2) prefer "find the right token" (`mappingColonAt`) over "judge this token" whenever the token's position is itself derived — a predicate over a guessed index silently inherits the guess; (3) when you reason your way to "this consequence is safe", write down the case class your probes actually covered, then ask which class you did NOT construct. My safety argument was true for the shapes I built and false for the ones I did not. |
| 162 | **A fix that works by ROUTING something through a shared path only holds for inputs that REACH that path — so when you write the premise into a comment ("X is now handled by the same tail as everything else"), the very next move is to enumerate the early exits ahead of it and construct the input that takes one.** Shipping the column-0 document-key slice (S149, ③ of the S146 plan), the whole design was "route column-0 scalars through the SHARED emission tail, which also arms the multi-line continuation guard for them" — and that is genuinely what fixed the three measured Defect-B false positives. I wrote the premise into the enumerator's doc comment in exactly those words. But the arming sits at the END of the loop body, behind **five** scope `continue`s (a line outside any tracked container, a depth-3+ line, a block-sequence item, a line with no open child scope, a dedent). A multi-line value opened on any of those lines arms nothing — and once column-0 lines are *emitted* rather than skipped, its folded continuation becomes a flagged "document key" on a file `quarto render` renders **exit 0**. `custom:\n  note: "multi\ntoc: banana\n  end"` is one YAML string; we squiggled its interior. A NEW cardinal-sin false positive, introduced by the very commit that claimed to remove that class, found by the mandatory §9 review and not by me. **Why my own grounding could not see it: 82 render-grounded cases, and every single fold case opened the multi-line value on a line the enumerator EMITS.** That axis — *emitting vs. skipped line* — was held fixed across the whole battery, which is Learning #159 recurring for the third consecutive session in this family (S146 never varied the separator, S148 never varied the cell-option surface, S149 never varied the skip path), each time in a session that had read the previous one. There is a second, sharper edge: the arming token is not always "the value after the separator". A block-**sequence** item whose own scalar is quoted (`- "intro.qmd`) has no separator colon at all, so S148's proof — "no colon anywhere ⇒ the line hosts no value ⇒ nothing could have opened a multi-line scalar", correct for a *mapping* line — silently does not apply to it, because a sequence item's value **is** the item. **When to apply:** (a) whenever a fix's mechanism is "it now goes through the shared path", grep that path for every `return`/`continue`/`break` above the behavior you are relying on and write one test per exit — the shared-path claim is a *reachability* claim, and reachability is exactly what a comment cannot prove; (b) when inheriting a predecessor's safety proof, re-derive it for the shapes the predecessor's surface did not contain, since a proof is scoped to the input class its author had in mind; (c) when designing the battery, list the axes explicitly and ask which one every case shares — that shared axis is the blind spot, and its size does not shrink it. |
| 163 | **A ledger that is complete is not the same as a file that is clean — and the cleanup is where the losses hide.** `BACKLOG.md` had accumulated **115 KB of completed work (46% of the file, 35 top-level `[x]` blocks + 41 nested)** across ~150 sessions, despite its own header rule and SESSION_RUNNER Phase 3F both saying completed items are removed in the commit that records them. The ledger was in good shape — 34 of 35 blocks were fully corroborated in `CHANGELOG.md` — so the naive read was "this is a safe bulk delete". It was not. **Three distinct classes of live content were embedded inside blocks marked `[x]`,** and each needed a different lens to see: **(1)** genuinely-open `- [ ]` sub-items nested under a completed parent (2 found — a structural scan finds these); **(2)** open work written as *prose sub-bullets* with no checkbox at all (2 found — the Marketplace publish prerequisites, and a standing "unproven against a real Python formatter" verification gap — invisible to a checkbox scan, found only by grepping completed blocks for forward-looking markers like *Operator step* / *remains UNPROVEN* / *not an agent task*); and **(3)** one genuine **failure-mode-#27 ledger gap** — Session 36's b2-iii planning deliverable (commit `6223e15`) had no `CHANGELOG.md` entry at all, so the block scheduled for deletion was the last surviving record of a real deliverable. Deleting first and checking later would have destroyed all three. **The second-order effect is bigger than the first:** removing the content invalidated **194 positional citations** to it (`BACKLOG:NNN`, `BACKLOG item N`, and two more spellings) spread across 70 files including shipped `src/` comments — and that convention was *already* broken before the cleanup (12 of 34 distinct cited line numbers landed on blank or unrelated lines; one had been wrong the day it was written). A line number is worse than a broken link: an insertion above it silently **retargets** it at a different item, so it keeps looking valid. | When removing or condensing any accumulated document: (a) run BOTH a structural lens (checkboxes, headings) and a *forward-looking-content* lens (grep the doomed regions for operator instructions, standing constraints, known limitations, unproven claims) — the structural lens cannot see prose; (b) verify each region against its durable destination BEFORE deleting, and treat a missing destination as a ledger gap to backfill, not a reason to keep the file dirty; (c) immediately grep the WHOLE corpus for positional references to what you removed (Learning #10), and resolve each one from git history (`git blame` the citing line -> `git show <sha>:FILE`) rather than guessing; (d) rewrite citations to *name* their target, and point at the ledger — not at a title in the file you just emptied, which is a fresh dangling reference; (e) make the replacement text quote- and backtick-free, because citations live inside `describe(...)`/`it(...)` string literals and template literals as well as in comments — the quoted form broke 5 test files and had to be reverted and redone. |
| 164 | **When a filed bug names ONE site but the root cause is a SHARED helper, grep the helper's consumers FIRST and ground each — the named site count is a lower bound, and doing this pre-emptively turns the mandatory review from corrective into confirmatory.** P3 (the escape-decoding FP, S151) was filed as a `~1-line` fix to `isWrongValue`, because the FP was first observed there. But the actual root cause is the shared `unquote` (which returns raw text with no YAML escape decoding), and `unquote` has TWO live consumers: `isWrongValue`'s enum membership and the `.qmd` format-scalar path's `isKnownFormatName`. Both carried the identical false positive (`toc-location: "\x62ody"` and `format: "\x68tml"` each decode to a valid value quarto renders exit 0, yet both were flagged). This is the exact "the plan named TWO sites and there were FOUR" shape of P2/S148 (Learning #161) — the family's single most-repeated blind spot is that a named site count undercounts. I caught site B *before writing code* by treating the deliverable as a DEFECT CLASS and running `grep -rn "unquote" src` to enumerate every consumer, then reproducing each firsthand against both `quarto render` and the real installed schema (a `tsx` probe that ran the actual predicate on the escape token). Consequence: the MANDATORY §9 review came back CLEAN across all four lenses — the FIRST time in this family the panel found nothing author-missed in 8 consecutive clusters — because the two things a suppression fix can get wrong (a missed sibling call site; over-suppression) had both been grounded pre-emptively rather than discovered by the panel. **Two guards against over-reading a clean panel:** (a) a clean result is only trustworthy if it is NON-VACUOUS — confirm the lenses did real, independent work (here the numeric lens independently reran the unit suite to the same 1293; the re-grounding lens reproduced every quarto verdict from scratch), not a hollow all-clear; (b) a clean panel can still share the author's blind spot when the lenses reach for the same instruments the author used — so deliberately give at least one lens an independent reproduction path and treat "no finding" as "no finding by these instruments", not "provably correct". **Second, on the fix shape:** a suppression guard justified by an FN-ONLY invariant ("a valid member never contains the skipped character") should be made deliberately BROAD and invariant-shaped, not narrowly targeted at the observed bad input — `rawToken.includes("\\")` skips every backslash token (escape-form-agnostic: all YAML double-quote escapes start with a backslash, so it is complete), rather than an `\x`-specific regex that would silently miss `\u`/`\U`/`\t`. Narrowing a safety guard to the reproduction case is how the next escape form reopens the FP. | When implementing a filed bug fix: (1) if the root cause is a shared helper/grammar/predicate, `grep` its consumers and ground each firsthand BEFORE coding — the filing's site count is a lower bound (cf. Learning #161); fixing all instances of one defect class is completing one deliverable, not scope creep (FM #26 is about two DIFFERENT capabilities). (2) Shape a false-negative-only suppression guard around its INVARIANT, broadly, not around the observed input — and prove the invariant holds across every branch the guard short-circuits. (3) When the mandatory adversarial review returns clean, verify it is non-vacuous (independent tool calls, independently-derived numbers) and give at least one lens a reproduction path that does not reuse your own instruments — a clean panel confirms; it does not prove. |
| 165 | **A change that CLOSES a documented divergence has an inverted doc-to-code ratio, and the staleness it creates lives entirely OUTSIDE the diff.** Combo 3 (S152) was a ~15-line branch closing the last top-level-scalar divergence between the `.qmd` and `_quarto.yml` format-NAME surfaces. The code was trivial and clean; the disproportionate work was everything the closure made stale — and NONE of it was in the diff, because those places DESCRIBE the old divergent state: a live comparison doc (`docs/POSIT-COMPARISON.md`, 4 spots asserting "377 of 378 … `format:` the one divergence" and "the `_quarto.yml` scalar `format:` NAME … deferred"), a unit-test COMMENT that both mislabelled a sibling case (`html: default`, the container-key form) and called Combo 3 "deferred", and 3 planning snapshots carrying "deliberate FN on both surfaces" / "the ONE divergence" as standing facts. A diff-scoped review sees none of them; only a whole-corpus doc-completeness lens (Learning #10) does. Separately, closing ONE arm of a mirror-pair made the still-open arm newly conspicuous: the §9 missed-sites lens found the identical `format:` NAME in a directory-level `_metadata.yml` is validated by quarto with the SAME schema but never flagged (the whole `_quarto.yml` project-value feature gates on the `_quarto.yml`/`_quarto.yaml` basenames only) — a PRE-EXISTING safe-FN gap Combo 3 did not create but made visible; filed not fixed (widening the gate is its own slice — `_metadata.yml` carries document-level, not `project:`/`website:`/`book:`, metadata). Two more transferables: (a) a predecessor's forward-looking "when you build X, also carry guard Y" warning about a shared-helper defect class (here: carry the P3 backslash guard onto the new `unquote` consumer) is a HARD requirement, not advice — the §9 over-suppression lens AND a firsthand 37-shape FP battery both confirmed `format: "\x68tml"` (decodes to html, quarto exit 0) would have been a cardinal-sin FP without it; this is Learning #164's defect-class rule in the FORWARD direction (adding the Nth consumer, not finding the Nth site). (b) When a review-workflow reports `agents_error > 0`, the aggregated survivor list silently drops the failed slot — recover the unverified finding from the run's `journal.jsonl` (one `{"type":"result"}` line per agent) and adjudicate it firsthand; here one verifier hit its structured-output retry cap and its finding (BACKLOG.md staleness — a required Phase 3F action anyway) would otherwise have been invisible. | On any change that CLOSES a documented divergence, gap, or "deliberate FN": (1) budget for doc-drift that exceeds the code and lives outside the diff — grep the WHOLE corpus (comparison docs, code comments, plan snapshots, count/parity claims like "N of M") for statements of the OLD state, fix the LIVE ones, and leave append-only dated ledgers (`PROJECT_LEARNINGS.md`, `CHANGELOG.md`) untouched; (2) run a missed-sites lens for the MIRROR surface the closure now makes conspicuous, and file it if it is a separate slice; (3) treat a predecessor's "carry guard Y onto surface X" warning as mandatory and prove the guard load-bearing firsthand; (4) if a review workflow errors any agent, read its journal and adjudicate the dropped finding yourself. |
| 166 | **Narrowing a heuristic is provably false-positive-free when the new predicate is a strict SUBSET of the old on the FP-risk axis — and porting an already-shipped-and-reviewed reference into sibling code makes that the whole safety argument.** S153 brought the two `.qmd` front-matter value enumerators (`findFrontMatterValueLines`, `findNestedFrontMatterValueLines`) to the arming discipline `findProjectConfigValueLines` already shipped: arm the multi-line continuation guard from EVERY scalar line (not only emitted ones — fixes a cardinal-sin FP where a folded key was flagged on a doc quarto renders exit 0) and NARROW the arm to a token whose first char (past a stripped node property `&anchor `/`!tag `) opens `"'[{` instead of `scanFlow`-ing the whole token (fixes a phantom-quote FN where `title: Don't Panic`'s apostrophe swallowed the block). The review came back CLEAN on the two files because the narrowed arm calls the SAME `scanFlow` gated only by the first char, so it is a strict subset of the old arm — it can NEVER arm a line the old code left unarmed on the same token, so the only behavior changes are FEWER phantom arms (the FN fix) and arming from previously-skipped lines (the FP fix); no new FP path can exist. The `"'[{` gate is also provably COMPLETE for YAML: a value that folds a following `key: value` line MUST start with a quote or flow bracket (a plain scalar is terminated by `: `; block-scalar content must be more-indented), verified firsthand by the §9 over-suppression lens. Two more transferables: (a) **the defect class ⊇ the named sites — grep the whole class, then scope PER SITE.** The recurring "count is a lower bound" theme (Learnings #161/#164/#165) held AGAIN: the backlog named TWO enumerators but the phantom-quote arm (Defect A) spanned THREE — `findCellOptionLines` (the `#|` cell-option enumerator, `src/core/qmd/model.ts`) arms `scanFlow(m[4],0,null)` over the whole token too, so `#| fig-cap: Don't do this` swallows a later `#| echo: banana` (quarto exit 1) — realistic, captions carry apostrophes. But this time the third site was legitimately OUT of scope (a different surface — `#|` lines folded within a code cell, not `---` front matter — with only Defect A, not the emit-only Defect B), so the discipline is grep the class then FILE-or-FIX per site (FM #26), not reflexively fix all. (b) **Prove FP/FN pins non-vacuous by running the OLD code**: the re-grounding lens extracted the pre-fix enumerators via `git show <fix>^:src/core/…` and ran them through `npx tsx`, confirming they produce the WRONG output for exactly the pinned shapes — the strongest form of "this test would have failed before," stronger than reasoning about it. | When narrowing any suppression/arming heuristic: prove the new predicate ⊆ the old on the FP-risk axis (then only the safe FN direction can change) and state that as the review's safety argument; when the change is a port of a shipped+reviewed reference, cite the reference and diff against it. When a filed item names N sites of a defect, grep the whole defect CLASS first (here: every `scanFlow(…,0,null)` arming site), then decide fix-or-file per site by scope (FM #26), don't assume N is complete. To prove a regression pin non-vacuous, extract and run the pre-fix code (`git show <sha>^:<path>` → tsx), don't just argue it. |
| 167 | **Porting a reviewed reference's helper does NOT port the review — ground the helper's ADVERSARIAL corners on the NEW surface, and adjudicate a fold-FP with the CLOSED form.** S154 fixed the phantom-quote FN on the THIRD/last arming site (`findCellOptionLines`, `src/core/qmd/model.ts`) by narrowing the continuation-guard arm to a value-token first-char opener (`"'[{`) past a stripped node property — the mirror of S153's front-matter fix, and the same completeness argument (#166) makes it FP-free: a cell-option value that folds a following `#|` mapping line MUST start with a quote or flow bracket. Two transferables the §9 review surfaced. **(a) The reference's node-property strip regex `/^(?:[&!][^\s]*[ \t]+)+/` carried a latent bug the port inherited:** its greedy `[^\s]*` swallows a flow bracket that ABUTS the anchor (`&a[one,`) and its REQUIRED trailing whitespace then fails to match, so nothing is stripped, the opener is seen as `&`, the arm never fires, and the folded continuation is flagged — on a doc quarto renders **exit 0** (`fig-cap` takes a list, so js-yaml/quarto accept an anchor abutting `[`). The OLD whole-token arm avoided this; the narrowing REGRESSED it into a cardinal-sin FP. Fix: exclude flow indicators/quotes from the name charset and make the trailing ws optional (`/^(?:[&!][^\s[\]{}"']*[ \t]*)+/`) — a strict superset of the old strip (arms MORE = the FN-safe direction). Lesson: when you port a shipped+reviewed helper (here the strip regex, reviewed clean in S153 only against the SPACED `&a [` form), the review does NOT transfer — re-ground the helper's own adversarial corners (the abutting/no-space form) on the new surface, or you inherit a latent defect the reference never exercised. The same weaker regex is latent in the 3 S153 reference enumerators (a list-accepting closed key would surface it there); filed, its own cross-module slice. **(b) Adjudicate a fold/continuation FP with the CLOSED form.** Firsthand-refuting the `&a[one,` finding, I first used the UNTERMINATED `&a[one,` + `echo: banana` (no closing `]`) → quarto exit 1 (`unexpected end of stream within a flow collection`) and nearly dismissed a REAL FP as "doc rejected anyway." The TERMINATED `&a[one,` + `echo: banana]` renders exit 0 and IS the FP. An open fold fails to parse for an unrelated reason; only the closed fold reproduces the exit-0 doc the FP requires — test the closed form. (c) A §9 review that finds ONE real in-scope defect is the norm, not the clean-panel exception (#166/S151): a value-token-scoped narrowing has a corner a whole-line narrowing doesn't, and the adversarial branch-interaction lens is what caught it. | S154 §9 review (`wf_7456a5f0-85a`, 4 lenses + adversarial verify, `agents_error:0`), which caught the abutting-anchor regression FP the author's initial port missed | When porting a shipped+reviewed helper/predicate into a new call site or surface: re-ground its adversarial corner cases on the new surface (do not assume the reference's review covered them). When firsthand-adjudicating a fold/continuation false positive, use the CLOSED (terminated) form so quarto actually renders the doc — an unterminated form fails to parse for an unrelated reason and masks the FP. Grep the defect class (here the shared strip regex across all 4 arming sites) and fix-or-file per site by scope (FM #26). |
| 168 | **A predecessor's "latent/safe" tag is a lower bound to disprove, and a suppression/fold finding must be grounded with a NULL-TOLERANT context key or it hides itself.** S154 filed the abutting-anchor strip under-arm (`&a[one,`) as LATENT on the 3 front-matter/project value enumerators ("needs a list-accepting closed-enum key; `title:` renders exit 1"). It was NOT latent: `keywords`/`resources`/`fig-cap` ARE list-accepting keys, so `keywords: &a[one,` / `df-print: banana]` folds to `keywords: [one, {df-print: banana}]` and quarto 1.7.33 renders it **exit 0** — a LIVE cardinal-sin FP on all three surfaces (the folded closed-enum key flagged on a doc quarto accepts). The fix: hunt the key/shape that surfaces a "latent" finding before scoping (cf #161/#164/#165 — a named characterization is a lower bound). Three transferables. **(a) Porting a reviewed helper does NOT port the review — #167 applied recursively to me.** I ported S154's hardened strip charset `[^\s[\]{}"']` verbatim; the mandatory §9 review then found IT over-excludes quotes. YAML's c-flow-indicator (the chars an anchor NAME may not contain) is ONLY `,[]{}` — a QUOTE is a legal anchor-name char. So `myref: &a'b` (a node named `a'b` with a null value quarto ACCEPTS, exit 0) had its `'b` misread as a quote opener, arming a phantom fold that SWALLOWED a following real key quarto rejects (exit 1, sole error) — an over-suppression false-NEGATIVE regression. The correct charset is `[^\s,[\]{}]` (exclude only `,[]{}`, KEEP quotes). #167's own lesson (re-ground a ported helper's adversarial corners) applied to the very charset #167 shipped — the strip regex is now correct at the 3 value enumerators and FILED on the cell-option site it came from. **(b) Ground a suppression/fold finding with a NULL-TOLERANT context key, or the ground truth hides.** Firsthand-refuting the over-suppression, my FIRST attempt used `keywords: &a'b` — but `keywords` REQUIRES a list, so `keywords: null` itself makes quarto exit 1, and I wrongly concluded "safe FN on an already-broken doc." The §9 verifier used `myref: &a'b` (an UNKNOWN, null-tolerant key): `myref: null` is accepted (exit 0), so the ONLY error is the following key — proving the suppression drops a real TRUE POSITIVE. The KIND of anchor-line key decides whether a suppression is a safe FN (key rejects null → doc already exit-1) or a lost TP (key tolerates null → doc otherwise exit-0). This is #167's "adjudicate a fold FP with the CLOSED form," sharpened for a SUPPRESSION finding: choose the surrounding context so the document is otherwise VALID. **(c) The author's own first firsthand refutation can be wrong; verify the verifier's counter-shape, don't dismiss it.** The over-suppression would have shipped as a silent regression had I trusted my (wrong-key) refutation instead of re-grounding the verifier's `myref` shape. | S155 §9 review (`wf_d4d42586-d66`, 5 lenses + adversarial verify, `agents_error:0`, `agents_empty_result:0`), whose over-suppression lens caught the quote-exclusion regression the author's port inherited, and whose author's first firsthand refutation used the wrong (list-requiring) key | When a predecessor tags a defect "latent/safe/rare": treat it as a hypothesis and hunt the key/shape that surfaces it before scoping. When porting a reviewed regex/predicate: re-ground ITS adversarial corners against the spec (here YAML c-flow-indicator = `,[]{}` only), not by copying. When firsthand-adjudicating a suppression/fold finding: choose the context key so the surrounding document is otherwise VALID (null-tolerant/open key) — a self-erroring key masks the true positive the suppression drops. |
| 169 | **When a fix corrects ONE decision path of a defect class, ground the SIBLING paths that decide the SAME thing — a strip/normalization applied to one arm rarely covers a parallel arm.** `findCellOptionLines` decides "does this cell-option value OPEN a multi-line fold?" in TWO places: the SINGLE-LINE arm (strip a node property, test the first char — `model.ts:601`) and the MULTI-LINE CONTINUATION of an already-open flow (`scanFlow(m[4], …)` at `~:547`). S156 corrected the arm's strip charset so a quote in an anchor NAME (`&a'b`) no longer phantom-arms — but the continuation path re-scans the whole token with a node-property-BLIND `scanFlow`, so the SAME anchor-name-quote defect survives when the value is a CONTINUATION (`#\| fig-cap: [` / `#\| one, &a'b` / `#\| ]` / `#\| echo: banana` → echo swallowed, a lost TP quarto flags exit 1). The §9 missed-sites lens caught it; I grounded it firsthand and FILED it (pre-existing, out of scope — FM #26). **Two transferables. (a) Enumerate every code path that makes the same decision for the same class and ground each** — the sibling path is invisible to a diff-scoped review and to a review that only exercises the changed line; grep the class (`scanFlow` / the arm predicate), not just the edited call site. This is Learning #166's "the defect class outran its named sites" applied to PARALLEL decision paths within ONE function, not sibling functions. **(b) An "out of scope" residual comment must ground WHICH shapes are benign vs which drop real TPs.** My first comment cited only the BENIGN single-line residual (`&a'b [1,`, which quarto REJECTS — no TP lost) and implied the whole residual was harmless; the review found the real lost-TP class on the continuation path the comment omitted. An honest deferral distinguishes the benign curiosity from the real-but-deferred defect, or it misleads the next session into thinking the residual is a non-issue. | S156 §9 review (`wf_06a97818-8e0`, 5 lenses + adversarial verify, `agents_error:0`, `agents_skipped:0`, `agents_empty_result:4`), whose missed-sites lens found the continuation-path node-property-blind `scanFlow` lost TP that the single-line-arm strip fix did not cover, and flagged the fix's own comment for citing only the benign single-line residual | When a fix corrects a defect class on one decision path: enumerate every code path in the same function (and siblings) that makes the SAME decision for the SAME class, and ground each firsthand — a normalization/strip on one arm does not transfer to a parallel arm that re-derives the decision differently. When writing an "out of scope / residual" comment: ground which shapes are benign vs which drop real TPs, so the deferral is honest. |
| 170 | **A defect class that spans PARALLEL decision paths funnelling through one shared helper is fixed at the HELPER (root cause), not per-arm — and that single fix closes every path and every caller at once.** Learning #169 (S156) said "when a fix corrects one decision path of a defect class, ground the SIBLING paths that decide the same thing." S157 acted on it and found the right fix was NOT to patch each arm: `findCellOptionLines` decides "does this `#|` value open a fold?" in two places — the single-line arm (`model.ts:601`, S156) and the multi-line-continuation skip `scanFlow(m[4], …)` (~:547) — but BOTH funnel the opener decision through the shared `scanFlow` helper. The anchor-name-quote defect lived in `scanFlow` itself (it read a quote inside a YAML node-property NAME `&a'b`/`*a'b`/`!t'x` as a scalar opener, when a quote is a legal `ns-anchor-char`). One ~7-line fix in `scanFlow` (skip a `&`/`*`/`!` node-property name to the next whitespace or c-flow-indicator `,[]{}`) closed the class on BOTH cell-option paths AND — because `scanFlow` is shared — the three front-matter/project VALUE enumerators' continuation paths too, with no regression (§9 missed-sites lens confirmed all four surfaces; over-suppression lens clean). Contrast S148/S151/S153/S155, which fixed the SAME class across sibling FUNCTIONS by editing each — here the sibling paths shared a primitive, so the primitive was the fix. **Three transferables. (a) Fix the shared primitive, not each caller, when parallel paths funnel through it** — grep for the helper (`scanFlow`), enumerate its callers, and fix once; it closes current AND future callers. This is #169's sibling-path rule resolved: the sibling path is sometimes a shared call, and then the root cause is upstream of both arms. **(b) A docstring's "X is handled" claim can be a latent bug, and must be re-derived PER FAILURE AXIS.** `scanFlow`'s doc asserted "scanning the WHOLE token is node-property-aware" — TRUE for the DEPTH axis (a node-property name holds no brackets, contributes 0) but FALSE for the QUOTE axis (the name may contain a quote, which the char-scan then misread as a delimiter). The claim was reasoned on one axis and silently generalized; a "handled" claim is only as broad as the axis it was proven on. **(c) A test guard/canary ported from a sibling surface can be VACUOUS on the new surface — verify its discriminating power ON THIS surface.** The mandatory §9 review earned its keep on TEST QUALITY (not code): the no-over-suppression integration guard used `#| number-sections` on the folded line, copied from the front-matter tests where `number-sections` IS validated — but it is NOT a validated CELL option, so in a cell context it can never flag whether folded OR emitted, making `!flaggedLines.includes(7)` inert (fixed by switching to a folded `#| echo`; the identical S156 sibling test had the same dead guard and was fixed too). Also a tag pin used a quote-free `!!str x` that passed pre-fix (didn't discriminate the `!` arm; switched to `!t'x`), and the double-quote `&a"b` continuation shape was unpinned (a double-quote-only regression would have escaped the whole suite). The lesson: a canary's validated-key set differs per surface — re-verify each guard is non-vacuous (would fail on the pre-fix source / on a mutant) where it now lives, don't assume a copied canary still bites. | S157 §9 review (`wf_2be01ba3-f22`, 5 lenses + adversarial verify, 14 agents, `agents_error:0`, `agents_empty_result:1`): missed-sites lens confirmed the shared-`scanFlow` fix covers all four enumerator surfaces with no regression; test-quality lens found the vacuous cell-context canary, the non-discriminating tag pin, and the missing double-quote pin — all fixed in-session, not filed | When a defect class spans parallel decision paths that funnel through ONE shared helper, fix the helper (root cause) — grep its callers, fix once, and state the blast radius; it closes every path and future caller, unlike per-arm edits. Treat any docstring "X is handled/aware" claim as axis-specific and re-derive it per failure axis (a claim proven on depth may be false on quotes). When a test guard or canary is ported from a sibling surface, prove it is non-vacuous ON THIS surface (run it against the pre-fix source or a mutant) — a validated-key set that makes a canary bite in front matter can leave it inert in a cell context. |
| 171 | **A skip-based FP fix in an INDENTATION-scoped construct is bounded by an indent boundary, not a delimiter — track the parent indent, terminate on dedent, and prove the over-skip only bites docs the tool already rejects.** S158 fixed the block-scalar (`\|`/`>`) cell-option FP: `findCellOptionLines` tracked only quote/flow-bracket continuation state (DELIMITER-scoped, via `scanFlow`), so a value opening a YAML block scalar (`#\| fig-cap: \|` / `#\|   echo: banana`) had its indentation-folded continuation emitted and flagged — a cardinal-sin FP on a doc quarto renders **exit 0**. Unlike a quote/flow (which closes on a delimiter), a block scalar's extent is INDENTATION-scoped: its content is every line more-indented than the opening KEY. The fix tracks the key's folded-indent, skips more-indented (and blank) lines, and terminates on the first non-blank dedent. **Three transferables. (a) The folded indentation of a `#\|` line = the post-pipe whitespace minus the ONE space quarto's `^#\s*\\\| ?` directive strips.** `CELL_OPTION_PREFIX` greedily captures that indentation into the gap group `m[3]` and DROPS it from `m[4]`, so it must be recovered as `m[3].startsWith(" ") ? m[3].length-1 : m[3].length`; grounded firsthand that a sibling at the SAME folded-indent renders exit 1 (real option) while one space more renders exit 0 (folded) — a STRICTLY-GREATER boundary. **(b) A skip-based fix's "over-suppression" finding is a SAFE FN only if the over-skip band exists exclusively on docs the tool ALREADY rejects — ground the renderability boundary (exit-0 vs exit-1-STRUCTURAL) before dismissing it.** The §9 review found the skip uses the KEY indent, not YAML's auto-detected CONTENT indent, so an option mis-indented in the `key < indent < content` band is swallowed. Grounded firsthand: that band ALWAYS renders a structural `YAMLException: bad indentation` (exit 1), never a value error, and a real option dedented to the key is correctly KEPT with its value diagnostic — so the over-skip is the safe-FN direction on already-malformed docs, not a lost value TP. The fact that decides "real regression vs immaterial safe-FN" is WHICH exit-1 reason the dropped line carries (structural vs value) and whether an exit-0 doc can exhibit the band at all. **(c) A "does-not-X" preservation pin is VACUOUS when the discriminating signal cannot appear on a valid doc — the honest discriminating pin sometimes asserts model behavior on an input the tool rejects.** The first `a \| b`-does-not-arm pin put its follow-on at the key's indent, where the termination guard emits it regardless of arm state (identical pre-fix AND against an over-arming mutant `/\|/` — doubly vacuous). The discriminating shape needs an INDENTED follow-on (a spurious arm would suppress it) — but that shape is malformed YAML (exit 1). The pin is still legitimate as a guard on the enumerator's ARM invariant, provided the comment states it is not a quarto-exit-0 fold. Verify every "does-not-X" guard against a targeted MUTANT, not only the pre-fix source. | S158 §9 review (`wf_bba35d38-f9f`, 5 lenses + adversarial verify, 7 agents, `agents_error:0`, `agents_empty_result:0`): missed-sites / header-regex / indent-model lenses CLEAN (grounded firsthand — the 3 value enumerators are indent-based by construction so have no block-scalar FP; the header regex tracks quarto's fold/error boundary exactly; the folded-indent model is faithful across `//\|`/`# \|`/tab/no-space prefixes); over-suppression lens found the intermediate-band safe-FN; test-quality lens found the vacuous plain-pipe pin — both adjudicated firsthand and FIXED in-session, none filed | When fixing a false positive by SKIPPING folded/continuation content in an indentation-scoped construct (block scalars, indented blocks): track the parent indent, terminate on dedent, and recover the tool-folded indentation, not the raw captured gap. When a review reports an "over-suppression" finding on a skip-based fix: ground the exact renderability boundary — a safe FN fires only where the tool already fails, and the deciding fact is whether an otherwise-valid (exit-0) doc can exhibit the dropped line and with what error KIND. When writing a "does-not-arm / does-not-match" preservation pin: verify it against a targeted MUTANT (not just the pre-fix source); if no valid doc discriminates it, assert model behavior on the rejected input and SAY SO in the comment. |
| 172 | **Widening the REACH of a name-resolution rule can make a latent FP live — before shipping a "resolve more keys" fix, ask what ELSE now resolves, especially on lines the guard should have suppressed; and when the widening is pre-existing at root, fix the root or revert the widening, never ship it.** S159 unquoted the YAML key on the `.qmd` value surfaces so `"toc": banana` resolves to `toc` — a LIVE lost TP (quarto exit 1, we were silent), correctly grounded firsthand before any code. The §9 review then found the fix would make a PRE-EXISTING cardinal-sin FP newly reachable: `mappingColonAt` picked the colon INSIDE a quoted key (`"a: b": "text`), so the arm token was bogus, the continuation guard never armed, and the FOLDED line below was emitted — on a doc quarto renders exit 0. That line was harmless while its key stayed unresolvable; unquoting made it resolve and flag. The transferable is a review question, not a code rule: a resolution fix and a suppression guard interact — everything the enumerator wrongly EMITS is inert only for as long as it fails to resolve, so "improve resolution" silently promotes every latent emission bug into a live diagnostic. S159 measured the direction rather than arguing it (real enumerators + real installed schema, pre-tree vs post-tree, over the review's own 18-document corpus): with a BARE folded key the FP was already live, proving PRE-EXISTING; with a quoted one it was new, proving WIDENED. That pre/post split is what turned "is this mine?" from a judgment call into a measurement. Action taken, and the rule for next time: fix the root where it is shareable (the front-matter twin lived in the SHARED `mappingColonAt`, so skipping a quoted key there removed BOTH the widening and the pre-existing FP — Learning #170 again), and REVERT where it is not (the cell arm lives in the import-free `model.ts`, which `yaml-context.ts` imports, so sharing the rule is cross-module plan-mode work — the half-fix was reverted and filed with its required fix ORDER: arm grammar first, then the unquote). Shipping a known FP widening because "the pre-existing one is worse" is not a trade this project makes. | S159 §9 fp-cardinal lens (HIGH), adjudicated firsthand | Before shipping any change that makes MORE keys/paths/names resolve (unquoting, aliasing, normalization, case-folding, a wider charset): enumerate what the change newly resolves, and re-run the suppression-guard FP corpus — not just the recovery cases. If a latent FP goes live, measure pre/post with a BARE (already-resolving) variant to classify pre-existing vs widened, then fix the root or revert; never ship the widening. |
| 173 | **A predicate that is safely APPROXIMATE while it classifies one item becomes load-bearing the moment it also decides where a RUN of items ends — re-derive its exactness against ground truth before promoting it.** S160 taught `findCellOptionLines` quarto's leading-block rule by `break`ing on the first body line that fails `CELL_OPTION_PREFIX`. That regex was already the enumerator's definition of "is this a `#|` option line", so reusing it read as free. But it is deliberately STRICTER than quarto's real directive predicate (`^<comment>\s*\| ?`) because it must also SLICE the line into key/value spans: its gap is `[ \t]` not `\s`, and it ends `(.*)$` where `.` excludes U+2028/U+2029. In its ORIGINAL role that strictness cost one line — an unparseable directive was skipped and everything below it still validated (a bounded FN). In its NEW role it cost the whole cell: one non-breaking space, vertical tab, or U+2028 silently discarded every real option below it, converting a one-line FN into a whole-cell LOST TRUE POSITIVE — measured firsthand at quarto exit 1 with a real `Field "echo" has value banana` on all three shapes, each of which the PRE-S160 enumerator had reported. The session's own §9 review found it; the fix was to SPLIT the roles (a permissive `CELL_OPTION_DIRECTIVE` decides where the block ENDS, the strict `CELL_OPTION_PREFIX` still decides what is EMITTED), which is safe precisely because emission is untouched and so no new diagnostic can appear. **When to apply:** any time an existing predicate is reused to delimit a REGION (terminate a block, bound a scan, end a run) rather than to classify a single item, ask what its false-NEGATIVES now cost — they stop being "one missed item" and become "everything after it". Re-derive the predicate against the authority's own rule (here: read quarto's `optionCommentPattern` behaviour off the real binary and measure it), and if the exact and the sliceable forms differ, use the exact one for the boundary and keep the strict one for the payload. Generalizes #170 (fix at the shared root) to the case where the shared thing is a PREDICATE and the two consumers need DIFFERENT strictness — sharing it is the bug, not the fix. |
| 174 | **Replacing a hard-coded constant with the authority's own TABLE moves the risk from the value to the LOOKUP — and a downstream consumer that re-derives spans from RAW text does not learn the new rule.** S161 replaced `findCellOptionLines`'s two hard-coded comment chars with quarto's per-language `kLangCommentChars`. The table itself was the easy part; three separate defects lived in the seams around it. **(a) The authority's table may not be its EFFECTIVE table.** The static literal in `web-worker.js` has 46 entries, but quarto overlays `handlers/lang-comment-chars.yml` at startup, making 47 (`mermaid: "%%"`). Transcribing "the table" from the obvious source silently under-copied it — and, separately, copying the missing row would have made things WORSE, because `mermaid` is a cell-HANDLER language quarto never schema-validates (`handlers/languages.yml` is exactly `["mermaid","dot"]`, and a handler cell renders any option value at exit 0). The fidelity question and the reachability question are different questions; answer both before adding a row. **(b) Widening a detector promotes every DOWNSTREAM approximation into a live defect.** Two shipped-quality FPs appeared only because the enumerator started emitting for new languages: value-diagnostics resolved keys against ALL schema fields when `engineFor` returned `undefined` (quarto scopes its cell schema to the DOCUMENT engine, so `--| cache: banana` in `{sql}` renders exit 0 in a markdown-engine doc), and it re-derived the value span from the RAW line text — which still carries a block-comment language's CLOSING delimiter, so the VALID `/*| echo: false */` failed `echo`'s closed set as `false */`. Both were inert while the detector was narrow. The general rule: **when you widen what a detector reports, re-audit every consumer that re-derives anything from the raw input, because the detector's new knowledge does not travel with the data** — fix it by shipping the bound (`contentEndCol`), not by duplicating the grammar downstream. **(c) A CONTROL added for non-vacuity can change the fixture's meaning.** The §9 review found the engine-scope integration pin justified itself with a false claim: the `{r}` cell added to prove `cache` is a real key is exactly what makes the document KNITR, where quarto DOES validate the `{sql}` cell's `cache` — so the test pinned an accepted safe FN while claiming to guard an FP. Measured: that document renders exit 1, and removing the `{r}` cell renders exit 0. **When to apply:** any time a fix replaces a constant with a lookup table sourced from another tool — verify the EFFECTIVE table (grep for runtime overlays, not just the literal), and ask separately whether each new row is REACHABLE and VALIDATED by that tool. Any time a detector is widened — grep every consumer for re-derivation from raw input. Any time a control is added to a shared fixture — re-measure the fixture, because a control is part of the input. | S161 §9 review (`wf_c7cd5eb0-9af`, 6 lenses, 34 agents, `agents_error:0`, ~1.99M subagent tokens; 5 findings, all confirmed firsthand by the session before acting — 2 were defects in its own work and fixed, 3 were pre-existing with distinct root causes and filed). The engine-scope and closer-in-span FPs were caught by the session's own layered verification before the review ran. | When swapping a hard-coded constant for another tool's table: confirm the effective table (runtime overlays), and check reachability + validation per row, not just the value. When widening any detector: re-audit consumers that re-derive spans/keys from raw text, and ship a BOUND rather than duplicating the grammar. When adding a non-vacuity control to a test fixture: re-measure the fixture against ground truth, since the control is itself input. |
| 175 | **A universal-negative justification ("this costs no true positive", "this key is unvalidated") needs an EXHAUSTIVE sweep over the domain it quantifies — a handful of probes will confirm it and still be wrong, especially along a variable you already know changes the verdict.** S162 suppressed cell-option validation in quarto's two cell-HANDLER languages and justified it with "the suppression costs no true positive", generalised from five keys measured in a markdown-engine document. The §9 review disputed it; an exhaustive sweep of all 47 keys the feature can flag, one render each, in BOTH engines, showed 46/47 exit 0 under markdown (the one exception being a field we could never flag anyway) but exactly one real value-dependent failure under knitr (`include`) — a true positive the fix gave up. The same error shape appeared twice more in the same session: `theme: banana` renders exit 0, so the docstring called `theme` unenforced, but the schema is `null|string` and `theme: [1,2]`/`42` render exit 1; and `{mermaid}` + `#| echo: banana` renders exit 0 only because the fixture had no `{r}` cell — S161's OWN Learning #174c had already recorded that an `{r}` cell makes the document knitr. Each time the discriminating variable was one the session already knew about and had not varied. A companion failure, same root: the pin's blast-radius justification named four consumers from architectural memory (outline, virtual docs, cell-background, crossref index) and every one was wrong — only two modules import the enumerator at all, and for these two languages the vdoc paths bail on an unmapped `cellLanguageId` before reaching it. The DESIGN survived all of it; only the CLAIMS were false — which is the point: a false justification passes every test, ships green, and misleads the next reader about what the tool actually does. | Whenever a fix ADDS suppression, or a comment/docstring states a universal negative about another tool's behaviour. Before writing "costs nothing" / "is never validated" / "renders exit 0": (1) enumerate the domain the claim quantifies (every key the feature can flag, not the ones you probed) and sweep it mechanically — 47 renders is minutes; (2) list the variables already known to change the verdict (document engine, comment char, case, fence token) and vary each one; (3) for any blast-radius claim, produce it as a GREP of who imports the symbol plus a reachability check, never as recollection; (4) if an exception survives, keep the design and state the exception with its measurement — an honest bounded claim beats a clean false one. |
| 176 | **When you SUPPRESS on another tool's say-so, the domain you must sweep is "everything the tool still rejects", and it is almost never the one mechanism you found the flag in — a suppression gate inherits every OTHER layer's validation as a lost true positive.** S163 taught the value-diagnostics feature to honour quarto's `validate-yaml: false` escape hatch. The gate was read straight from quarto's source (`context.format.render?.["validate-yaml"] !== false` around `validateDocument`), grounded across 58 documents, and the session correctly discovered on its own that the front-matter FORMAT NAME survives the flag (`format: banana` renders exit 1 regardless, because format RESOLUTION runs earlier) — and then wrote "THE ONE SURFACE the flag does not suppress is the format NAME" into three places. False. The exhaustive sweep the §9 review forced — all **170** top-level keys the feature can flag, one render each with the flag set — returned **26 survivors** (`toc`, `citeproc`, `ascii`, `incremental`, `columns`, `dpi`, `wrap`, `eol`, …), every one of which also fails without the flag, i.e. 26 real value errors the fix now silences. **The mechanism is the transferable part: they are rejected by PANDOC's Aeson decoder, a completely different layer from the quarto YAML-schema pass the flag gates.** The session had found ONE non-schema rejection layer (format resolution), generalised "so that's the exception", and never asked how many layers there are. A suppression gate does not suppress a mechanism — it suppresses a whole class of diagnostics, and every independent validator downstream of the gated one becomes a silent false negative. **Two corollaries, both measured.** (a) **The domain to sweep is what YOU can flag, not what the tool rejects** — the review swept "keys quarto rejects" and got a different, less useful number; the honest domain is `isWrongValue("banana", field) === true`, because a key the feature can never flag cannot cost a true positive (the S162 `layout` lesson, reapplied). (b) **A raw-token test against a PARSED-value gate is incomplete by construction.** The same session's `isValidationDisabledValue` compared the token to `false`/`False`/`FALSE` while quarto compares the parsed node, so anchored (`&a false`), tagged (`!!bool false`), aliased (`*a`) and next-line spellings all disarm quarto while we keep flagging — and the obvious repair is a trap: `!!str false` renders exit 1 while `!!bool "false"` renders exit 0, so a strip-the-tag-and-compare fix INVERTS the quoted-scalar rule it was built on. **Process note that mattered:** the session's own first anchor probe returned exit 1 and appeared to refute the review; it was wrong because shell double-quote escaping left a literal backslash in the YAML. Re-running with a heredoc reversed the result. A probe that contradicts a careful reviewer deserves a second look at the probe before the conclusion. | S163 §9 review (`wf_231f6a13-885`, 6 lenses, 42 agents, 2 refuters per finding, `agents_error:0`, ~2.58M subagent tokens; 18 findings surfaced, adjudicated firsthand — the false universal and two weak tests fixed in-session, the behavioural gaps filed with their sweeps). The 26-key exception and all four value spellings were re-measured by the session itself, and its own sweep was sharper than the review's. | Whenever a fix SUPPRESSES diagnostics on another tool's opt-out flag, feature detection, or version gate: (1) enumerate every INDEPENDENT validation layer the tool runs, not just the one the flag lives in — grep for other error sites, and expect at least one more than you found; (2) sweep the domain YOU can flag, mechanically, one probe per member, and report the survivors as a measured trade rather than an exception; (3) if the tool's gate tests a PARSED value, a raw-token matcher is incomplete on principle — enumerate the node-level spellings (anchor/alias/tag/multi-line) before claiming a spelling rule is complete; (4) when your own probe contradicts a reviewer, re-derive the probe (shell escaping, engine, format) before trusting your result over theirs. |
| 177 | **When you mirror another tool's decision, mirror its FILTER and then prove the mirror by running BOTH over the same domain — and when you assert what the other tool does, make sure the probe you measured it with is valid input to that tool.** S164 had to decide the schema scope of a cell option once the document engine is known. Reading quarto's `makeEngineSchema` showed the rule is one line (keep a `cell-*` field iff its `tags.engine` is absent, equals the engine, or is a LIST containing it) and that `SchemaIndex.cellOptions` already implemented that same filter — so the missing input was the engine NAME, not a new schema model. The proof then cost four lines: run quarto's rule and ours over all 43 fields the feature can actually flag, for all four engines, and set-diff. They agreed exactly — far stronger than the "only a knitr answer widens" written from a sample — and the same sweep surfaced the one genuine divergence (`engineTag` maps a multi-engine list to *agnostic*; quarto excludes such a field from the markdown/julia schemas), inert today only because the single such field is open-valued. **The second half is what cost this session most.** Three docstring claims about quarto's behaviour were false, and the worst rested on a probe that was not valid input: `{dot}` cells with `1 + 1` as the body, whose exit 1 came from a graphviz SYNTAX error and was written up as a structural chunk-option rejection. Re-measured with `digraph { a -> b }`, the claimed exit 1 was exit 0 and the real justification was a different key entirely. **Reusable rule:** a probe that fails for ANY reason proves nothing until you have read WHY it failed — an exit code is not a measurement, the error text is. Pair every negative probe with a positive control on the same document (S163's lesson) AND confirm the artefact under test is well-formed in its own language. **Where it would have bitten:** believing "markdown means no validation" would have silenced `#| echo: banana` under `engine: markdown`, which quarto renders exit 1 — trading a false-positive fix for a lost true positive, the same error shape S163's filed prescription made one session earlier. | S164 (the `engine:` override); the §9 review findings on `cellOptionScopeFor`'s handler branch and the `"unknown"`-is-exact claim | Whenever a feature's correctness rests on "we classify X the same way tool T does": find T's classifier, re-run it over T's own resource, set-diff against ours restricted to what we can act on — and before quoting any probe's exit code as evidence, read its error text and confirm the probe document is valid input. |
| 178 | **When a fallback turns "we could not read it" into a CONFIDENT answer, every existing decline in the module silently becomes a claim — re-audit all of them before shipping the fallback, not after.** S165 taught the value-diagnostics feature quarto's default engine resolution (from the document's cell languages) so that `undefined` — previously "keep the per-cell language guess" — became reachable only in exotic cases. Every place the module already declined to resolve something (a node property, a block scalar, an alias, an empty token, a flow mapping, a front matter our scanner cannot see) had been written when declining was INERT: the caller fell back to the per-cell language, which was all it had anyway. After the fallback, those same declines resolved through the languages to a document-WIDE answer — knitr on any document holding an `{r}` cell — and so squiggled every OTHER cell of documents `quarto render` accepts. The §9 review measured **twelve** such shapes, all cardinal-sin false positives introduced by one line of intent ("an unresolved selector should not block the fallback"). The fix was one rule applied at every site: separate "we READ it and it names nothing" (quarto matched nothing either — follow it) from "we could not READ it" (quarto may have matched — block the fallback), which meant turning two booleans into three-way answers (`readEngineScalar`, `readTruthiness`). **The general form:** changing what a sentinel MEANS is a change to every producer of that sentinel, even though the diff touches none of them; grep every `return undefined` / `return false` in the module and ask what it now asserts. A second, cheaper tell: the docstrings that justified those declines (in three different files, including one the diff never opened) each contained a sentence of the form "declining returns us to the previous behaviour" — that sentence is the invariant the fallback breaks, so its presence anywhere in the module is the audit list. | Whenever a change makes a previously-inert default reachable as a real answer — adding a fallback, a resolver, a default branch, a coalescing operator over a value that used to mean "unknown". Before writing the fallback, enumerate the sentinel's producers and classify each as "definitely nothing" or "cannot tell"; only the first may feed the fallback. And run the end-to-end oracle against the PREVIOUS build as well as the new one: S165's own 48-document corpus reported "0 regressed" while twelve regressions sat outside it, and only replaying three builds (`git archive` + a `REPO` override) over the review-widened 64-document corpus turned that into a number. |
| 179 | **A verification harness that lives in a session scratchpad is not evidence — it is a claim; and the act of committing one is what exposes the drift it had already accumulated.** S164 and S165 each built an exit-code oracle (17 then 64 documents), each leaned on it for their strongest safety claim, and each left it in a disposable scratchpad — so "18 improved, 0 regressed" could never be re-checked by anyone afterwards, including by the next session touching the same code. S166's deliverable was simply to commit it, and porting it surfaced three defects that two sessions of heavy use had not: (a) **the mirror had silently drifted from the feature it mirrors.** `cellOptionFlags` re-walks `computeValueDiagnostics`'s loop because that function imports `vscode` and cannot be called headlessly — and S165's copy omitted the S163 `validate-yaml` escape hatch entirely. No corpus row used the flag, so the omission was invisible; a mutant deleting the guard (i.e. reproducing S165's version) now kills the new pins. **A copy of a decision needs a pin per step, or it is only a copy of the decision as it was on the day it was written.** (b) **the resource path was true on one machine** (`/Applications/quarto/...` hard-coded) where the product itself resolves it via `quarto --paths` through an already-tested pure function — a harness should reuse the product's resolvers, not re-derive them. (c) **the harness parsed quarto's schema with the CURRENT build's parser while replaying an OLD build's decision**, which is not a faithful replay; the parser must come from the build under test too. **The transferable rule:** when a piece of verification machinery is used twice, committing it is not tidying — it is the only way its own correctness gets reviewed, and its findings become reproducible rather than testimonial. **Two design points earned their keep immediately.** A committed BASELINE must require a written REASON for every row it records as wrong, because when the oracle fails the cheapest fix is to re-seed it, which silently promotes a new defect to "expected"; the gate was verified by deleting one reason and watching it name that row.
