And the comparison must NAME every regressed row and report rows present in only one build as *incomparable* rather than dropping them — silent omission is precisely how S165's own oracle reported "0 regressed" while twelve regressions sat outside its corpus. **Confirmation the port was faithful:** replaying `git archive 87b3f38` reproduced all three of S165's headline numbers independently — pre-S165 FP 16, shipping FP 4, 18 rows improved — which is the strongest available evidence that a re-implemented harness measures the same thing the original did. | S166 (committing the oracle). The three defects were found by porting, not by review; the escape-hatch gap is the one that would have produced wrong numbers. | Whenever a session builds throwaway measurement machinery and then relies on its output for a safety claim: budget the session AFTER it to commit the harness, and treat the port as an audit rather than a move. Pin every step of any decision the harness MIRRORS from production code (a mirror drifts silently and its output stays confident); reuse the product's own path/config resolvers instead of hard-coding what worked locally; make the build under test a parameter so any commit can be replayed; require a written reason for every accepted defect in the committed baseline; and name — never merely count — the rows that moved. |
| 180 | **A grep whose pattern cannot match its target returns the identical empty output to a grep that genuinely found nothing — so an inventory that publishes the empty result is presenting a measurement failure as evidence.** S167's plan was required (SESSION_RUNNER §Planning Sessions) to carry a grep-based evidence inventory, and it published `grep -n "diagnostics\[[0-9]\]\|\.at(\|sort(" test/integration/suite/yaml-value-diagnostics.test.ts` → no matches, concluding "the suite never asserts by array index". The suite names its array `diags`, not `diagnostics`, in all 11 declarations, so the pattern was structurally incapable of matching; six index-based assertions existed. The stated conclusion (diagnostic ORDER is unpinned) happened to survive, but only for a reason the plan never established — every one of those six sites is guarded by an `assert.strictEqual(diags.length, 1, …)` immediately above, and a one-element array has no ordering. This is the same defect class as Learnings #175/#176/#177 (a universal asserted from an unswept domain) with a new and more insidious mechanism: there, the sweep was too small; here, the sweep *silently did not run*, and its output was indistinguishable from success. The adversarial panel found it; the author did not. | Before publishing ANY negative grep result — "no matches", "no other call sites", "nothing references this" — first prove the pattern can match at all: run it against a form you KNOW returns hits (grep the bare identifier, drop the bracket, or grep a file you know contains the shape), and only then trust the empty result. Applies with double force inside a mandatory evidence inventory, where an empty result is read by the executor as a verified fact rather than an unrun command. Corollary for reviews: when a conclusion is right but the evidence offered for it is unsound, fix the evidence — a true conclusion resting on a broken measurement will be inherited by the next person who trusts the prose. |
| 181 | **An inventory grep is only as complete as the SYMBOL LIST you derived it from — and the safest source for that list is the deleted file's own exports, never the plan's prose.** Session 168's plan (itself panel-reviewed, and whose §11 headline was an unsound grep) told the executor to sweep for `oracle/flags\|cellOptionFlags\|OracleCoreApi\|oracle-flags` before deleting the mirror. That pattern is sound and matches; it is also incomplete. The modules being deleted and rewritten exported a fifth name the plan never enumerated — `loadCoreApi` — whose third consumer (`test/unit/oracle-corpus.test.ts`) went red at runtime the moment the deletion landed. The executor re-derived every *safety rationale* the plan asserted (per §11's standing caution) but inherited the plan's *inventory* wholesale, which is the same trust in different clothing. Compounding it: `tsconfig.test.json` covers `test/oracle/**` and `test/integration/**` but not `test/unit/**`, so the dangling import was invisible to `check-types` AND `compile-tests` — the type error could only ever surface as a test failure. | Before deleting or renaming a module, build the grep's symbol list MECHANICALLY from the artefact itself — `git show <rev>:<path> \| grep -E '^export (async )?(function\|const\|interface\|type\|class)'` — and sweep each name, rather than trusting a list written in prose by an earlier session (including your own plan). Then re-run the FULL test matrix at that boundary, not the plan's per-layer subset: a per-layer gate list is an abbreviation, and anything it omits is structurally invisible at exactly the moment you are most likely to break it. |
| 182 | **A per-layer verification list in a plan is a floor, not the gate — following it literally can ship a red checkpoint commit.** The plan's Layer 3 stated its gates as `compile-tests` + `test:oracle`, and Session 168 ran exactly those and committed. That checkpoint (`c0d4fd2`) left `npm test` RED: Layer 3 removed `loadCoreApi` while a unit test still imported it, and neither stated gate could see `test/unit`. The breakage was found one layer later and its cause was then misattributed in the Layer 4 commit message to the *deletion* of the mirror files rather than to the *removal of the export* one commit earlier. Vertical-slice gate (c) says the complete build/test matrix runs at EACH layer boundary; a plan that lists a convenient subset per layer quietly overrides that, and the executor who follows the plan faithfully is the one who breaks it. | When executing a multi-layer plan, treat each layer's listed commands as ADDITIONS to the full matrix (`check-types`, `compile-tests`, `npm test`, plus whatever that layer names), never as a replacement for it — especially at a layer that removes or renames an export. When a breakage IS found late, trace it to the commit that introduced it before writing the cause into a message: the commit that surfaces a defect is usually not the commit that caused it. |
| 183 | **A citation is not evidence — when a docstring cites a prior finding to justify a hazard, re-read the cited lines, because the thing most often cited is the GUARD that already killed the hazard.** S169's new `document-engine-resolve.ts` opened with its load-bearing justification: passing `findFrontMatterValueLines` where `findFrontMatterTopLevelLines` belongs "type-checks and silently un-ships the container form (`document-engine.ts:38-45`)". Measured, that substitution is `error TS2345: Property 'hasChildren' is missing` — and `document-engine.ts:38-45`, the very citation, exists to record that `hasChildren` was MADE required for exactly this reason and closes with "Requiring the field turns that call into a compile error." I had read that block, taken the hazard from it, and not noticed it also reported the hazard fixed. The tell is grammatical: a citation supporting a present-tense hazard claim should be checked for past tense. **When to apply:** any time a comment justifies a design with "X would silently go wrong" and cites a source — open the source and check whether it describes the problem or the solution. A guard cited as a threat inverts the reader's model of what is protected. |
| 184 | **"Which cells does this change affect?" must be answered by enumerating the function's input space, not by naming the example that motivated the work — the motivating example is a sample, and a sample is not a direction.** S169 fixed completion scoping and described it, in three places, as fixing `{python}`/`{sql}` cells. A four-line table over (cell language × document engine) showed `{sql}` was never affected in that direction at all (`engineFor("sql")` is `undefined`, the UNFILTERED set, so `cache` was already offered) while `{ojs}`/`{js}` — never mentioned — were live instances, AND that two cells NARROW (`{sql}` in knitr, `{r}` in jupyter). The change was documented as a widening; it is both. The §9 review found the false half and, separately, that the `{ojs}` half was unpinned: a mutant excluding ojs re-opened the exact defect with all 1545 tests green. **When to apply:** before writing any prose that names which inputs a behavioural change affects, generate the full cross-product of the changed function's arguments and compute before/after for each. Cheap (one throwaway test printing a table) and it catches both the false claim and the missing pin, which are the same defect seen from two sides. |
| 185 | **A stated invariant can be true, precisely scoped, and still be the thing hiding the regression — check what it is silent about.** S169 claimed "the offered set is never a strict subset of the flagged set", which is true and was the point of the design: completion can no longer refuse a key the validator squiggles. But it compares completion to the VALIDATOR, not to what completion offered yesterday, so it is structurally incapable of noticing that two cell classes now offer FEWER keys than before. The invariant was not chosen to hide that — it was chosen before the narrowing was known — but it functioned as a reason not to look. **When to apply:** whenever a change ships behind an invariant, write down the question the invariant cannot answer and answer that separately. For a scoping change the two questions are "is it still safe?" (the invariant) and "did anyone lose anything?" (a before/after diff over the input space, Learning #184). |
| 186 | **Verify a mechanical multi-site edit by AUDITING the result, not by running the tests — a regex can land the right text in the wrong syntactic position and still produce a green suite.** S169 added a required third argument to 70 `completionContextAt(...)` call sites with one non-greedy `perl -0pi -e` substitution. The pattern's first `\)` matched the INNER `offsetAt(...)` call, so all 71 sites became `completionContextAt(text, offsetAt(text, 1, 5, () => undefined))` — argument in the wrong call, `completionContextAt` still receiving only two. It was caught by counting call shapes (`grep -o ... \| sed 's/offsetAt([^)]*)/OFFSET/' \| sort -u`), before any test ran. Tests would have caught this particular one (a missing thunk throws), but the near-miss is the lesson: the same regex applied to a site whose extra argument is merely *ignored* would have been invisible. **When to apply:** after any scripted edit across more than a handful of sites, enumerate the distinct resulting SHAPES and eyeball that set — it is a two-command check that does not depend on the tests being able to observe the mistake. |
| 187 | **A branch that DECLINES to answer is not neutral once the caller starts acting on answers — re-ask whether every historical decline is still the right one, especially the ones that were correct when written.** S164 made the `.Rmd` extension a VETO (`return undefined`) for an exactly right reason: an `engine:` key must not silence a document quarto validates anyway. That was correct, and it stayed in place through S165 (which turned `undefined` into a *fallback* answer) and S169 (which gave `undefined` a second consumer, completion) — two sessions that each raised its cost without revisiting it. By S169 the `.Rmd` was the ONE document class whose engine was knowable without reading anything and the ONE where BOTH surfaces were wrong. The tell is in the S164 docstring itself: "What this deliberately does NOT do is scope those documents TO knitr" — a decline that documents its own incompleteness is a filed item, not a settled design. | When a module gains a new consumer, a new fallback, or a new confident-answer path, grep its own `return undefined` / early-decline branches and re-read each one's justification against the NEW caller. A decline written when the caller had nothing to lose becomes a claim the moment the caller acts on it. |
| 188 | **A verification corpus with a uniform input dimension cannot see a change keyed on that dimension — and it will report green rather than report nothing, which is worse.** Every one of the oracle's 66 documents was named `doc.qmd`. The change was keyed on the FILE EXTENSION. C1 therefore ran byte-identically green over the whole corpus — 59 agree / 3 lost TP / 4 cardinal FP, matching the predecessor's close-out figure to the row — while the branch it altered was untouched by every document in it. That green is not evidence of safety; it is evidence of nothing, and it reads exactly like evidence of safety. This is S165's horizon lesson recurring on a different axis: S165 missed twelve regressions on front-matter *shapes* outside its corpus; S170 would have missed the entire change on a *file name* dimension the corpus held constant. | Before believing a harness's verdict on a change, name the INPUT the change is keyed on and check the corpus actually varies it. If every row holds it constant, the run is vacuous — add rows first, then read the number. Applies to any fixture-driven gate, not only this oracle: a constant is invisible precisely because nothing looks at it. |
| 189 | **A pin whose two operands agree by coincidence proves nothing, and the coincidence is easiest to introduce when the pin is about an OVERRIDE.** The pre-existing `.Rmd` pins were written to prove the extension is honoured — but they used `{r}`-cell documents, whose LANGUAGE fallback already answers knitr, so both the correct build and a build that ignored `fileName` entirely produced the same answer. The same was true of the integration pin: its fixture's cell was `{r}`. Three pins across two levels, all passing, none discriminating. The fix is mechanical: for a pin about input X, choose the other inputs so the answer WITHOUT X differs from the answer WITH it — here a `{python}`-only document, whose fallback is jupyter, so `"knitr"` can only have come from the extension. | Whenever pinning a rule that OVERRIDES or PRE-EMPTS another rule, construct the case so the two rules disagree. If the overridden rule would have produced the same answer, the pin measures nothing. Ask of every assertion: "what does the build that ignores this input return here?" — if it is the expected value, the pin is vacuous. (The project-specific form of the `pyDoc` habit S165's §9 review established, now shown to apply to the extension branch too.) |
| 190 | **When a widening's safety rests on a quantified claim ("all N of these are real"), the sweep is cheap and the reasoning is not — and the reasoning was measurably wrong once in this very session.** Adopting knitr on an `.Rmd` newly exposes 20 fields to being flagged, and the safety argument is that quarto rejects all 20. That was swept — 20 renders, all exit 1 — rather than argued from the schema tags. The value of doing so was demonstrated minutes later on an adjacent claim: writing the oracle baseline note, I derived "5 lost TP / 7 agree" for the pre-change replay by reasoning about which scopes contain `cache`. The measured split was **8 / 4**. Same session, same domain, same confidence, wrong by 60%. | For any change that widens what a tool reports, enumerate the newly-reachable set programmatically (a set-difference over the real schema, not a remembered list), then measure EVERY member against ground truth. Never publish a count, split or tally that was derived rather than observed — if a number appears in a docstring, a commit message or a ledger entry, it should be copy-pasted from a run. |
| 191 | **A filed item's measured DEFECT does not make its prescribed FIX measured — and a prescription, unlike a measurement, gets repeated verbatim across sessions without ever being re-tested.** The blank-line item was filed by S164 with the defect grounded firsthand (exit 0, with both controls), and in the same breath prescribed the fix: "closing it means teaching the scanner quarto's `trimLeft`". That clause was copied into `document-engine.ts` twice, restated by S165, and carried into S170's handoff as the ranked #1 recommendation — four sessions in which the defect's evidence lent unearned credibility to a fix nobody had probed. It was wrong. Quarto runs TWO partitioners and they disagree on purpose: `partitionYamlFrontMatter` (ENGINE) opens with `lines(markdown.trimLeft())`, while `validateDocumentFromSource` (front-matter VALUES) gates on `breakQuartoMd`'s first cell literally `startsWith("---")` — a byte-0 test with no trim. Sweeping 17 keys whose bad value quarto rejects at line 0 and re-rendering each behind one blank line, **10 render exit 0**. The prescribed shared-scanner fix would have closed 1 cardinal FP and opened 10. The actual fix — trim in `resolveDocumentEngine`, the one entry point both ENGINE consumers share — closes 5 with 0 regressions. **When to apply:** treat a filed item as two separate claims with two separate evidence bars. Before implementing the prescription, go to the upstream tool's SOURCE and check whether the surface you are about to change is the one the tool actually keys on; where a tool has two code paths over the same syntax, assume they diverge until measured. A defect verified firsthand is not a design verified firsthand. |
| 192 | **A test row that shares its NAME with the defect is the most dangerous kind of vacuous coverage, because its name is what future sessions read instead of its mechanism.** The oracle corpus already carried `REVIEW blank line BEFORE --- + engine: markdown`, baselined `agree` — which reads as "this shape is handled". It is not: that row uses the shared `rThenPy` document, whose flagged cell is `{python}`, and the per-cell fallback answers JUPYTER there, so `cache` was out of scope and we stayed silent for the wrong reason. Swapping in an `{r}` cell — whose fallback answers knitr — turned the same shape into a cardinal FP immediately, and the pre/post replay then scored 10 → 5. This is the third instance of this corpus's own horizon warning (S165's twelve regressions outside a 48-document corpus; S170's 66 rows all named `doc.qmd`) and the second where the row LOOKED like coverage rather than being absent — S170's three `.Rmd` pins were vacuous the same way, all using `{r}` documents whose language already answered knitr. **When to apply:** when a corpus or test row shares a name with the case you are about to change, do not read its name or its baselined class as coverage. Ask by what MECHANISM it currently passes, and check that the mechanism is the one under test; if the row would pass against a build that ignored your input entirely, it is not a row about your input. The general form: a passing assertion proves something only once you can state what would make it fail. |
| 193 | **When a fresh measurement contradicts an earlier one on what should be the same input, suspect the harness before the tool.** Probing whether a jupyter-selecting front matter narrows completion, I got exit 1 where a control had earlier given exit 1 and a no-selector row gave exit 0 — the reverse of a measurement I had taken correctly twenty minutes before. The tool had not changed; my harness had. The documents were built in a single-quoted shell string where `` \` `` is a literal backslash-backtick, so the "fences" were `\`\`\`{r}` and none of those documents contained a code cell at all. Every exit code in that batch was real and about a different document than the one I named. Rebuilt with Python writing exact bytes, the sanity control came back exit 1 and the anomaly vanished. Two things made it catchable: I had an earlier measurement of an overlapping shape to contradict, and I read the contradiction as a signal rather than as a surprising finding about quarto. **When to apply:** keep at least one overlapping row between measurement batches so a harness break has something to contradict; when shell-quoting documents that contain backticks, dollar signs or backslashes, dump the bytes (`cat -A`) or build them in a language with unambiguous string literals; and when a result would be a genuinely surprising fact about a mature tool, re-derive the INPUT before writing the fact down. |
| 194 | **Inheriting a predecessor's framing is the same error as inheriting its inventory — "both surfaces move" was true twice and false the third time.** S169 unified diagnostics and completion behind one resolver and S170 moved both through it, so "both surfaces move because they share one entry point" became the family's stock sentence. I began writing the matching completion integration pin for S171 on that basis. Reading `completionEngineFor` instead of assuming: a `"markdown"` document maps to the `"unknown"` scope, which falls back to the cell LANGUAGE — precisely what `undefined` did before the fix. The offered set is identical on both sides, so the pin would have asserted behaviour this change does not produce, and its passing would have been evidence of nothing. Only DIAGNOSTICS move for this document class. (The shape where completion WOULD move needs a front matter selecting a NARROWER engine, and the obvious probe for it, `jupyter: true`, quarto rejects outright as invalid jupyter metadata in both shapes — an unrelated error, not an engine measurement.) **When to apply:** a shared entry point makes it POSSIBLE for two consumers to move together, never certain — each consumer applies its own mapping downstream. Before writing a test that asserts a second surface moved, trace that surface's own decision function for the specific input and confirm the before and after values actually differ. State in the test which surfaces the change does NOT touch, so the next session inherits the boundary rather than the slogan. |
| 195 | **A filed item can be right that a defect exists and wrong about what the defect IS — and the second error survives longer, because the symptom keeps confirming it.** Three sessions independently filed the fence-token divergence, and all three described it as CELL_INFO being too PERMISSIVE: it "captures the token greedily and then accepts any trailing characters". True, and incomplete. The `[^}]*` tail is also too RESTRICTIVE — a character class excluding `}` cannot span a `}` inside a quoted chunk option, so the WELL-FORMED knitr header `` ```{r, fig.cap="}"} `` was not a cell to us while quarto validates it (measured exit 1). Every filed symptom was a malformed token, so every filing generalised to "reject malformed tokens" and none tested the well-formed direction. Worse, `diagram-regions.ts`'s prescribed fix — "require the engine token to end at whitespace, a comma, or `}`" — would have been WRONG on one of its own four examples: ending at `=` keeps the language `mermaid`, and `{mermaid=x}` would still have been drawn as a diagram, because it IS a cell to quarto whose language is literally `mermaid=x`. **When a filed item prescribes a fix, test the PRESCRIPTION against the upstream tool's source, not just the symptom against a render — and enumerate the input space in both directions, because a defect filed N times from N symptoms is still filed from symptoms.** Second consecutive session in this family to refute an inherited prescription (S171's was the scanner trim). | Session 172 (2026-07-27) — the `CELL_INFO` fence-token grammar | When picking up any filed item that names a fix. | 196 | **One upstream tool can carry several grammars for what looks like one concept, and "be faithful to quarto" is meaningless until you say WHICH one.** Quarto 1.7.33 recognises a fenced `{lang}` block with THREE different regexes: `breakQuartoMd`'s cell recognizer (`[=A-Za-z]+` — letters and `=`, no digits), `languagesInMarkdown`'s engine-selection scan (`[a-zA-Z0-9_]+` — digits and underscore, no `=`), and a jupyter kernelspec variant. They disagree on real input in BOTH directions: `{python3}` is a LANGUAGE but not a CELL; `{=html}` is a CELL but not a LANGUAGE. So the two of ours that mirror them must ALSO disagree, and consolidating them — which a BACKLOG item and a src docstring both proposed — would regress both directions at once. **A shared-looking concept is not one rule; before unifying two regexes, check whether the upstream tool unified them.** This is S171's two-partitioner finding recurring in a different family within one session of it, which suggests it is a property of quarto rather than a coincidence: check for a second grammar BEFORE assuming the one you found is the one that governs. | Session 172 (2026-07-27) — the `CELL_INFO` fence-token grammar | Before consolidating two similar-looking parsers/regexes, or before calling a transcription "faithful". Name the upstream function each one mirrors. |
| 197 | **A control that fails is worth more than a probe that passes, and the cheapest control is a row whose answer you already know.** Three of this session's ~85 renders were harness errors, and every one was caught by a control rather than by inspection. | 200 | **When adding a type-check project, the compiler options are the deliverable and they must be measured as a matrix — the "obvious" flags this project had been passing along for ten sessions inject phantom errors into `src/`.** Every handoff since S162 carried `npx tsc --noEmit --skipLibCheck --target es2022 --module esnext --moduleResolution bundler --strict <file>` as the way to check a unit test. | 201 | **A gate nothing runs is the same hole with extra files — pin REACHABILITY through the npm script graph, not the spelling of one script.** The valuable half of this deliverable was not `tsconfig.unit.json`; it was that `check-types` invokes it and `compile` invokes `check-types`, which is what silently puts the gate on `package`, `vscode:prepublish`, `test:integration` and `test:lsp`. | 203 | **An ignore file is a DENYLIST, so the packaged set is allow-by-default — the durable fix is to invert the default, not to add one more line.** `.vscodeignore` ships anything new at the repo root unless a human remembers to exclude it, and `vsce package` reports a clean exit either way, so no build, test, type-check or packaging step can observe the mistake. Closing the filed item costs two `.vscodeignore` lines; closing the CLASS costs a gate that asserts the packaged set against an EXPLICIT top-level allowlist, so a new root entry reds instead of shipping. **The evidence that this is not theoretical: the gate's very first run caught its own author's brand-new `check-package.js`, which would have shipped exactly as `tsconfig.unit.json` did.** Assert the reverse too — every allowlisted root must be PRESENT — because an over-broad exclusion that drops `dist/` produces a `.vsix` that packages, publishes and installs perfectly cleanly. | Session 174 (2026-08-01) — the `scratchpad/` `.vsix` packaging leak | Any project with a denylist-style ignore file governing a distributable artifact. | 204 | **Before wiring a gate into a tool's lifecycle, MEASURE which of its commands invoke that lifecycle — the answer chooses the wiring point and can rule out a recursion hazard.** Pointing `vscode:prepublish` at a marker-writing probe and running each command: `vsce package` ran it (1 marker), `vsce ls` did not (0). Both halves were load-bearing. The first made `vscode:prepublish` a strictly stronger wiring point than the project's own `package` script, because vsce walks that edge itself — so even a bare `npx vsce package`, which never touches any npm script of ours, now runs the gate. The second is what made the design possible at all: the gate shells out to `vsce ls` from inside `vscode:prepublish`, which would have been an infinite recursion had `vsce ls` also run the prepublish step. Neither fact is in the tool's docs at the point of use, both took one probe. **A reachability pin must then model the implicit edge**, since no `npm run` appears in it. | Session 174 (2026-08-01) — the `scratchpad/` `.vsix` packaging leak | Any time a gate is wired into a third-party tool's lifecycle hook rather than into your own script chain. | 205 | **An oracle for "what actually ships" must be the packaging tool's own answer, and its faithfulness to the real artifact must be measured, not assumed.** Re-implementing `.vscodeignore` matching rules in a test would be the hand-transcription this project has been burned by repeatedly. That measurement is what justifies a ~7s oracle over a ~36s one producing a 30 MB artifact — **and the renames would have silently broken an allowlist spelled from the archive's names.** The re-derivation command belongs in the script, for the next dependency bump. | Session 174 (2026-08-01) — the `scratchpad/` `.vsix` packaging leak | Whenever a check uses a fast proxy for an expensive ground truth. | 217 | **A filed item's CAUTION is a hypothesis too, not only its prescribed fix site — and this one's premise was measurably false.** Learning #214 (S177) established that a filed item's FIX SITE is a hypothesis while the defect is the fact. This session met the same shape one layer deeper: the item's ⚠ CONFOUND warning said adopting quarto's `\s*` fence indent would "make a fence inside an indented code block a cell for the outline, run-cell, virtual documents and highlighting, which is the widening direction" — i.e. it named the *reason not to fix it*. Measurement refutes the premise: a fully indented ```` ```{r} ```` fence+body, placed after a paragraph and a blank line so it sits in genuine CommonMark indented-code context, is **EXECUTED by knitr** — a `6 * 7` body printed `[1] 42` into the rendered HTML. There is no "documentation idiom" for Quarto to protect: an author cannot show a cell as literal example text by indenting it (only a fence-LESS indented block is literal). A list-nested indented cell renders as a properly highlighted code cell, which makes indented cells a legitimate idiom we were blind to at 4+ spaces. The prior decision the caution rested on — Learning #14(b), where an adversarial review ADDED the 3-space cap — was reached by CommonMark reasoning before anyone measured quarto, and its two test pins survive today only because their fixture's fence is UNCLOSED (a shape quarto also declines to treat as a cell). **A caution inherits the evidence grade of the argument behind it, and "CommonMark says X" is not evidence about a tool that does not read CommonMark.** | Before accepting a filed item's reason NOT to do something — a CONFOUND, a "widening direction" warning, a trade said to be settled — ask what measurement it rests on and whether that measurement was ever taken. A caution derived from a spec, a sibling tool, or a prior review's reasoning is a hypothesis. Three renders settled this one. Also: when a documented decision's test pins still pass after you contradict its stated premise, find out WHY they pass — here it was a property of the fixture, not of the rule, and saying so in the comment is what stops the next reader inheriting the wrong generalisation. |
| 218 | **The first GREEN can be green for the wrong reason, and only asking "why did that pass?" finds it.** This session's opening RED→GREEN cycle went green on the first implementation attempt — widening the cell fence OPENER made the filed lost true positive flag exactly as predicted. It was wrong: `FENCE_CLOSE` caps indentation at 3 too, so the indented cell never CLOSED, ran to end-of-document as an unterminated cell, and its option line was emitted by the unterminated-cell path rather than by a real cell. The test asserted the right value against the wrong mechanism. One `findAllCells` call (`endLine` was the document's last line, and the closing fence sat inside `code`) exposed it in seconds. Had the session moved on, the closer would have shipped unwidened and the very next document with a cell after an indented one would have lost its flags — a defect the passing pin would have actively concealed. **A passing test tells you the assertion holds; it does not tell you which code path produced it.** | Under strict TDD, when a GREEN arrives on the first try — especially for a change you expected to need more than one edit — spend one call inspecting the intermediate state (the parsed model, the built object, the span) and confirm the mechanism is the one you intended. Budget it exactly when the cycle feels easy: an unexpectedly cheap GREEN is the signal, not a reward. Cf. Learning #197's control discipline, applied to your own implementation instead of to the measurement harness. |
| 219 | **Two mutants survived a 1636-test suite AND a 121-document corpus, and the one no test could see was the "harmless tightening".** S177's Learning #216 said to mutate at the level the user experiences; this session did, and found the panel still had holes. (a) Removing the `CELL_INFO` gate — so the fence widening reaches PLAIN fences as well as cells — survived every unit test, including **the control written for exactly that mutant**, and was caught only by an oracle row. The reason is worth more than the fix: the unit fixture's plain fence had an *indented* closer, so the mutant found no 0–3-space closer and declined for the wrong reason. The killing shape needs a COLUMN-0 fence further down for the mutant to grab, at which point the indented plain fence opens a region that swallows a real cell whole. (b) Narrowing quarto's indent class `\s` to `[ \t]` — which reads as tidying, since who indents with anything else — survived unit AND corpus entirely, yet a FORM FEED, a VERTICAL TAB and a **NO-BREAK SPACE** indent each render **exit 1**, so it would have lost three real true positives in silence. NBSP is not exotic in practice: it is what pasting indented code out of a rendered web page produces. A third mutant (accepting tilde fences) was confirmed EQUIVALENT for the cell surface by measurement (`    ~~~{r}` renders exit 0) and deliberately left unkilled rather than pinned for symmetry. | When mutating, do not stop at the mutants you predicted would die. Include the ones that look like harmless tightenings — a narrowed character class, a dropped guard you believe redundant — because those are the ones no one writes a test for. And when a mutant survives a control you wrote FOR it, do not add a second assertion to the same fixture: work out which property of the fixture let it escape, and build the shape that has the opposite property. Finally, distinguish a surviving mutant that is EQUIVALENT (prove it by measurement, then record why it is unkilled) from one that is a hole. |
| 220 | **Two divergences can be one defect, and fixing half of it moves the symptom without moving the flag.** The filed item named the fence CLOSER's LENGTH rule (CommonMark allows a longer closer; quarto's `breakQuartoMd` requires `=== inCode`) and, separately, an UNTERMINATED column-0 cell. Tightening `isCloser` alone was measured to leave the user-visible flag **byte-identical** at `7:cache=banana` — it only moved the document off the closed path and onto the unterminated one, where the other defect emitted the same option anyway. The item said so ("the 4/3 case is the unterminated path wearing a different hat"), which is why it said do them together; what was worth doing was *demonstrating* it rather than trusting it, because the demonstration is also what proves the first RED could not have gone green by accident. Assert at the surface the user sees — a cell-SHAPE assertion would have gone green on the half-fix. | When a filed item says two defects must be fixed together, spend one run proving the half-fix is a no-op before building the whole one. It converts the item's claim into your own measurement, and it tells you which surface the RED must assert on: the one that does NOT move when only half the cause is removed. |
| 221 | **The fix that retires a false positive can introduce a regression on a DIFFERENT surface of the same document, and the pin for the first surface cannot see it.** Making a cell fence decline to open when quarto would not close it retired the filed cardinal FP — and the flag pin went green. But the leftover mismatched fence, no longer consumed as a closer, then opened a PLAIN region under the old "an unclosed fence runs to end of document" rule and swallowed every heading below it, on a document `quarto render` emits as a real `<h1>`. The flag surface was silent either way, so nothing in the RED→GREEN cycle could have caught it; it surfaced only from inspecting `findAllCells`/`findHeadings` on the same bytes right after the green. The resolution generalised the rule rather than special-casing the leftover: measured with `quarto pandoc -f markdown -t html`, pandoc's `markdown` — quarto's dialect, NOT `commonmark` — requires a fence to be CLOSED or it is not a code block, at line 0, after a blank, and mid-paragraph alike. So both fence kinds take the same lookahead and only the closer's LENGTH rule stays split. | After a GREEN on a change to a SHARED model, re-read the other views over that model on the same input before moving on — headings as well as cells, regions as well as flags. And when the repair needs a new rule, check whether the general rule is the measured one: here "every fence needs a closer" was both simpler than the special case and what the renderer actually does. Cf. Learning #218 — same discipline, applied to a surface the test never asserted on rather than to the path it took. |
| 222 | **A performance refactor can silently make the rule it supports untestable, and only a mutant shows it.** Extending the closer lookahead from indented cells to every fence made `scanRegions` quadratic — measured 0.7 ms → **162 ms per scan** on a document of 2000 unclosed openers, on the editor's debounced hot path, while a realistic 200-cell document stayed at 0.4 ms because the scan stops at each cell's own closer. That is the shape of a cost that ships unnoticed. Indexing the closers by `(isCell, char, length)` fixed it (2.1 ms) and was behaviour-preserving — green before and after. But the index now decides whether a cell OPENS, so reverting `isCloser`'s cell branch to `>=` became invisible: that mutant survived 1650 unit tests AND 130 corpus documents, because the comparison only decides WHICH line closes an already-open cell, and the two answers differ **only** when a longer run sits ABOVE the exact closer. No such document existed anywhere. | When a refactor moves a decision from one place to another — even a pure, green, behaviour-preserving one — re-run the mutants for the rule it supports, not just the tests. A rule can keep working while every pin on it stops discriminating. And when the surviving mutant is the deliverable's own headline rule, the missing document is usually the one where the two implementations' answers first diverge: construct that, do not add another assertion to a fixture that cannot see it. |
| 223 | **A filed fix that is stated as "one line" can be measurably wrong in the direction that deletes user data — and the cheapest way to find out is to build it and score it before writing a test.** The item prescribed "a one-line adjacency test in `computeRegions` (a heading needs a blank or a region boundary above it)". Applied to a throwaway copy of `src` and scored against the real renderer, that rule moved **10 documents toward quarto and 5 away**: a heading below a thematic break, a pipe-table row, an indented code block, a link-reference definition or a raw HTML block is REAL, and the naive rule deletes it. On this repo's own 108 markdown/qmd files it deletes `SESSION_NOTES.md`'s "Session 83 Handoff Evaluation" heading outright. This is Learning #214/#217 (a filed item's fix site, and its caution, are hypotheses) reaching its third consecutive session on this scanner — but with a new and cheaper technique: **patch a scratchpad COPY of the source and replay it over a measured corpus BEFORE committing to a rule.** Building the candidate is research, not implementation; it costs minutes and it is the only thing that turns "this prescription sounds right" into a number. Apply whenever a filed item names its own fix. |
| 224 | **When the correct rule needs block-level state a scanner does not have, make the approximation's ERROR DIRECTION one-way and say so in the code.** Pandoc's real rule is "a heading may not interrupt an OPEN PARAGRAPH", which needs block structure this model deliberately does not track (no list context, no indented-code blocks, no tables). Rather than build that, `CLOSES_PARAGRAPH` is a permissive per-line predicate: a line it MISSES deletes a real heading (forbidden), a line it matches too EAGERLY merely retains a pre-existing phantom (permitted). Choosing the direction, writing it into the docstring as load-bearing, and pinning the retained phantoms as KNOWN RESIDUAL turns an approximation into a stated contract — a future reader tightening a pattern to close a residual now knows exactly which way they are trading. The measured cost is real and worth quoting: the "precise table" variant closes one more phantom and deletes one real heading. |
| 225 | **An extension you are relying on may be a DEFAULT the document can switch off — check for the override before shipping a rule built on it.** `blank_before_header` is on by default in pandoc's `markdown`, and every measurement in this session assumed it. It is not an invariant: a front-matter `from: markdown-blank_before_header`, `markdown_strict`, `gfm` or `commonmark` — or a nested per-format `format:`/`  html:`/`    from:` — really does bring the heading back, all four verified on the real render path. Without a bail the whole change becomes a real-heading deletion on those documents, the one direction it must never take. The bail keys on the KEY's presence rather than resolving the dialect, so it fails CLOSED. Note the adversarial pass proposed `reader:` as a sibling spelling and **measurement refuted it** — quarto rejects that key outright (exit 1), so shipping the pattern would have been unmeasured surface. Generalises past this defect: whenever a rule rests on a toolchain default, grep for the document-level knob that changes it. |
| 226 | **A table-driven pin can be ineffective for the exact row it was written for, and a mutant is what tells you.** The exemption table had a row "a heading below a setext underline IS a heading" whose fixture put the underline at `consecutiveBody === 1` — where the SETEXT branch consumes it and clears the paragraph flag itself. The `=+` pattern the row existed to test was therefore never executed, and deleting that pattern survived the whole suite. Two sibling mutants escaped the same way: every fence fixture had a blank line above the fence, making the fence's own reset pure redundancy in all of them; and every glued-heading fixture had only ONE heading under its paragraph, so "a declined heading resets the paragraph" was unobservable. The repair is never to bolt an assertion onto the ineffective fixture — it is to build the document where the two implementations FIRST diverge (indent a line so the underline is not consumed; delete the blank above the fence; put two headings under one paragraph). This is S178's Learning #219 recurring with a different disguise; treat "my control passes" as unproven until a mutant dies on it. |
| 227 | **Two predicates that answer the same question can have OPPOSITE safety polarity, and the instinctive move is to unify them.** `CLOSES_PARAGRAPH` (S180) and `OPENS_FRESH_BLOCK` (S181) both answer "is this line block-level?", sit twenty lines apart, and are individually correct. Substituting one for the other type-checks, reads better, and manufactures **24 phantom headings** — measured. The reason is that they point in opposite directions: a pattern MISSING from `CLOSES_PARAGRAPH` deletes an ATX heading (so that list must be permissive), while a pattern wrongly PRESENT in `OPENS_FRESH_BLOCK` invents a setext heading (so this one must be restrictive). Eight of the eleven `CLOSES_PARAGRAPH` patterns are wrong for the setext direction. The consolidation instinct is strong enough — and the duplication obvious enough — that a docstring alone will not hold; the guard has to be a mutant-killing test that fails on the substitution. | Before reusing an existing predicate for a new decision, ask which DIRECTION each error runs in, not whether the predicate is correct. If the two callers' failure modes are opposite (one over-reports, the other under-reports), they need separate lists no matter how similar the patterns look — and the anti-unification needs an executable guard, because the next reader will see duplication and try to remove it. |
| 228 | **A rule of the form "construct X resets the parser state" is usually wrong at X itself; the reset belongs to the line BELOW X.** The obvious implementation of "a block line starts a fresh paragraph" resets the counter at the block line. Measured, that deletes headings the previous build got right: a setext underline directly below a block line claims THAT line, overriding the block reading — `    indented code` / `===` renders `<h1>indented code</h1>`, and `***`, `___`, `##`, `| a | b |`, `[x]: url` and `\clearpage` all behave identically. The correct shape is a ONE-LINE DEFERRAL (`pendingFreshBlock`), not a reset: the construct keeps its own claim and only confers freshness on its successor. The first implementation passed its own RED and the whole new test block; only a PRE-EXISTING pin from the previous session caught it. | When implementing "construct X changes what follows", write the state change as a deferral to the next line and then ASK what the construct line itself should still be allowed to be. Test both: one fixture with the trigger directly above the construct, one with a line between. A rule verified only on the "line between" shape cannot see the regression. |
| 229 | **The corpus that scored the design decision can be systematically blind to the shape the fix breaks.** S181 scored six candidate rules over 95 rendered documents and presented 0-phantom / 0-repo-change figures to the operator before writing code — and every one of those documents had the underline TWO lines below the construct, because that is the shape the filed defect described. Not one had it directly below, which is exactly where the chosen rule regressed. The blindness was structural: the corpus was generated from the defect's own template, so it inherited the defect's framing. What found it was the existing suite (an unrelated pin from the previous session) plus a follow-up measurement round, not the corpus. | After a generated corpus produces a clean score, enumerate the DIMENSIONS the generator held fixed (here: the distance between the construct and the trigger) and generate a round that varies each one. A corpus built from the filed item's template tests the filed item's framing, not the rule. Treat a 0-regression score from a single-template corpus as unproven until a second template agrees. |
| 230 | **A mutation applied by regex can silently hit the wrong occurrence, and it reports as "the mutant survived".** S181's M8 deleted "a thematic break" with `s/^.*a thematic break.*\n//m` and hit `CLOSES_PARAGRAPH`'s row twenty lines above the intended one, reporting the mutant as surviving. Re-applied precisely, it died on two tests. The misfire was worth keeping, though: it incidentally proved that S180's own thematic-break entry is pinned by nothing (its removal survives all 1714 tests) — a real finding that arrived from a broken tool. | Make every mutation assert WHAT it changed, not just THAT the file changed — print the removed/replaced line and eyeball it, or anchor the edit to the enclosing symbol. A mutation script that only checks `diff` is non-empty will report false survivors, which is the failure direction that makes you stop looking. And when a misfire produces a surprising result, score it before discarding it. |
| 231 | **A scoring metric can be blind in exactly the way a corpus can, and the blindness hides the one direction that must never happen.** S182 scored six candidate rules by comparing our heading SET to quarto's for set EQUALITY, and read off "0 rows broken" for the variant it was about to ship. The metric was wrong: four documents already DIFFERED for an unrelated pre-existing reason (pandoc swallows an ATX line into a setext heading, so quarto's text is `# Heading Above` where ours is `Heading Above`), and a row that is already unequal cannot become "more unequal" under set equality. Re-scored per-HEADING — multiset difference, reporting `quarto-has-we-miss` and `we-have-quarto-doesnt` separately — the same variant was DELETING FIVE REAL HEADINGS. Learning #229 says a corpus generated from the filed item's template inherits its framing; this is the same failure one level up, in the instrument rather than the sample. | When scoring a change against an oracle, make the metric report the two error DIRECTIONS separately and at the finest granularity the domain has (per heading, per diagnostic, per symbol), never a single equal/not-equal verdict per document. Then check the metric can actually SEE a regression: take a row the change is known to break and confirm the number moves. A pass/fail-per-document metric silently masks any change inside a row that already failed. |
| 232 | **The mutation pass is not a formality after the corpus agrees — it found two defects that 220 rendered documents and 1724 unit tests both passed.** S182's implementation scored 79 phantoms removed / 0 added / 0 headings lost over the full corpus, with the repo control byte-identical, and was one commit from close-out. The adversarial pass then produced five survivors of which TWO were not missing pins but live bugs: (a) the ATX-adjacency regex was copied as `^ {0,3}` from `SETEXT_H1`, where the indent is correct — measured, the pandoc swallow needs column 0, so ` ===` (one space) invented a heading; (b) the adjacency flag was cleared at the FOOT of the scanner loop, which every `continue` path skips, so it leaked across blank lines and whole fenced regions and invented a heading under `# H` / blank / `===`. Neither shape existed in the corpus, because the corpus was generated with the run always DIRECTLY below the heading and always at column 0. | Run the mutants even when the corpus is green and the diff looks finished — especially then. A mutant that "survives" is a question about the SHIPPED code, not only about the test suite: ask whether the mutant is in fact MORE correct than what you wrote before assuming it merely needs a pin. Two of S182's five survivors answered yes. |
| 233 | **When a new predicate borrows a regex from a neighbouring one, the borrowed part is unmeasured — and the neighbour's correctness does not transfer.** `SETEXT_UNDERLINE_RUN` was written by copying `SETEXT_H1`'s ` {0,3}` indent allowance, which is right for a setext underline and wrong for the ATX-swallow the new predicate models: measured, `# Heading Above` / `===` renders a real heading below, and ` ===` renders none. The same session had just documented `CLOSES_PARAGRAPH` and `OPENS_FRESH_BLOCK` as having opposite polarity and never to be unified (Learning #227) — and then quietly inherited a fragment across the same kind of boundary anyway, because copying a sub-expression does not feel like unifying two predicates. | Treat every borrowed sub-expression as unmeasured until you measure it on the NEW predicate's own question. When you copy an anchor, a quantifier or a character class from a sibling regex, write down which document proves it belongs, and mutate it — the anchor and the indent allowance are exactly what a mutation pass tests cheaply. Learning #227's "do not unify" applies at fragment granularity, not only at predicate granularity. |
| 234 | **A permissive list whose docstring says "when in doubt, add the pattern" will accumulate rows nobody ever measured, and one of them will be wrong in the opposite direction.** S180 justified `CLOSES_PARAGRAPH`'s permissiveness correctly — a row it MISSES deletes a real heading, a row it matches too eagerly only retains a phantom — and scored the list as a WHOLE against a corpus, never row by row. S182 measured two of its rows: the `=+` row is wrong in every position where it is reachable (30 phantoms), and the thematic-break row is wrong in a whole class the corpus never contained (34 phantoms), because every document in S180's own construct table had a BLANK LINE above the construct, so the open-paragraph half of the behaviour was never exercised at all. The safety argument was sound; the evidence for the individual rows did not exist. | A list-level score is not a row-level score. When adding to a permissive list, record the document that proves EACH row, and pin each row separately — otherwise "when in doubt, add it" becomes a licence that later sessions cannot audit. And when a table fixes one element of its shape across every row (a blank line above the construct, the underline two lines down), that element is untested for the whole list: enumerate what the generator held constant before trusting the score. |
| 235 | **A measurement instrument can be wrong in the DIRECTION THAT MAKES A DANGEROUS CHANGE LOOK SAFE, and that is the failure to hunt for.** S183 inherited S182's `struct.sh` and found two defects in it. The first was cosmetic: `<pre>` was tokenised as an empty `p:` because the tag alternation listed `p` before `pre`. The second was not: the alternation also included CONTAINER tags (`blockquote`/`ul`/`ol`/`table`) paired with a lazy `(.*?)</\1>`, so ONE match consumed an entire container and every heading nested inside it vanished from the data. A document rendering `<blockquote><h2>quoted one</h2><h1 id="atx-below">ATX Below</h1></blockquote>` was recorded as having NO headings — turning "quarto emits a heading here" into "quarto emits none", which is precisely what makes a heading-DELETING change score clean. Under the corrupted data the candidate rule read "0 real headings lost"; re-measured, it lost one. Learning #231 says the metric can be blind like the corpus; this is one level deeper still — the metric was FINE, the PARSER feeding it was lying, and it lied asymmetrically. | Before trusting an extractor, feed it a document whose correct answer you already know FROM A DIFFERENT SOURCE and diff the two. Specifically, when a regex extractor pairs an opening tag with a lazy closing tag, ask which tags can CONTAIN the thing you are counting — any container in the alternation silently swallows its contents. Prefer an extraction that cannot consume (opening tags only, or a scan restricted to non-nesting elements like `<h1-6>`). And when an adversary complains about your instrument rather than your conclusion, read that clause twice: S183 dismissed "the prescribed one-liner silently UNDERCOUNTS" on first pass as a complaint about one row. |
| 236 | **A corpus of DEGENERATE one-line constructs measures the REGEX, not the CONSTRUCT — and the two disagree in the direction that deletes rows.** S183's first corpus put exactly one construct line in every document, which made twelve spellings look as though they NEVER close a paragraph: a lone `+`, a bare `:::`, `a \| b`, `...`. Read literally that says "remove these rows". The second corpus made each construct WELL FORMED — a three-line grid table, a `:::` div that is actually closed, a real pipe table with its delimiter row — and the same rows flipped to "correct, but must be gated". A one-line `:::` is not a fenced div; it is prose that the row's regex happens to match. Scoring it answers a question about the pattern, not about the construct the pattern exists to recognise. | When auditing a list of block-construct patterns, generate BOTH a degenerate and a well-formed corpus and score them separately. Where they disagree, the well-formed one decides whether the ROW belongs and the degenerate one decides whether the PATTERN is too wide — they are different defects with different fixes (gating vs narrowing), and conflating them either deletes a correct row or leaves a phantom. |
| 237 | **When a per-line scanner gates on block state, the construct's CLOSING line is the case that breaks it — and it breaks in the forbidden direction.** S183 gated `CLOSES_PARAGRAPH` on `paragraphOpen` and every intermediate variant deleted real headings, all for one structural reason: a closer follows its own construct's CONTENT. A div's `:::` sits under the div's body text; a YAML block's `...` sits under its last key; a block quote's lazy continuation sits under quoted prose. To a scanner with no block nesting, a closer therefore ALWAYS looks like it faces an open paragraph, so the gate suppresses exactly the lines whose whole job is to end a block. The fix is not a better gate but an exemption list tested BEFORE the bail — and the exemptions are not discretionary: each was measured to cost real headings. | Before gating any per-line predicate on "is a paragraph open", enumerate which of its patterns can appear as a construct's CLOSING delimiter, and exempt those. Then ask the same question about containers (block quotes, list items), where the enclosing construct — not the line — decides. If the model tracks no nesting, the honest move is to suspend the gate in that context and RETAIN its phantoms, because retaining a phantom is permitted and deleting a real heading is not. |
| 238 | **An untargeted `str.replace` in a patch or mutation script can silently rewrite a SECOND predicate, and the build will not tell you.** S183's variant builder replaced the fragment `if (paragraphOpen) {\n return false;\n }` to add a guard. That fragment occurs TWICE in `model.ts`: once in `closesParagraph` and once in `opensFreshBlock` — the predicate Learning #227 exists to keep separate, which has opposite safety polarity and no such parameter. The patch rewrote both. It was caught by READING the patched file, not by any gate. This is Learning #230 (a mutation can hit the wrong occurrence) recurring in the variant-BUILDING tool rather than the mutation tool, which is the half nobody instruments. | Anchor every programmatic edit to a string you have asserted is UNIQUE (`assert s.count(anchor) == 1`), or replace a whole named function body rather than a fragment inside it. Apply this to variant builders and refactoring scripts, not only to mutation scripts — and after any scripted patch, diff the result against its source and read the diff, because "it compiled" and "the tests pass" both survive a second predicate being quietly rewritten. |
| 239 | **A clean score over a large corpus is evidence about the corpus, not about the change — and "zero regressions over N documents" is the sentence that precedes a shipped deletion.** Session 184 narrowed two rows, scored **zero headings lost over 476 rendered documents across four independently-designed corpora**, and was deleting **31 real headings**. The corpora were not sloppy: they varied row, construct-ness, position and container, and each axis was written down in the generator's docstring. They simply had no `<meta>`, no `<svg>`, no `\vspace{1em}`. The defect is structural, not careless: a corpus is generated from the shapes its author already thought of, so it can only ever confirm the hypothesis that built it. **When to apply:** never let a corpus score be the last word on a change in the forbidden direction. Before shipping, spend a pass whose *only* goal is to find inputs the corpus does not contain — ideally generated by something that did not design the corpus. Report the score and the sweep as two separate pieces of evidence; the score alone is a statement about coverage. This is Learning #229's "a corpus inherits its generator's framing" escalated: S181 was blind on distance, S182 on adjacency, S183 on containers, S184 on tag and macro identity — four consecutive sessions, each blind on an axis it had not enumerated, the last two *after* writing the enumeration down. |
| 240 | **Some predicates are decided by a NAME, not by a SHAPE, and no regex over the line can ever be right — recognising that is the finding.** Session 184 tried to narrow `CLOSES_PARAGRAPH`'s raw-HTML row by tag form and its raw-TeX row by "has braces". Both are measurably impossible: `<ins>x</ins>` opens a block and `<em>x</em>` does not; `\vspace{1em}` is a raw block and `\textbf{bold}` is inline. The two lines in each pair are indistinguishable by shape — pandoc consults a *table of names*. A third case, the raw-TeX row's indent, is decided by the CONTAINING BLOCK's content column: correct at column 0 at top level, correct at column 2 inside a list item, and a per-line predicate can see neither. **When to apply:** when a narrowing keeps needing "just one more exception", stop and ask whether the distinguishing information is present in the line at all. If it is a name or an enclosing context, the honest deliverable is the measurement plus a filed item to transcribe the real table (as Session 172 transcribed `breakQuartoMd`'s cell recognizer) — not a cleverer regex. Shipping the cleverer regex is how a clean score turns into deleted content. |
| 241 | **Establish PROVENANCE per finding, not per session: probe every claimed defect against the previous build AND the one before it.** Session 184's adversarial sweep returned 121 divergent documents, and the instinct is to treat them as one pile. Rendering each against three trees — pre-S183, S183-shipped, current — partitioned them mechanically into 50 *this session introduced*, 17 *Session 183 introduced*, and 17 *pre-existing*, and that partition drove every decision afterwards: what to revert, what to fix, what to file, and what the commit message could honestly claim. It also corrected an agent's report, which had described a line-block deletion as a general limitation when it was specifically Session 183's gate. **When to apply:** any time a review or sweep returns a batch of defects in code with recent history. `git archive <sha> src` into a scratch tree and re-run the probe; it costs minutes and converts "here are 121 problems" into "here is the one you caused". Without it a session either takes blame for inherited defects or, far worse, ships its own inside the noise. |
| 242 | **A control assertion earns its keep by failing when the fix over-corrects — write the opposite direction into the same test.** Session 184 twice shipped a change that its own *positive* assertions passed and its *control* assertions caught: deleting the `<!--` alternative removed two phantoms and silently lost the "line is nothing but comments" headings, and the column-0 raw-TeX narrowing removed three phantoms and lost the list-item heading. In both cases the corpus score was clean or improving and the control was the only thing that objected. **When to apply:** for any change in a two-directional predicate, every test that asserts "X no longer happens" must carry, in the same `it()`, an assertion that "Y still happens" — chosen as the nearest shape on the other side of the new boundary, not a distant sanity check. The pairing is what makes the boundary a boundary rather than a slope. |
| 243 | **A filed item's SYMPTOM and its CAUSE carry different warranties — reproduce the first, but re-derive the second from a ladder that varies the axis the item names.** Session 184 filed "an INDENTED HTML block deletes the heading below it" and explained it as a `{0,3}`-versus-4-space boundary where "the gate then suppresses the closer". The symptom reproduced in fifteen minutes; both halves of the cause were refuted by a nine-rung indent ladder rendered against the real path. Pandoc's html-block rule does not look at leading whitespace **at all** — `<div>` against a two-line open paragraph releases the heading at 0/1/3/4/5/6/8 spaces, one tab, two tabs, space+tab and tab+space alike, while `<span>`/`<em>`/`<not-a-tag` release it at none of them — so the repair was to DELETE the indent cap, not extend it; and no closer is needed, because the opener alone deletes. An implementer who coded the stated mechanism would plausibly have written `(?: {0,3}|\t| {4,})` and missed three real deletions. **When to apply:** before implementing any filed item, reproduce its symptom, then build the ladder that varies the axis its explanation names. If the answer is flat across that axis, the explanation is wrong and the fix is simpler than the item thinks. When you FILE an item, mark which part is measured symptom and which is inferred cause. |
| 244 | **An unmeasured "refinement" is a narrowing, and narrowing is the deleting direction — including the three keystrokes you add while writing the predicate.** Session 185 wrote `LINE_BLOCK_CONTINUATION` as `/^[ \t]+\S/`, reasoning that a continuation "must have content". Pandoc's rule is only that the line BEGINS with whitespace. The `\S` excluded every line whose sole content is whitespace `BLANK_LINE` (`/^[ \t]*$/`) does not recognise — a form feed, a vertical tab, a non-breaking space — ending the block early and deleting four real headings. It was caught by the session's own mutation pass, where the mutant that removed the clause proved MORE correct than the code (Learning #232 paying out on the author's own new code rather than on inherited code). **When to apply:** every clause of a new predicate needs its own rendered document, including the ones that feel like obvious hygiene. If you cannot name the document that would fail without a clause, the clause is a guess — and mutate your OWN new code, not just the code you inherited. |
| 245 | **`<main>`-scoped HTML extraction is unsound for any document containing a raw stray closer, and it fails in the direction that makes a deletion look safe.** A `</div>` in the markdown is emitted verbatim into quarto's HTML and terminates `<main>`'s element early, so a heading rendered after it falls OUTSIDE the captured region and the extractor reports NO HEADING for a document that really renders one. Session 185 found this by reading the raw HTML of a single anomalous row, then replaced the extractor with a whole-file scan validated against all 74 documents of the discovery corpus (it agreed on 73 and differed only where the raw grep confirmed it was right). This is the **third** extractor defect in this family — S182's container-swallowing lazy closer, S183's `<main>` scoping, S185's stray closers — and all three failed in the same direction. **When to apply:** treat the measurement harness as code under test. Before trusting any zero, ask what class of input would make the extractor silently under-report, and validate against a document whose answer you know from a second source. Keep the old column beside the new one so the two can be diffed per row. |
| 246 | **State that is STICKY by design needs its clearing rule enumerated against every block boundary, not just the obvious one — and the cost of getting it wrong is a recovery that silently never happens.** Session 185's `inPipeTable` flag disarms the line-block rule across a table's body rows, so unlike its top-of-loop sibling it cannot be cleared each line. It was cleared only at a blank line, and the adversarial sweep found that a comment, a fence or a heading between a table and a genuine line block left the guard armed — the heading below was never recovered, silently, because nothing distinguishes "correctly disarmed" from "still poisoned". The phantom risk of clearing it more eagerly was MEASURED before the change (a comment, a fence and a heading each really do end the table, so quarto renders the heading either way) and the flag now clears at every region boundary that already ends a block. **When to apply:** when adding sticky state to a per-line scanner, list every `continue` path and every block-ending branch, and decide the flag's fate at each one explicitly. A flag that only clears where you first thought of it is a flag with an unbounded blast radius, and the failure mode is invisible. |
| 247 | **A figure repeated in prose is not a measurement, and a "visible signal" that does not encode magnitude cannot detect drift.** Session 174 deliberately kept `scratchpad/` out of `.gitignore` so that `?? scratchpad/` would appear in every Phase 0 `git status` — explicitly "the only human-visible trace that ~97 MB of prior-session scratch lives here". Eleven sessions later the directory measured **2,600,816 KB (2.5 GB) across 52,796 files**, 26× the recorded figure, and the signal had not changed by one character, because git collapses an untracked directory to a single line whose text is identical at 97 MB and at 2.5 GB. The figure meanwhile propagated *unmeasured* into three more sites (`BACKLOG.md` twice, `CHANGELOG.md`, `.vscodeignore`), and S183's own receipt asserted "~112 MB" on a day `scratchpad/s183` alone was already 2.3 GB — wrong ~20× at the moment of authorship. Two of the S174 decision's three premises survived measurement intact; only the one that quietly assumed the signal was quantitative failed. **When to apply:** whenever a decision is justified by "this stays visible", ask what quantity the signal actually encodes and what change it would fail to show. A boolean presence indicator can only ever answer *whether*, never *how much* — so if the thing being watched is a magnitude, the visible signal must carry a number, and something must re-measure it on a schedule. Treat a figure inherited from a prior document as a claim with a date, not as a fact: re-measure before you repeat it, and never let it cross into a new document unmeasured. |
| 248 | **Gate an irreversible bulk delete on a PROVEN disjointness check against the live reference set — never on a pattern that merely looks disposable.** Session 186 pruned 2,332 MB (91%) of regenerable `quarto render` byproduct from `scratchpad/`. The tempting form is `find … -name '*_files' -delete`, and it would have been *correct here* — which is exactly why the discipline matters, because nothing in that command tells you so. The form actually used: extract every `scratchpad/` path token from every **tracked** file (78), keep those that still exist (38), enumerate the delete set as top-most directories only (2,768), assert that **zero** surviving citation lies inside any of them, delete, then **re-assert all 38 afterwards** — and only then run the build matrix to confirm it is byte-unchanged. The pre-check is what converts "this pattern looks like byproduct" into evidence; the post-check is what catches an enumeration bug in the pre-check. The gate also produced a finding the delete never would have: 40 of the 78 citations were **already dangling before the prune**. **When to apply:** any irreversible bulk operation — delete, move, rewrite — over a tree that other artifacts reference. Build the reference set from the *durable* side (tracked files, committed docs), prove disjointness before acting, re-prove survival after, and keep both counts in the commit message. A dry run that only prints what will be deleted answers the wrong question; the question is what will be *broken*. |
| 249 | **Do not cite ephemeral evidence from a durable document — the receipt outlives the evidence, and nothing notices.** This project's sessions measure in `scratchpad/` (untracked, unpruned, unbacked) and then cite those paths from `HANDOFFS.md`, `CHANGELOG.md`, `PROJECT_LEARNINGS.md` and `docs/planning/*.md`, all of which are committed and reviewable. Measured at Session 186: **40 of 78** such citations already resolved to nothing — including `scratchpad/S131-findings.md` and `scratchpad/S134-findings.md`, findings documents cited by two committed planning documents, and `scratchpad/ground-project.cjs` and `scratchpad/matrix.json`. No gate detects this: `git diff` shows only the present-side edit, `bin/check-handoff` checks structure not resolvability, and Phase 0 reconcile checks the ledger frontier, not citation targets. It is Learning #7 (cross-reference completeness) with the destination *outside* version control, which is why the authoring-time check never fires. **When to apply:** at the moment you write an evidence citation into a committed file, ask whether the destination is under version control. If it is not, either promote the artifact into the repo in that same commit (the S185 harness family is ~60 KB — the cost is nil) or inline the evidence — the numbers, the command, the salient output — into the document itself. A path into a scratch directory is a promise the repo cannot keep. |
| 250 | **Pandoc classifies raw HTML by TAG NAME from two sets, and the set NAMES are the rule — `blockTags` is block in every context, `eitherBlockOrInline` only where no paragraph is open.** Session 187 transcribed `Text.Pandoc.Readers.HTML.TagCategories` at pandoc 3.6.3 (the build quarto 1.7.33 bundles) and then measured every entry, six shapes across four contexts, 2,051 rendered documents. Against an open paragraph 98 openers and 100 closers interrupt; with none open, 115 and 117 — and the difference is exactly `eitherBlockOrInline` plus processing instructions. Three names are measured exceptions to the source: `!DOCTYPE` (pandoc's `isBlockTag` admits any `!`-prefixed name, but `htmlTag`'s own `isName` guard then rejects it), and `textarea`/`title` (in `blockTags`, yet RCDATA — an unclosed opener swallows the document as text, so it opens nothing while its CLOSER is block). | Session 187, implementing the pandoc tag transcription | Whenever a rule is transcribed from an upstream source: the source names the candidate set and the shape of the rule; it does not tell you what the installed toolchain does. Measure every entry, in every context the consumer reads it in — and expect the exceptions to be where two layers of the upstream disagree with each other. |
| 251 | **A filed item's MEASURED claim can be false, and "measured" is not a warranty — it warrants the SHAPE that was measured, not the rule inferred from it.** `BACKLOG.md` asserted, on the strength of Session 184's adversarial sweep, that "`<ins>x</ins>` opens a block and `<em>x</em>` does not" and listed thirteen further names as block openers. Session 185 earned the sibling lesson (a filed item's stated CAUSE may be wrong while its SYMPTOM is right); this one is sharper, because here the SYMPTOM itself did not reproduce. |
| 252 | **When a per-line predicate reads only the HEAD of the line, the TAIL is an axis no corpus you design will vary — because you will write every probe with the construct alone on its line.** Session 187 built five corpora over 191 tag names and 26 non-tag spellings, and every single probe put the tag by itself. The measured rule is that the line must END AT A `>` or contain no `>` at all. | Session 187's adversarial sweep | Whenever a predicate is anchored at one end of its input, treat the other end as an axis you cannot self-test. Delegate the corpus for it — the value of the sweep is precisely that its author does not share your framing. And re-check any narrowing you wrote by intuition inside the same session (Learning #232): the `[ \t]*` in this session's own RCDATA closer was three keystrokes and cost three real headings. |
| 253 | **An extractor that pairs an opening tag with its own closer is unsound the moment the PROBE tag can itself be that kind of tag.** Session 185's whole-file heading scan pairs `<hN …>` with `</hN>`. This is the FOURTH extractor defect in this family (S182 containers, S183 `<main>`-scoping, S185 stray closers, S187 h-tag probes) and all four point the same way: they make a heading look ABSENT, which makes a heading-deleting change look safe. | Session 187, validating its instrument | Validate the extractor against documents whose answer you know from another source BEFORE scoring anything with it (Learning #235) — and specifically against a document whose probe construct collides with the extractor's own pattern. Prefer a match that cannot nest (`<hN …>text</hN>` with no `<` in the text) over a paired scan whenever the corpus permits it. |
| 254 | **A guard whose two sides derive from the SAME input cannot detect a defect in that input.** The inherited renderer failed loudly on a row count that disagreed with its input file count — but both were computed from one glob, so when the directory did not exist the glob did not expand, `files` held the literal string, total was 1, one junk row was written, 1 == 1, and it printed `rendered 1 documents` and exited 0. Found by walking into it: a persisted shell working directory (S185's own gotcha) made the path wrong. | Session 187, hardening the S185 harness | When writing a "fails loudly" guard, ask what the two sides share. Add checks that are independent of the suspect input — here, that the directory exists and that the glob actually expanded. A guard that can only compare a value to itself is decoration. |
| 255 | **When two of your own records contradict each other about a construct, the missing variable is usually CONTEXT — measure the axis before believing either.** `RAW_TEX_ENV_OPEN`'s docstring stated, as measured, that a bare macro is INLINE against an open paragraph; `BACKLOG.md` stated, as measured, that the same macro names are BLOCKS. Both were true. Pandoc's raw-TeX rule is context-dependent exactly as its HTML rule is (S187), and the five names it puts in BOTH `treatAsBlock` and `treatAsInline` (`clearpage hspace newpage pagebreak vspace`) are the whole middle class. The apparent contradiction was the single most informative signal available at Orient, and it was sitting in the repo unread for four sessions. | When two committed claims about the same construct disagree and both cite measurement, do NOT adjudicate which is wrong. Look for the axis neither varied — context, arity, position, indentation — and measure that axis first. A contradiction between two careful measurements is evidence of a hidden variable, not of carelessness. |
| 256 | **A probe that gives every construct ONE argument is malformed for anything that takes two, and the failure is silent and points toward deletion.** The S188 discovery corpus probed each macro as `\NAME` and `\NAME{x}`. For a two-argument macro `\NAME{x}` is incomplete, so pandoc's parser fails, the token falls through to the INLINE path, and the name measures as "inline in every context" — landing it in an exclusion list that then DELETES its heading. 22 of 316 class-C candidates were blocks at their true arity, `\newcommand{\foo}{bar}` among them, and it is one of the eleven spellings the filed item names. Found by a control assertion, not by a sweep. | When building a corpus over a family of constructs that take arguments, vary ARITY as an axis, not just presence-of-argument.
