This is Learning #252's blind spot (the head/tail axis) generalized: ask what axes the corpus does not vary, not merely whether it varies the one you thought of. |
| 257 | **On a case-insensitive filesystem, a name-keyed corpus silently collapses and the survivor carries the WRONG name.** macOS is case-insensitive by default, so `P-bare-AA.qmd` and `P-bare-aa.qmd` are one file: 62 collision groups lost 124 documents from a 2,944-document corpus, and the file that remained held one name's content under the other's key — actively wrong data, not merely missing data. The render harness's own row-count guard passed, because it counted the files that exist. | Never key a corpus filename on a case-sensitive identifier. Use an index (`{i:04d}`) plus a mapping file, and make the scorer FAIL LOUDLY on any identifier with no row — the guard that catches this is on the SCORER, not on the renderer, because the renderer cannot know a file was never written. |
| 258 | **The risk asymmetry belongs in the DESIGN, not only in the commit message: put the strict rule on the widening and keep it off the narrowing.** S188's measured tail rule ("only whitespace or further non-inline macros may follow") is correct and removes ~50 phantoms — but carried into the heading-DELETING row it deleted `\vspace*{1em}`, `\vspace2` and `\newcommand{\foo}{bar}`, three of the eleven spellings the filed item itself names. The same rule is safe on the heading-RECOVERING row, where over-matching costs a phantom. | When one artefact feeds two predicates of opposite safety polarity, do not assume a refinement that helps one helps the other. Ship the strict form where over-matching costs a phantom; ship the permissive form where over-matching costs a heading. State which is which in the docstring so the next session does not "unify" them. |
| 259 | **A per-line predicate cannot see a containing block's content column — but this file is a SCANNER, and the sentence that said otherwise stopped five sessions from fixing the row.** Session 184 filed the raw-TeX indent defect with the conclusion "a per-line predicate cannot see a containing block's content column, so the wider row stays and its top-level phantoms are the price", and the test it left repeated it. The premise is true of a predicate and false of `computeRegions`, which already carried `paragraphOpen`, `inPipeTable`, `lineBlockOpen` and four other pieces of block state — adding `contentColumns` beside them removed **1,043 phantoms** across 4,125 rendered documents with zero new losses. When a filed item explains why something is impossible, check whether the impossibility is a property of the CONSTRUCT or of the shape the last session happened to write it in. Ask "what would have to be true for this to be possible?" before accepting a documented dead end. |
| 260 | **When one word names two rows that fail in OPPOSITE directions, a ranked handoff will point the next session at the wrong one.** S188's receipt ranked the ` {0,3}` INDENT item #1 because its sweep attributed the largest residual-loss family (7 of 23) to "container content column", and both things are called an indent problem. Re-scoring S188's own 223-document corpus after shipping the item measured it draining **zero** of those losses (21 before, 21 after; 2 phantoms removed): the item is phantom-removing on the class-B row, while every loss in the family is heading-DELETING on the class-A row, whose cap is too NARROW rather than too wide. The work was still worth doing — it just was not the work the ranking promised. **Re-score the predecessor's own corpus after shipping, not only your own**; it is the cheapest available test of whether the reason you were given was the real one, and it is the only way the ranking gets corrected instead of inherited. |
| 261 | **Assert only the axis under test: a corpus that varies two axes at once measures neither, and the failures look like code defects.** Three of Session 189's own first-draft pins failed, and all three were wrong assertions rather than wrong code — two put the HEADING at column 4+, where `ATX_HEADING`'s own ` {0,3}` loses it whatever the row under test decides, and one asserted an indent inside a list item was dead when it is really an indented code block. The fix was to hold the heading at column 0 while the MACRO swept, which is legitimate precisely because pandoc's `rawTeXBlock` ends with `spaces` and swallows the next line's indent. **When a new pin fails, re-render the document before touching the implementation** — quarto adjudicated all three, and the third answer (column 6 renders) contradicted what the author of the pin believed twice over. |
| 262 | **An unreferenced footnote definition renders NOTHING, so a corpus that never cites its own footnote reads "no container here" and concludes the container does not exist.** The first content-column sweep measured a footnote definition's column as 0 — meaning no container at all — on documents shaped `[^1]: note` / … with no `[^1]` reference anywhere. Pandoc drops the whole definition from the output, so every indent read "no heading" for a reason that had nothing to do with the axis. With a live `See[^1]` reference the column is **4**, and a rule built on the first reading would have deleted a real heading under every indented macro in a footnote. This is the SIXTH instrument defect in this family and the sixth pointing in the direction that makes a heading-deleting change look safe (S182 containers, S183 `<main>`, S185 stray closers, S187 h-tag probes, S188 case-collision, now this). **Before scoring a "no output" result, prove the construct reaches the output at all.** |
| 263 | **A cap that "guards" a construct may only be MASKING a blindness — and the control that tells them apart is the SAME document with the construct un-indented.** Session 190 removed `RAW_TEX_BLOCK_MACRO`'s ` {0,3}` cap and a blind adversarial sweep immediately produced three new phantoms: a class-A macro indented inside a multi-line inline code span, inside an RCDATA `<textarea>`, and inside a CDATA section. The instinctive reading is "the widening caused these, so narrow it back". The measured reading is the opposite: **the pre-S190 build already emitted all three phantoms with the macro at COLUMN 0.** The cap never modelled those regions; it merely happened to stop the row firing on the indented *spelling* of a document the scanner was getting wrong at every indent. Narrowing back would hide three phantoms and re-delete 556 real headings. **The rule: before attributing a new error to your change, re-run the same document with your change's trigger removed on the OLD build.** If the old build fails it too, the change did not cause the error — it changed which spellings reach it, and the real fix belongs to a different capability (here: tracking raw regions), which is a separate item under FM #26. Pinned as a column-0 control beside each residual so a future region-tracking fix is detectable rather than silent. |
| 264 | **Quarto's auto-generated `<h2>Footnotes</h2>` is not a source heading, and counting it inflates the LOST column of every corpus that uses a footnote container.** Session 190's container corpus scored 27 residual losses in the footnote container — 9 each at class A, B and C, i.e. *every* document regardless of the macro under test, which is the shape of an instrument defect rather than a model defect. All 27 were quarto's auto-generated Footnotes section, emitted into the rendered HTML for any document with a live footnote reference; nothing in the source is a heading there and the model is right to report none. Across the session's seven corpora **111 of 362 residual losses were this artifact** (31%). ⚠ This is the SEVENTH instrument defect in this family, and the first whose polarity points the *safe* way — it makes the model look worse, not better — which is exactly why it could have survived indefinitely: nobody audits a number that flatters nothing. It still matters in both directions, because a real loss in one of those documents is hidden inside the same count. **The rule: before reporting a residual-loss family, look at ONE of its documents.** A loss that appears at every value of the axis under test is not about that axis. Note this compounds with Learning #262 — a footnote corpus needs a live reference to measure the container at all, and the live reference is precisely what summons the artifact. |
| 265 | **The Workflow tool's `args` arrives as a STRING even when passed as a JSON object, so `args.foo` is `undefined` and agents silently write to a directory literally named `undefined/`.** Session 189 self-assessed this exact defect; Session 190 read that self-assessment at Orient, passed `args` as a JSON object rather than a string believing that was the documented fix, and hit it again — 240 documents from eight agents landed in `<repo>/undefined/adv/`. No data is lost (the tree is intact and one `mv` recovers it) but the failure is silent at author time and only visible in the agents' own reports. **The rule: do not pass paths through `args`. Interpolate the absolute path directly into the script's own source when composing the workflow, and have the first agent `mkdir -p` and echo the path it actually resolved.** A cheap standing check: after any fan-out that writes files, run `ls` on the repo root and look for a directory named `undefined`. |
| 266 | **A derived file must never be able to take its own source's name — a one-line filter loop silently destroyed the truth file it had just read.** Session 190 split a 1,728-row rendered-truth TSV into four per-kind files with `for kind in kinds: write(f"{kind}.tsv")`. One of the kinds was literally `ctx`, so `ctx.tsv` — the *input* — was overwritten with the 540-row `ctx`-kind subset. The four outputs were correct only by the accident that the whole input had already been read into a dict before the first write; had the loop streamed, every later score would have been silently wrong. The corruption surfaced an hour later as an unrelated-looking mystery ("0 of 324 setext documents render anything") that briefly read as a degenerate corpus. **The rule: derived artifacts get a namespaced prefix (`k_ctx.tsv`), and any script that reads a file and writes a computed name asserts the two sets are disjoint before writing.** Corollary, which is the part that generalises: when a number looks impossible, suspect the instrument before the conclusion — the "degenerate corpus" reading would have thrown away a real 216-heading finding. |
| 267 | **A predicate with an early bail has TWO answer sets, not one, and the pre-bail set is the one nothing cross-checks.** `closesParagraph` and `opensFreshBlock` ask nearly the same question — "is this line block-level?" — and both bail on `paragraphOpen`. Session 188 added the class-A raw-TeX macro AHEAD of `closesParagraph`'s bail, because class A interrupts a paragraph in every context. Nothing added it ahead of `opensFreshBlock`'s, which reached raw TeX only BEHIND the bail via the class-A∪B row. Neither the type checker, the unit suite nor the oracle can see this: both functions compile, both are covered, and the divergence lives in which of two lists a name appears in. ⚠ **When two sibling predicates share a bail, diff their pre-bail sets explicitly** — the asymmetry is invisible in a diff of either one alone, and it is the *stronger* question (setext, which needs a fresh paragraph to START) that silently gets the *weaker* answer. Session 191. |
| 268 | **Before naming a phantom family after the construct you just changed, sweep the axis you think it is about — the confound is usually a different row entirely.** Re-scoring Session 190's corpus surfaced one new phantom on a document containing `        \hrule`, and `\hrule` is a class-A macro this session had just hoisted, so the obvious reading was "the hoist mis-classifies `\hrule`". The defect was `SETEXT_H1`/`SETEXT_H2`'s own ` {0,3}` indent — a different row, filed by Session 182, that the hoist merely made reachable. Naming it "the `\hrule` bug" would have produced a fix to the wrong row, in the heading-deleting direction. ⚠ One suspicious document is a hypothesis; the cheapest way to test it is a small full-factorial sweep over the axes you *believe* are implicated, because a confound announces itself as a column that does not vary. Session 191. |
| 269 | **Apply the trigger-removal control to EVERY new error, not only the ones that look pre-existing — the ratio is the finding.** Learning #263 established the control (re-run the same document with your trigger removed, on the OLD build). Session 191 applied it mechanically to all ten new phantoms from a blind 240-document sweep, replacing the class-A macro with `<div>` — an opener already tested ahead of the same bail on the pre-build. **Nine of ten fabricated the identical heading on the PRE build**, which converts "ten phantoms I caused" into "nine more doorways onto two already-filed defects, and one genuinely new spelling". Had the control been applied only to the three that looked suspicious, the remaining six would have read as caused, the trade would have looked like 722 headings against 7 fabrications instead of against 1, and narrowing back would have been defensible. ⚠ The control is cheap (one scripted substitution, one render, one probe) and its value is *proportional to how guilty the change looks* — so run it when you are most convinced you broke something. Session 191. |
| 270 | **A scorer must exclude the renderer's own refusals and its auto-generated output, or both land in the divergence column as if they were model defects.** Session 190 filed two instrument defects against this project's scorer; Session 191 fixed both before scoring anything, and both fired immediately. (a) **Non-zero exit:** `quarto render` refusing a document is not a disagreement between two heading models — one container document crashed quarto outright (**exit 139**, SIGSEGV) and three adversarial documents exited 1. Scored naively, a refused document reads as "quarto produced no headings", so every heading the model produces there is a phantom. (b) **Auto-generated headings:** quarto emits `<h2>Footnotes</h2>` for any document with a live footnote reference (Learning #264). Both exclusions are one line each and they are reported *separately* rather than silently dropped, so a corpus that is 30% refusals is visible as such instead of reading as a clean sweep. ⚠ Fix a filed instrument defect BEFORE the measurement it would corrupt, not after the number looks strange. Session 191. |
| 271 | **A filed item's prescribed FIX can be wrong even when its measurement is right — and the corpus that reveals it is the one dimension the filer never varied.** Session 182 filed, and Session 191 promoted with a 27-document factorial, that `SETEXT_H1`/`SETEXT_H2`'s ` {0,3}` is too wide and the underline is "anchored at column 0", pointing at `SETEXT_UNDERLINE_RUN` as the model to copy. Every document behind that claim had NO container. Session 192 re-rendered before writing code (Learning #251) and swept the one axis the item held fixed — the containing block: a `- ` item renders the heading at underline column 2, a `1. ` item at 3, a `-   ` item at 4, and three-deep nested bullets at 0, 2, 4 AND 6. The rule is an EQUALITY against `[0, ...contentColumns]`, and the prescribed column-0 anchor would have DELETED every container heading — the direction this project has got wrong before. **The tell is structural, not statistical: an item that prescribes a fix by pointing at a sibling row is asserting the two rows answer the same question, and that is a claim to test, not inherit.** Two rows can both be "about indent" and still take opposite rules (Learning #260's raw-TeX pair, again). Before implementing a filed prescription, list the axes its evidence holds FIXED and vary the one that would change the answer. |
| 272 | **When a change is simultaneously too wide and too narrow, "narrow it" and "widen it" are both wrong and the sign of the net score hides that.** The ` {0,3}` cap emitted phantoms at columns 1-3 everywhere AND deleted real headings at container columns 4+. Either one-directional edit trades one error class for the other and can still look like an improvement, because a count that moves in the right direction says nothing about which SET moved. Session 192 scored per heading with the two directions separate and then computed the SET difference (`newerr.py`): 97 phantoms drained and 57 headings recovered across 481 documents with **NEW LOST = 0** — an empty set, not a smaller number. **A count improving in both directions is compatible with introducing new errors in both directions; only the set difference excludes it.** Compute NEW-error sets against the pre-build, never deltas of totals. |
| 273 | **A tab inside a heading name silently truncated the score through a tab-separated probe — and the UNIT TEST caught it, because the assertion had been written from the instrument instead of from the model.** The blind sweep reported a tenth new phantom, `h1:Fir` against quarto's `h1:Fir Underline`, which reads exactly like a text-truncation defect. The model emits `h1:Fir\tUnderline`; the probe wrote TSV, the heading's own tab opened a third column, and the scorer read the first two. Writing the pin as `toEqual(["h1:Fir"])` failed against the real `findHeadings`, which is how the instrument defect surfaced at all. Fixing the probe (escape tabs) and normalising whitespace on BOTH sides — quarto's extractor already collapsed `\s+` — moved that document from NEW PHANTOM to RECOVERED HEADING: nine new phantoms, not ten, and forty-four recoveries, not forty-three. **This is the eighth instrument defect in this family and the fifth to point in the direction that makes the change look WORSE or SAFER than it is.** Two standing rules: a delimiter-separated probe must escape its own delimiter out of every free-text field, and a residual pin must be written from the MODEL and then reconciled with the instrument, never copied from the instrument's output. |
| 274 | **When the pre-existence control lands on a document where the renderer's own answer changes with the trigger, the control is inconclusive — say so rather than borrowing the family's verdict.** Session 192 ran Learning #269's control (every underline moved to column 0) on all nine new phantoms. It proved five pre-existing outright and was silent on the rest for two distinct reasons, which must not be conflated: for two documents the PRE build already fabricated the identical defect ELSEWHERE IN THE SAME DOCUMENT (a stronger proof than the control), while for one — a footnote definition used as a setext title — quarto's own answer differs at column 0 and at column 4, so the control compares two different questions and settles nothing. That one is classified into a family its siblings prove, and is LABELLED as inference. **A control that cannot fire is not a control that passed.** Record which of the three states each finding is in — proven, proven-by-a-different-route, or classified-by-family — because a successor re-reading the pin cannot recover the distinction from a bare "PRE-EXISTING". |
| 275 | **One constant, three consumers, three polarities — and a filed item names only the consumer that motivated it.** `INDENTED_CODE_LINE` was read in three places: `CLOSES_PARAGRAPH` (an indented-code line closes a paragraph, so narrowing REMOVES phantom ATX headings), `OPENS_FRESH_BLOCK` (it makes the line below a fresh paragraph, so narrowing DELETES setext headings) and the code-RUN exception in `computeRegions` (the 2nd line of a run can never be a setext title, so narrowing ADDS them). Session 189's filed item, and Session 192's handoff that ranked it #1, both described only the first — "the fix is `contentColumns` applied to one more row, so effort is small". The row count was right and the consumer count was not: the run exception turned out to carry the session's single largest effect, **80 recovered headings**, which no reading of the filed item predicts. **When a change edits a shared CONSTANT rather than a call site, enumerate its readers before designing the corpus, and give each reader its own sweep** — a threshold proven on one reader is unmeasured on another reader's question (Learning #233), and here the three readers happened to agree only because the same physical rule underlies all three. That agreement is a finding, not an assumption to start from. |
| 276 | **Re-scoring the PREDECESSOR's own corpus is the only thing that corrects a ranked magnitude, and it has now corrected three in a row.** The item claimed "36 of the 41 residual phantoms". The pattern is now established rather than anecdotal: Session 188's item drained 0, Session 189's was overstated by 60%, this one by 80%. **A filed magnitude is a measurement of the build that filed it, and every session since has moved the rows underneath it.** Re-score before quoting the number in a close-out, and quote the re-scored one — the cost is a probe run against cached renders, and the alternative is passing an inflated ranking to the next session, which is how the wrong item gets picked. |
| 277 | **A corpus in which NEITHER side can emit a heading scores 0 / 0 / 0 and reads exactly like "no divergence anywhere".** This session's consumer-C corpus — 196 documents of `<ind>a` / `<ind>b` / `<col>===` — was designed to isolate the code-RUN exception and instead isolated nothing: pandoc renders no heading for that shape at any indent, and neither did we, before or after. The effect was measured anyway, but through a DIFFERENT corpus (the setext sweep, where the same mechanism produced 80 recoveries), i.e. by luck of design rather than by plan. **Before trusting a corpus's silence, prove it can speak: check that the RENDERER emits the construct under test somewhere in it.** A corpus whose quarto column is empty everywhere is an instrument, not evidence — the same class of defect as the 0-row probe (S191 gotcha 7) and the control that cannot fire (Learning #274), and it fails in the same direction: it makes a change look safe. |
| 278 | **A delegated lens that stalls is a DROPPED lens unless you run its role yourself, and the completeness role is the one that finds what the designed corpora cannot.** Eight blind adversarial lenses returned 240 documents and moved this change's score by exactly one phantom — a strong safety result and an almost useless discovery result, because every lens attacked constructs pandoc tries AFTER indented code. The ninth (a completeness critic) appeared stalled, so its role was run firsthand instead: fifteen named gaps neither the lenses nor the designed corpora touched (the `from:` dialect override, front-matter adjacency, line blocks, mid-document YAML, skipped comment regions, CRLF, markers wider than 8, an executable cell inside a list). That 24-document pass drained **10 more phantoms** and produced the session's only genuinely NEW unfiled defect — a blank line inside an indented code block re-arms the following code line as a setext title. **Fan-out buys breadth, not completeness; the question "what did none of these lenses look at?" is answerable only by someone holding all their answers at once.** Ask it explicitly, and if the agent asked to ask it does not deliver, ask it yourself rather than recording nine lenses when eight ran. |
| 279 | **When a rule is a MEASUREMENT rather than a threshold, sweep it as an EQUIVALENCE — the design that cannot presuppose its own answer.** The filed item said the container stack's pop counted spaces where it should count columns. A threshold sweep ("the pop should fire at column N") would have baked the conclusion into the corpus. Instead every tab spelling was paired against the SPACE spelling reaching the SAME column — 6 containers × 13 columns × up to 4 spellings × 2 consumer families — and the only question asked of quarto was *do these two render the same?* It answered identically in **276 of 276** pairs, and each column's space spelling was its own built-in control, so the corpus could have refuted the prescription as easily as confirmed it (Session 192's did exactly that). The shape generalises: when the suspected defect is *how a quantity is measured* rather than *where a boundary sits*, the corpus should vary the SPELLING and hold the quantity fixed. A sweep whose cells are all "what happens at N" can only ever locate a boundary; a sweep whose cells are pairs can falsify the unit. **When to apply:** any filed item of the form "X counts A where it should count B". |

Only a corpus built for a DIFFERENT question separates them. This is the second distinct reason to re-score the predecessor (Learning #276 gave the first, correcting a ranked magnitude), and it is the stronger one: #276 improves a ranking, this one prevents shipping a regression. |

FM #26 exists to stop a session bundling two INTENTS; it does not license shipping a known regression to keep a scope declaration tidy. The test that distinguishes the two, and the one to write into the record: **would this defect exist if my change did not ship?** For the raw-TeX row the answer was no in effect (it was invisible before), so it was closed in the same session and disclosed as an amendment with the measurement that forced it. For four other families of the identical rule — the setext underline's tab, `ATX_HEADING`'s cap, and the two spaces-only openers — the answer was yes, they were pre-existing and unchanged, so they were pinned with controls and filed. | 282 | **A measured fact and the rule inferred from it are different artifacts, and a code comment that records only the fact will be read as recording the rule.** Session 192 wrote, in `setextUnderlineLevel`'s docstring, "⚠ A TAB is not the content column" and cited the document that proves it: `- item` / `  Some Title` / `\t===` renders no heading. | 285 | **A corpus that reads a shared structure through ONE of its consumers has not measured the structure — and the direction of error it cannot see is usually the expensive one.** This session's ground corpus was 1,265 documents rendered through the real `quarto render` path, swept as a falsifiable equivalence over 5 container contexts × 24 opener spellings × 11 underline columns. It scored the change at **NEW LOST = 0**, 21 headings recovered, and every one of its 24 new phantoms proven per document to be its space twin's pre-existing error. That verdict was wrong, and not by a little: a BLIND 240-document adversarial sweep measured **39 NEW LOST**. The reason is structural rather than a gap in coverage. `contentColumns` has three consumers — the setext underline, `indentedCodeLine`, and `rawTexMacroLineIsBlock` — and the ground corpus's probe was a setext underline, through which pushing a column can only ever ADD a heading. The blind lenses reached `indentedCodeLine`, where pushing a column RAISES the code base, turns a code block into an open paragraph, and `blank_before_header` then DELETES the ATX heading below it. Same rule, same documents' shape, opposite sign — and no amount of breadth along the corpus's own axes could have found it, because the probe was the thing that was narrow. This is Learning #280's mechanism seen from the other side: there the masking defect was in the code, here it is in the instrument. **Countermeasure: before scoring, enumerate the consumers of the structure you are changing and give EACH one its own probe — the sweep's axes are not its coverage. `grep` the field's readers (here `contentColumns` had 4 call sites) and check the corpus exercises every one.** |
| 286 | **"Is this new error already present in the OTHER spelling?" is decidable mechanically, per document, and it is the question that separates a defect you introduced from one you merely made reachable.** When a change asserts that two spellings are equivalent, every new error it produces is one of exactly two things, and counting cannot tell them apart. The test that can: construct the twin document mechanically (here, expand every leading TAB to the spaces reaching the same column), render BOTH through the real toolchain, and ask whether the PRE-change build already erred on the twin. Applied to all 25 remaining new errors across both corpora — including both remaining LOSSES, the expensive direction — every one was pre-existing in its twin, from three separately-filed rules. The same construction doubles as an independent check on the premise: quarto answered identically for 495/495 ground pairs and 240/240 blind pairs, 735 with no counterexample, and our build went 450/495 and 114/240 before to 495/495 and 240/240 after. **The equivalence figure, not the error count, is what shows a rule of this shape is right: it can be measured on a corpus built by someone trying to refute you, and it has a defined failure value.** Contrast the weaker habits this replaces — "the net count improved" (hides new errors, Learning #272) and "the twin looks pre-existing to me" (an argument, not a measurement). |
| 287 | **An adversarial critic's concrete predictions must be RENDERED before they are believed — and running it is still worth it, because its by-catch outlives its thesis.** A completeness critic reviewed this change and returned seven specific predicted new errors, each with an exact document. It returned **228 INTRODUCED**, so the unanimity was a property of the data and not of the script. This is the same discipline as proving a repo control EFFECTIVE BY INJECTION rather than merely quiet, applied to a verdict rather than to a diff. **When a classifier returns one label for every case, spend one run proving it can return another** — the cost is a minute and the alternative is shipping on a constant function. |

A citation to a spec (`CommonMark §4.2`, `RFC …`) in a docstring is a provenance marker meaning UNMEASURED, and its accompanying test is not independent evidence. Applies with most force where the project's renderer is a *dialect* of the cited spec rather than the spec itself. |
| 301 | **`null` as "suspend the rule" silently widens when the rule it fell back to gets wider — a fail-safe is relative to its fallback, not absolute.** Session 199 gave `atxHeadingMatch` a `null` columns state meaning "accept the bare regex match", correct while the regex was ` {0,3}`-capped. The very next commit widened that class to `[ \t]*` so the row could reach column 4, and every `null` caller silently went from "0-3 spaces" to "any indent whatsoever" — fabricating a heading at column 4 under all five `from:` dialects, in a corpus that had scored clean the commit before. The fix was to make the relaxation a bounded SET (`COMMONMARK_HEADING_COLUMNS`) rather than an absence. ⚠ The block-quote caller genuinely does want the unbounded form (measured: pandoc re-parses the quote's content, so every indent 0-8 renders), so the two `null` reasons needed DIFFERENT fallbacks — the shared sentinel had been hiding that they were different questions. | When widening a predicate that has a "suspended"/`null`/default branch, enumerate every caller of that branch and re-derive what it now means. Prefer an explicit bounded set over a sentinel wherever the fallback is a real rule rather than a genuine absence — and when two callers pass the same sentinel for different reasons, that is the signal they need separate parameters. |
| 302 | **"My change caused it" and "my change made it reachable" are not the only two verdicts — a third is that the OLD build was right by accident, and the accident is worth naming in the record.** Session 199's per-error adjudicator returned exactly one INTRODUCED error: at a container's content column the pre-session build produced quarto's heading text exactly, and the shipped build does not. It was not correctness — ` {0,3}` could not see the heading at all, so the line fell through to the setext path, which keeps the literal `#` that pandoc keeps. Fixing the heading row removed the accident and exposed a deliberate, separately-filed product divergence (S182: we strip the `#` on purpose). The honest close was to disclose it, note that the behaviour is now UNIFORM across columns where it previously disagreed with itself, and extend the filed item rather than reverse another session's decision (FM #26). ⚠ S197 recorded the same shape in the opposite direction, where the accident was masking 262 defects. | When an adjudicator says INTRODUCED, ask *why* the old build was right before assuming you broke something. If it was right by not looking, you have removed an accident, not added a defect — and the correct record says so explicitly, because the raw count reads identically either way. |
| 303 | **Two rules can sit one line apart in the same scanner, look like the same question, and have genuinely different answers — so a result measured on one is a HYPOTHESIS about the other, never an inheritance.** Session 199 measured an ATX heading's indent as an exact EQUALITY against the enclosing block's content column: quarto's pandoc gives it no leading-space tolerance at all, and columns 1-3 render as literal paragraph text. The obvious generalisation was that `FENCE_OPEN`'s neighbouring ` {0,3}` cap was the same defect and wanted the same fix. It is not and it does not. Measured over a 96-document grid (8 container shapes x 12 indents, `scratchpad/s200/gnd`): a fence keeps CommonMark §4.5's 0-3 TOLERANCE and merely measures it from the container instead of from source column 0 — top level accepts 0-3, a `-   item` accepts 0-7, a three-deep list accepts 0-9. Had the equality been carried across, every fence at `c+1 .. c+3` would have stopped being a fence. S199's own handoff said not to assume it, in the same sentence that ranked this item #1; following that instruction is the only reason the grid swept those columns at all. | Before reusing last session's measured answer on an adjacent row, write down the competing candidates and build the corpus that SEPARATES them. Adjacent regexes are not evidence of a shared rule; the renderer is the only evidence, and it is cheap to ask. |
| 304 | **When a defect is filed as "widen X", check whether the thing X gates is reachable at all — the guard on the other side may make the widening a measurable no-op.** `FENCE_OPEN`'s cap was filed as one narrow row to relax. But since Session 179 a fence opens only if `hasCloserBelow` finds a closer, and `FENCE_CLOSE` carried the SAME ` {0,3}` cap — so on the very documents the item was filed on (opener and closer both indented into a container) the widened opener still declined, because its closer was in no bucket. The opener and closer are one capability, not two, and shipping the filed half alone would have changed nothing while looking like a fix. Found by reading the closer path during the pre-claim coupling survey, before any code was written; it is what put the closer inside the declared scope. | A one-row fix whose row feeds a lookahead, a gate or an index is not a one-row fix. During the coupling survey, trace what CONSUMES the row's decision as well as what produces it, and ask what would have to be true for the change to be observable at all. |
| 305 | **A pre-pass index cannot answer a question that depends on scan-time state — but freezing the state at the moment the region opens can, and the alternative "over-accept now, filter later" fails in the heading-DELETING direction.** `buildCloserIndex` runs once before the scan, so it cannot know a closer's container stack. The cheap shape — index every run and let `isCloser` reject bad columns during the scan — makes the lookahead over-accept, which opens a fence whose only candidate closers sit at code depth; `isCloser` then never fires and the region swallows every heading to end of document, where quarto renders those same documents as ordinary paragraphs with their headings intact (measured, `scratchpad/s200/ax` `unt_b4_i04`). What made an exact answer possible: while a fence is open the scanner `continue`s ABOVE the container-maintenance block, so the stack is frozen for the whole region and the acceptable closer columns are fully determined at OPEN time. Keying the index by column and enumerating that bounded set keeps the lookahead exact. | When a pre-pass must answer a scan-time question, look for state the scan FREEZES rather than settling for an approximation — and when you must approximate, work out which direction the error points. An over-accepting lookahead that deletes headings is not a safe default just because it is simpler. |
| 306 | **The obvious fix for a self-caused error can itself be measurably wrong, and the corpus that found the error is already the instrument that says so.** Session 200's completeness pass found one phantom its own change had caused: a plain fence recognised directly under an open PARAGRAPH, where quarto reads the whole item as one paragraph and the backtick pair as an INLINE code span. The instinctive closure — bail out of the fence branch while `paragraphOpen` — is wrong: at column 0 the fence really DOES interrupt a paragraph (rendered), so bailing deletes a heading both builds get right, and the interrupt rule further turns on the fence CHAR (a `~~~` opener at column 0 does not interrupt where a backtick one does), which is conclusive that it is a different question from the indent. What shipped instead is the narrow honest boundary — while a paragraph is open the row keeps the width it had BEFORE the session — expressed as a bounded `[0]`, not a `null` suspension, because the regexes had widened to `[ \t]*` one commit earlier (#301). Every error the change introduced there disappears; every error already there is carried unchanged and filed. | Render the fix before shipping it, not just the bug. "Bail out where I broke it" is a rule shaped to the diff rather than to the renderer; the defensible boundary is "my change does not apply here", which is checkable against the pre-session build on identical bytes. |
| 307 | **A guard that DECLINES is not the same kind of defect as a rule that MIS-RECOGNISES, and a filed item that says "widen the guard" may be asking for the harm to be spread.** `BULLET_LIST_MARKER` was filed for three sessions as a ` {0,3}` cap "too narrow at a container column", by analogy with the `ATX_HEADING` and `FENCE_OPEN` caps that Sessions 199 and 200 widened. But those two rows decide *whether a construct is recognised*, where this one decided *whether to emit a heading at all*: widening it would have converted a text divergence into a heading DELETION, on top of a deletion it was already causing at columns 0–3. Before adopting a filed item's proposed fix, ask what the row DOES with its answer — recognise, suppress, or transform — because the same regex shape in three rows can want three different repairs. | Reading any filed item that proposes a fix by analogy to a shipped one; especially when the analogy is to work you did yourself in the last session or two. |
| 308 | **When a docstring states a fact AND a conclusion, the fact can be right and the conclusion wrong — and the fact is often the evidence against the conclusion.** `BULLET_LIST_MARKER`'s docstring said pandoc "strips the marker and nests the heading INSIDE the `<li>`" and concluded the model "must decline (a false negative) rather than emit a wrong top-level heading". Re-rendered, the HTML was exactly as described — `<ul><li><h2 id="solo-item">solo item</h2></li></ul>` — which is precisely why declining was wrong: the heading exists and its text is obtainable. Learning #300 says a spec-provenance rule is unmeasured; this is its sibling — a *measured* premise can still carry an unmeasured inference, so check the reasoning separately from the observation. | Any docstring or test comment of the form "X is true, therefore we must Y", especially where Y is a deliberate false negative justified by this project's FP-safe doctrine. |
| 309 | **An arbitrary-looking constant may be a correct rule measured from the wrong origin — so measure the boundary before deleting it.** The ` {0,3}` cap on the bullet-marker guard turned out to be exactly `indentedCodeLine` counted from source column 0 instead of from the enclosing container's content column: accidentally RIGHT at top level (strip at 0–3, keep at 4+) and wrong at every container column. Removing the cap outright therefore introduced a text divergence at code depth that a BLIND lens caught after the designed corpora had scored clean. Sweep the axis the constant lives on and ask what rule would produce that exact number. | Deleting or widening any hard-coded threshold; when the constant's value happens to equal another rule's threshold (4, 3, `base + 4`), suspect that the other rule is what it meant. |
| 310 | **Scoring TEXT rather than presence changes what the instrument can see, and the rendered-HTML extractor is a different alphabet from the model's source text.** This family's earlier sessions scored heading PRESENCE, where `H_DIRECT` (heading text containing no nested tag) is sound. Scoring text exposed three separate limits at once: `H_DIRECT` reports `[]` for any heading containing inline markup, so 7 rows read as "quarto renders nothing"; the extractor collapses whitespace runs, so tab-versus-space fidelity is unmeasurable; and rendered HTML has already applied emphasis, code spans, entities, smart typography and section numbers, which the model neither can nor should reproduce. Verify the last one by control — a plain `# *emphasised* atx heading` shows the same gap on the pre-build, with no marker anywhere. | Any session whose divergence is a text divergence rather than a presence divergence. Switch to the tag-stripping column, print how many verdicts the normalisation changed, and prove the representation gap pre-existing rather than excusing it. |
| 311 | **A repeated transform needs the state its own repetitions create.** The marker strip began as one regex, `(?:[-*+][ \t]+)+`, which is correct only while every marker sits at a nestable column. A marker can be at indented-code depth *inside the item its predecessor opened* — `-` + 5 spaces + `- title` keeps the second marker, where 1–4 spaces strips it — so the transform has to walk one marker at a time, carrying the content column each opens. When a rule is "apply X repeatedly", ask what X changes for the next application before reaching for `+` on the regex. | Writing any repeated/greedy strip, unwrap, or dedent over structured text where each step establishes a new baseline (list nesting, block-quote depth, container columns). |
| 312 | **Two rows can key on the same front-matter fact and still need SEPARATE flags, because their fail-open directions are opposite.** Session 199 gave `atxHeadingMatch` a dialect relaxation keyed on the PRESENCE of a `from:` key, never on its value — deliberate, because firing on a non-CommonMark document costs that row a phantom. Session 202's setext rule DELETES the heading at underline column 0, so the same flag would have cost a real heading under `from: markdown` and `from: markdown_strict` (both measured rendering it). The reuse looked like a one-line change and would have moved both rows. | Before reusing an existing flag, predicate or constant for a new rule: ask what happens when it is WRONG, for each reader of it. Same fact + opposite polarity = separate state. The corollary is that a shared constant whose numbers coincide (`COMMONMARK_HEADING_COLUMNS` as absolute columns vs the same four values as offsets) should also stay separate — see #303. |
| 313 | **When a rule is dialect-conditional, the ALLOWLIST must be measured per spelling and matched exactly, because one plausible member behaves the opposite way.** `markdown_github` is documented by pandoc as a deprecated SYNONYM for `gfm`; quarto 1.7.33 renders it exactly like `markdown` — verified against the raw HTML, not through an extractor. Any classifier written as a pattern ("contains github", "is not markdown") would have deleted that heading. `GFM` in upper case, by contrast, makes quarto refuse the document outright (exit 1), so it has no truth to agree with. | Whenever a rule keys on a named value from an external tool. Enumerate the spellings, render each, and write the allowlist from the results — never from the upstream's own documentation of what the names mean. |
| 314 | **A line that is PART of a construct can also open a container, and the container block runs first — so "the innermost open column" is not the one the construct belongs to.** A lone `-` is both a level-2 setext underline and a list marker; `a. ` is a title's text under a CommonMark reader and a fancy-list marker to `listItemContentColumn`. Both pushed a column that displaced the correct one and DELETED a heading. Session 201 surveyed this same coupling for its row and correctly derived it harmless there — the derivation does not transfer, because that row read the stack for a code-depth test where a deeper column is inert, and this one reads it for an innermost-column test where a deeper column is decisive. | Whenever a rule reads the container stack from inside a multi-line construct. Ask which lines contributed to the stack you are reading, and whether your row's question makes a spurious column inert or decisive. Both defects here were found by BLIND lenses after the designed corpora scored clean. |
| 315 | **A wrong INPUT cannot be repaired by the rule that reads it, and the honest response is to bound the blast radius rather than widen the change.** Under `gfm` and `commonmark` a definition list is not a construct, so `:   body` opens no content column — but `contentColumns` pushes 4 for it under every reader. The old accepted set `[0, 4]` was wrong at both members; the new `[c … c+3]` is wrong at four, so the change adds 6 phantoms it cannot fix from inside its own row. Narrowing the stack was measured WORSE: `commonmark_x` really does have definition lists and `gfm` really does have footnotes, so refusing the `:`/`~` spelling trades 6 phantoms under two readers for a DELETION under a third. | When your change makes a pre-existing wrong input more visible. Prove the input wrong through a consumer you did NOT touch (here an ATX heading at the same column, on the pre-session build), disclose the count, and file the root cause — do not reach into shared machinery three other rows read to hide it. |
| 316 | **A filed item that names NO fix is a different hazard from one that names the wrong fix, and the difference is where the unmeasured part hides.** Sessions 200, 201 and 202 each found the filed item proposed a fix by analogy that was wrong; the countermeasure was to distrust the named fix. Session 203's item named none — it stated the defect ("`consecutiveBody === 1` is dialect-blind") and stopped. That reads as *less* risk and is *more*: with no proposal to refute, nothing forces the unmeasured questions into the open, and here they were the whole job. The join itself took one RED→GREEN cycle; the six that followed were all guards against fabrications the item never mentioned — an indented code run already sits at `consecutiveBody === 2`, a paragraph interrupt, a fence opening the run, a paren-wrapped `(1)`, a fenced div, an HTML block. **Write the candidate rules AND the fail-open direction into the 1B claim before rendering even when the item proposes nothing to argue with** — the claim's job is to make the unmeasured visible, and an item that argues nothing supplies none of it. | Reading any filed item at Phase 0. An item that only describes a symptom needs MORE pre-claim survey than one that proposes a fix, not less. |
| 317 | **Two lists can answer the same question — "is this line block-level?" — with THREE different defaults, and the default is set by what the reading row does with a YES.** `CLOSES_PARAGRAPH` removes headings when a pattern is wrongly present, so it is deny-by-default. `OPENS_FRESH_BLOCK` ADDS them, so its documented rule is the opposite: "when in doubt, leave it out" (24 measured phantoms, Learning #227). Session 203's `COMMONMARK_PARAGRAPH_INTERRUPT` inverts it AGAIN: a pattern wrongly present only DECLINES a multi-line title, which is the pre-existing behaviour and a residual, while a pattern MISSING stitches a heading out of two different blocks. So its rule is "when in doubt, put it in" — and reusing either neighbour would have been wrong in a different direction. **Before adding a block-classification list, ask what the consuming row does with a YES and a NO, and derive the default from that rather than from the list's shape.** A fourth row would need the question asked again. | Adding or reusing any regex list that classifies a line as block-level. The polarity is a property of the CONSUMER, not of the patterns. |
| 318 | **A guard that walks a run must ask what OPENED the run, not only what is inside it — and the two questions have different answers for the same construct.** Session 203's interrupt guard tested lines 2..n and a blind lens found the hole in one document: an UNCLOSED fence is deliberately not treated as a region (Session 179, because pandoc requires a closer), so the fence line falls through to ordinary body and became the run's FIRST line, which nothing tested. The first line cannot be tested the same way, because a BULLET or ORDERED marker there opens a list item whose content IS a paragraph and quarto joins it, while a fence, an HTML block or a block quote opens something that is not a paragraph at all — two lists, measured separately. **A construct a file goes out of its way NOT to treat as a block is the one most likely to turn up where a block is not expected.** | Writing any rule that consumes a RUN of lines rather than one line. Enumerate what can open the run, not just what can appear in it — and check the constructs your own code deliberately declines to classify. |
| 319 | **A filed item's SYMPTOM can be one line wide while the defect has no width limit at all, and the predecessor's "the pattern to copy exists" is then the most expensive sentence in the handoff.** Session 203's item read "an HTML-BLOCK opener releases the line BELOW it as a fresh paragraph", and its forward note offered its own one-line `prevOpenedHtmlBlock` lookback as the proven shape to copy — labelled **derived**, correctly. But under a CommonMark reader `<div>` opens a block that runs to the next BLANK line, `<pre>` runs to its own `</pre>` or to end of document, and an unterminated `<!--` swallows everything after it: the state needed is a REGION with a type-specific end condition, which no adjacency flag can express. The tell was in the item's own words — "runs to the next blank line" sat in the same bullet as "the line below it", and only one of those is a width. **When a filed item names both a symptom and a mechanism, size the work from the MECHANISM; when the predecessor names a shape to copy, first ask what QUESTION that shape answers.** A one-line lookback answers "what was the previous line?" and can never answer "am I still inside something?" (Session 204) |
| 320 | **A borrowed list is not a measured list, and the only context that can tell two lists apart may be one the corpus does not naturally contain.** This file already held `PANDOC_BLOCK_OPEN_TAGS`, 98 names, exactly the "is this an HTML block opener?" question a CommonMark type-6 test needs — and reusing it would have DELETED 24 real headings, because pandoc's list carries DocBook names (`para`, `note`, `programlisting`, `sidebar`, `warning`, …) CommonMark has never heard of. ⚠ **The decisive detail is that a 130-document corpus could not see it in the obvious context.** With no paragraph open, CommonMark type 7 accepts ANY complete tag — `foo` and `mytag` included — so every name swallows and the two lists produce identical output. Only against an OPEN PARAGRAPH, where type 6 may interrupt and type 7 may not, do they separate. **Before reusing a list, ask which context makes its members DISAGREE, and put that context in the corpus — a grid built from the natural shape can score 100% and prove nothing.** (Session 204) |
| 321 | **A control corpus can refute a conclusion you are one commit from acting on, and the refutation is worth more than the finding was.** A blind lens rendered `from: markdown_github` and `from: markdown_phpextra` swallowing an HTML block — CommonMark-ish behaviour from two readers the dialect allowlist deliberately excludes — and a 20-document control agreed. The obvious reading was "the allowlist is missing four names". A 144-document grid over the SAME four readers then showed they agree with `gfm` on only 14 of 36 cells: `<div>` / `# H` without a closer does not swallow, `<pre>` does not survive a blank line, `<!--` does not swallow at all. The agreement was one shape wide and the mechanism was different (`markdown_in_html_blocks` OFF makes a CLOSED raw block's content literal). Widening the allowlist would have deleted 22 real headings per reader AND silently moved two other shipped rows that read the same flag. **When a finding suggests widening a shared gate, re-measure the WHOLE grid for the new members before touching it — "agrees on the shape I noticed" is not "is the same rule".** (Session 204) |
| 322 | **A harness flag chosen for hermeticity can silently invalidate a whole class of corpus row, and the control that catches it is a document with none of the feature under test.** `render*.sh` has passed `--no-execute` since Session 185 so renders are hermetic and cacheable. Under it, a heading BELOW an executable `{python}`/`{r}` cell renders as a paragraph, so any corpus document containing a cell scores as a divergence that is neither the model's fault nor quarto's. It surfaced as one row of a completeness pass and looked like a real defect in the area under test — until a control containing `x = 1` and no HTML at all behaved identically. ⚠ Whether execution changes it could not be determined (`quarto render` without `--no-execute` exits 1 with no kernel), so the honest outcome is an EXCLUSION rule and a disclosed caveat, not a fix. **When a probe in your own completeness pass fails, build the control that removes the feature under test before believing the probe.** (Session 204) |
| 323 | **When a filed item names a defect as "unmodelled", check whether the rule is modelled and merely SWITCHED OFF — and the evidence is often a column already sitting in the item's own cited corpus, uncompared.** Session 204 filed "the DEFAULT reader's own HTML-block rule is unmodelled" over ~15 measured rows, and its own ground grid carried a `nofrom` twin of every `from: markdown` row. Quarto renders the twins identically; this model did not — all 7 phantom `md` rows had an *agreeing* `nofrom` twin (`g_md_div_d2_atx` vs `g_nofrom_div_d2_atx`). So `blank_before_header` was implemented and correct, and `dialectOverride` disabled it for the whole document the instant ANY `from:` key appeared. Diagnosing from the item's symptom would have produced a second HTML-block rule beside Session 204's, with two rules disagreeing about the same lines; the defect was one boolean in the front-matter scan. ⚠ The generalisation is about EVIDENCE SHAPE, not about this rule: a corpus with a control axis contains the diagnosis as well as the symptom, and the filer may have measured the control without ever diffing it against the case. Read the predecessor's corpus, not only the predecessor's sentence. Cost of missing it: an entire second implementation of a rule that already existed. |
| 324 | **A knowingly-taken trade, disclosed in a docstring, is a filed item that no backlog can see — and the docstring goes stale the moment someone pays it down.** `FRONTMATTER_FROM_KEY`'s docstring said "the bail keys on the key's PRESENCE… the cost is that `from: markdown` retains the phantom, which is the permitted direction". That sentence was the whole of Session 205's deliverable, written by Session 180, sitting in `src/` where `check-backlog` and every Phase 0 backlog scan are blind to it. Two consequences, both acted on this session: **(a)** grep the source's own disclosed costs when sizing an area, not only `BACKLOG.md` — the most precisely-stated open work in this project lives in docstrings; **(b)** when you pay one down, the docstring becomes FALSE and must move in the same commit as the code. It now says which consumer was fixed, which was deliberately left, and why the two cannot share a flag. ⚠ Corollary that bit immediately: a trade taken to fail SAFE can be masking a second defect. Restoring the bail exposed that `?>` matched nothing — the `?>` line left a paragraph open, the heading below was suppressed by nothing, and the right answer came out for the wrong reason for five sessions. |
| 325 | **Re-measure the filed item's OWN named documents against the new build before draining it — that is where a regression hides that no new corpus can see.** Session 205's 262 designed and blind documents all scored INTRODUCED 0. Re-scoring Session 204's `end` corpus — cited by name in the item being closed — returned **INTRODUCED 1**: `e_md_pi_after` had agreed and now diverged. The new corpus could not contain it, because it was built around the rule being changed and a processing instruction is not that rule. ⚠ The check is cheap and mechanical: the filed item lists its evidence documents; run them. It also pays forward — the same re-scoring showed Session 204's `end` going 67/72 → 72/72 and its `gnd` 155/180 → 162/180, which is how a session learns it fixed more than it set out to. **And it decides the drain honestly:** `hdg_10` still diverged, so the item was rewritten down to its residual half rather than deleted, which `check-backlog` alone would never have caught. |
| 326 | **Two of quarto's own HTML furnishings are not the document, and both were scoring as headings this model had lost.** A document naming a non-HTML `format:` grows `<h2>Other Formats</h2>` in the alternate-formats MARGIN SIDEBAR; a document with any footnote definition grows a trailing generated `<h2>Footnotes</h2>`. `render*.sh` scores the whole file on purpose (Session 185 measured `<main>` scoping unsound for any document containing a stray closer), so both land in the heading set. ⚠ The second was actively MASKING a genuine two-heading recovery — `para_04` looked like a stuck divergence and was in fact a clean fix plus a footnote section. Both were caught by the same discipline (Learning #322): build the control that removes the feature under test — same body with and without a footnote, same body under `format: revealjs` and `format: html` — rather than arguing from the HTML. Both normalizations are applied on the scoring AND adjudication paths, because computing one in a single place makes a row score right and adjudicate wrong. |
| 327 | **When one predicate feeds two consumers, widening it ships a regression to the consumer you were not thinking about — and the completeness pass is what catches it, not the ground corpus.** Session 206 widened `FRONTMATTER_FROM_KEY` so a quoted `"from":` key and a flow-mapping `from:` would finally be seen. That predicate sets `dialectOverride`, which is read at TWO sites: the ATX paragraph bail (the row being fixed) and the ATX heading COLUMN set (a row nobody was thinking about). The 64-document ground corpus scored INTRODUCED 0 because every one of its documents reads the flag through the bail. The budgeted completeness pass — one probe per CONSUMER, generated with the NEW spellings rather than the plain one — returned INTRODUCED 2 immediately. ⚠ **The probe must use a spelling the change actually introduced:** a completeness probe written with the plain `from:` key scores green on a build where the whole session did nothing, because that spelling already worked. ⚠ And the fix's shape matters as much as its existence: narrowing the column set on the ABSENCE of a CommonMark resolution would have DELETED headings (a nested per-format `from: gfm` is honoured by quarto — `scratchpad/s206/ctl5` `z_nest_gfm` renders, `z_nest_md` does not), where narrowing on a POSITIVE resolution to a measured markdown base cannot, because an unresolved document takes neither branch. Key a narrowing on what you proved, never on what you failed to prove. |
| 328 | **Three extractor artifacts in one session, all pointing the same way — and the corpus's own DOCUMENT NAMES were the cause of one of them.** `render*.sh`'s scored column `H_DIRECT` matches `<hN …>text</hN>` where the text contains no `<`, which is what makes it nesting-safe. Session 206 walked into three separate ways for a heading to carry a tag: (a) **`number-sections: true`** wraps the number in `<span class="header-section-number">`, so EVERY heading in such a document vanishes and the row reads "renders nothing"; (b) **`markdown_strict` turns `intraword_underscores` OFF**, so this project's own `snake_case` document names became `<em>` — the corpus was invisible to its own extractor for exactly the readers it was testing; (c) quarto's **`toc:`, `citation:`, `license:` and `copyright:` keys each grow a generated heading** (`Table of contents` — renameable via `toc-title:` — plus `Citation`, `Reuse`, `Copyright`), which a realistic front matter hits at once. All three were settled by the Learning #322/#326 discipline — the feature-free control pair, one key added and nothing else — and all three point in the direction that makes a heading-deleting change look safe, now nine for nine across S182–S206. ⚠ **Check the extractor against the corpus's own naming convention, not only against its content.** |
| 329 | **A YAML value has many spellings and a line regex has one, and the way to widen without moving a measured allowlist is to NORMALISE the input rather than loosen the matcher.** Session 206 resolved the front matter's top-level `from:` once, from the whole block, and rewrote it as the canonical line `from: <value>` for Sessions 202's and 205's untouched allowlists to classify. **For a plain top-level key the line handed over is byte-identical to the source line**, so those allowlists cannot move by construction — the 1,878-test suite passing across the hoist was the evidence, not an argument. ⚠ **The polarity differs per flag and the design must too:** the KEY question fails OPEN (not firing suppresses a heading quarto renders; firing wrongly costs a phantom), so it stays a set of cheap line regexes matched at ANY indent; every VALUE question fails CLOSED (firing wrongly DELETES), so it is resolved once, at the front matter's own TOP LEVEL, and returns `null` — today's behaviour — whenever it cannot resolve with confidence. ⚠ **"Top level" is the SHALLOWEST content line, not column 0**, and that single choice is what makes a uniformly indented mapping reachable while leaving an `abstract: \|` block scalar's prose unreachable: YAML requires a block scalar's content to be indented past its own key, so the minimum can never land inside one. |
| 330 | **A blind lens's DOCUMENTS are the deliverable and its predictions are noise — but its decoys are worth more than its positives.** Session 206's four lenses returned INTRODUCED 0 and FIXED 0: not one of the 57 scorable blind documents exercised a spelling the session shipped, because the lenses that wrote exotic YAML wrote it around `gfm`, where the KEY already fired and the answer came out right for the wrong reason. What they DID deliver was five independent witnesses to one pre-existing item (an `abstract:`/`description:` block scalar whose prose wraps across the words `from: …`, which the any-indent KEY predicate reads as a reader declaration), and two genuinely new rules: the extension list is **LAST-WINS** where `fromKeepsBlankBeforeHeader` tests disables-first (`markdown-blank_before_header+blank_before_header` SUPPRESSES), and a YAML **merge key** `<<:` pulls a nested `from:` into scope. ⚠ **Brief the lens to write half its corpus as decoys** — shapes that must NOT resolve — because a decoy that trips the scanner is a measured defect, where a positive that already worked is a measured nothing. |
| 331 | **A NARROWING and a WIDENING of the same predicate have opposite safety polarities, and the guard must be rebuilt for the new direction rather than inherited.** Sessions 180, 205 and 206 each WIDENED `FRONTMATTER_FROM_KEY`, and every one of their docstrings records the same reassurance: firing wrongly costs only a phantom at heading columns 1-3, this project's permitted direction. Session 207 NARROWED it, and that sentence inverts — a `dialectOverride` that stops firing where quarto really did select a reader re-engages the paragraph bail **and** collapses the heading column set to `[0]`, and **both of those DELETE a real heading**. The inherited guard tests were all of the form "this spelling must still be SEEN", which a narrowing can satisfy while deleting elsewhere. ⚠ **The countermeasure is mechanical: before narrowing a predicate, write the guard block that asserts a heading which must SURVIVE at every position the predicate is measured to accept — and write it BEFORE the narrowing** (S204's gotcha 5, now inherited four sessions running). Session 207's guard was five positions on the column row plus two on the bail; it passed on the pre-narrowing build, which is exactly what a guard should do, and it is the artifact that made the refusal list safe to write. Corollary, and it is Learning #327's rule read backwards: a widening needs a completeness probe per CONSUMER; a narrowing needs a guard per ACCEPTED POSITION. |
| 332 | **A scorer keyed on a document's BASENAME silently overwrites colliding names, and blind-lens corpora collide across sessions BY CONSTRUCTION.** Session 207 staged ten predecessor corpora to re-measure them (Learning #325) and scored them from one merged probe file. `score207.py`'s `read()` builds a `dict` keyed by name, so the second `yaml_01` overwrote the first — and `yaml_01`, `real_01` … `real_16` exist in Session 205's blind corpus AND Session 206's, because every lens is briefed to name its documents `<lens>_NN`. **38 of 198 staged documents collided**, and two whole corpora scored **`0/16 agree`** — a number indistinguishable from a catastrophic regression, whose actual cause was that the heading TEXT being compared came from a different session's document of the same name. It was caught only by reading a divergence line and noticing that the reported source text (`h1:yaml_01 Baseline`) was not the text in the file (`h1:Yaml Alpha One Baseline`). ⚠ **The fix is not to remember to score per corpus** — Session 201 shipped the remember-at-every-call-site version of exactly this hazard and lost a measurement to it anyway; `read()` now REFUSES a duplicate key, which makes the merged file impossible to read rather than quietly wrong. ⚠ And note the shape: a guard whose two sides share an input cannot detect a defect in that input (S187 recorded the same shape in `render*.sh`'s row-count check). |
| 333 | **`bibliography:` grows a trailing `h2:References` — the SEVENTH distinct piece of quarto furniture, and the first found by a corpus that was REALISTIC rather than adversarial.** Session 207's blind `real` lens wrote working-scientist manuscripts, ten of which cite a `references.bib` (the lens itself reported eleven — verified firsthand, and one more agent claim that did not survive the check). The corpus scored **8/16**, and every one of the eight divergences was this one generated heading; normalised, it scores **16/16 with INTRODUCED 0**. Proven the only way these are ever proven — the feature-free control pair `scratchpad/s207/ctl2`, `y_bib` and `y_nobib` being the same document with and without the single key (Learning #322/#326). ⚠ **The furniture list is a property of the front-matter keys a corpus USES, not of the rules it tests**, so a corpus written to be realistic finds furnishings that a corpus written to be adversarial never will — `toc:`/`citation:`/`license:`/`copyright:` came from S206's designed grid, `Other Formats` and `Footnotes` from S205's, and `References` needed a document that actually cited something. ⚠ A second, cheaper corollary from the same lens: `render*.sh` renders each document ALONE in a temp directory, so any corpus with a sibling asset (`references.bib`, a CSS file, an image) renders a DIFFERENT document from the one its author validated in place — `render207.sh` now carries sibling non-`.qmd` files across. Seven for seven, every furnishing has pointed in the direction that makes a heading-deleting change look safe. ⚠ Count furnishings, not matched strings: the list holds EIGHT strings because `toc:` has two spellings (`Table of contents` and `Contents`, the latter reachable via `toc-title:`). |
| 334 | **When two filed items are derived to be one capability, the merged row can carry a failure direction that NEITHER item names — and here it was the deleting one.** Session 206 filed its #1 (the any-indent key) as *phantom direction, MEDIUM* and its #2 (the per-format value) as *phantom direction, LOW*, then correctly derived that they were one capability. Both sizings were right about their own item and the merged capability is worse than either: the documents where a nested `format:`/`html:`/`from:` DISAGREES with a top-level `from:` exist only when both halves are in scope at once, and Session 207 measured three of them as **heading-DELETING** (`c_fmhg_topm_bail`, `c_fmhg_topm_col3`, `c_fmhm_topg_set0`). Precedence is not mentioned anywhere in either filed item, because neither item alone can produce a collision. ⚠ **So when you merge two items, re-derive the failure direction from the merged scope rather than taking the worse of the two labels** — and expect the interaction cases to be the valuable ones. ⚠ The measurement itself needed a design most collision corpora get wrong: a first round put the nested key first in BOTH collision documents, where "the nested one wins" and "the FIRST one wins" predict identical output; only a second round writing the top-level key first could separate them. **A precedence claim needs both ORDERS, not just both directions.** |
